### [1] [Dynamic Governance of Multi-LLM Agent Systems for Collaborative Conversational Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11207)
*Alexander Liss,Nicholas Desmond,Santiago Gil Gallego*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A control-theoretic governance layer called Experience Orchestrator (EO) improves LLM agent interactions by +32% conversion rates using contextual bandits, PID control, and POMDP belief tracking in simulated financial services conversations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When LLM agents with opposed objectives interact without a shared goal function, conversations collapse rather than compete. The paper investigates whether a control-theoretic governance layer can substitute for the missing goal function in multi-turn agent interactions.

Method: The Experience Orchestrator (EO) uses three mechanisms: 1) Contextual Bandit (CB) selecting content arms calibrated from real web analytics, 2) PID controller enforcing behavioral consistency via dynamic schema constraints, and 3) POMDP belief tracker maintaining probabilistic model of visitor intent.

Result: EO achieved +32 percentage point lift in high-intent advisor contact rate (78.1% vs. 46.1% over naive LLM control). Contextual Bandit selection accounted for 97% of between-factor outcome variance. Persona analysis revealed two regimes: governance critical for reluctant visitors, naive LLM sufficient for already-aligned visitors.

Conclusion: Control-theoretic governance can prevent collapse in opposed-objective LLM interactions, but findings are conditional on LLM-to-LLM simulation. The PID controller needs calibration for human unpredictability, and validation on live traffic is the critical next step.

Abstract: When two LLM agents with structurally opposed objectives interact across multiple turns, the absence of a shared goal function produces not competition but collapse: the visitor capitulates, the site agent stops varying its approach, and the conversation terminates without achieving either agent's stated objective. This paper asks whether a control-theoretic governance layer can substitute for that missing goal function. The Experience Orchestrator (EO) addresses this in a simulated financial services environment where a site agent guides a visitor toward advisor contact while the visitor maintains psychologically realistic resistance. EO governs the joint trajectory through three mechanisms: a Contextual Bandit (CB) that selects content arms calibrated from real-world web analytics, a PID controller that enforces behavioral consistency via dynamic schema constraints, and a POMDP belief tracker that maintains a probabilistic model of visitor intent. Across 60,000 simulations, EO achieves a +32 percentage point lift in high-intent advisor contact rate (78.1% vs. 46.1% over a naive LLM control), with CB variant selection accounting for 97% of between-factor outcome variance -- confirming that the governance policy, not environmental initial conditions, determines where trajectories end up. Persona-level analysis reveals two distinct regimes: for visitors with no natural inclination toward conversion, the governance layer is the difference between a functional system and a non-functional one; for visitors already near alignment, a naive LLM's empathetic defaults are largely sufficient. All findings are conditional on LLM-to-LLM simulation. The PID controller has not been calibrated against real human unpredictability, and validating EO on live traffic is the critical next step.

</details>


### [2] [Distribird: Literature-Informed Prior Distribution Design for Bayesian Model Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11210)
*Patrik P. Süli,György Eigner,Roland Hollós*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Distribird is an AI-powered web app that automates building informative Bayesian priors by searching literature, extracting parameter values, and fitting distributions, with transparency and local processing for scientific use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers default to uniform priors despite methodological advances because building informative priors from literature is slow and requires domain expertise. This creates a barrier to proper Bayesian calibration of process-based models.

Method: Multi-agent pipeline that: 1) searches literature given parameter description, 2) extracts and weights reported values by domain relevance, 3) fits probability distributions via AIC model selection, 4) falls back to sensible uninformative priors when no literature exists, and 5) provides transparency about evidence sources and confidence.

Result: Evaluated on 24 parameters across 10 domains using three LLMs vs. single-prompt baseline: full pipeline matches baseline in prior quality, provides traceability to source papers, includes validity layer that rejects out-of-scope requests (vs. baseline producing unfounded priors in 11/30 cases), and processes everything locally.

Conclusion: Distribird's transparency, traceability, validity checking, and local processing matter more than marginal accuracy improvements for scientific use, making it a practical solution to the long-standing problem of building informative priors from literature.

Abstract: Bayesian calibration of process-based models requires a prior distribution for each model parameter. Despite decades of methodological work, researchers almost always fall back on uniform priors. The main reason is that building informative priors from scientific literature is slow and needs both domain and statistical expertise. We present \textbf{Distribird}, an agentic web application that automates this process. Given a parameter name, physical description, and domain context, Distribird deploys a multi-agent pipeline that searches the literature, extracts and weights reported values by domain relevance, and fits a probability distribution via AIC model selection. When no literature is available, the system falls back to sensible uninformative alternatives, and clearly reports both the evidence behind and the confidence level of every prior it produces. It is designed for the problems where the models have physically interpretable parameters, where domain knowledge exists in the published literature. We evaluate the tool on 24~parameters across 10 scientific domains comparing three open-weight models (Qwen3.6 27B, Gemma 4 31B, Mistral Small 4 119B) with a single-prompt LLM baseline. On prior quality the full pipeline \emph{matches} this baseline. Every prior is traced to the specific papers and values from which it was constructed; a built-in validity layer declines to produce priors for out-of-scope requests, whereas the single-prompt baseline returns confident but unfounded priors for them in 11 of 30~model--parameter cases; and every language-model call runs locally, so no parameter description or unpublished modelling detail is transmitted to a third-party LLM provider (only generated search terms reach the public literature databases). For scientific use, we argue these properties matter more than a marginal improvement in point-estimate accuracy.

</details>


### [3] [A Forced-Structure Reduction and Verifiable Bounds for Conway's 99-Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11211)
*Aalok Thakkar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI research agent systematically attacks Conway's 99-graph problem, providing verifiable contributions including structural reductions, computational bounds, and a framework for future work, with current best verified coverage at 69.43% of constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether a strongly regular graph with parameters srg(99,14,1,2) exists, which is Conway's famous 99-graph problem that has remained open.

Method: Autonomous AI research agent using systematic, reproducible methods: exhaustive proof for circulant graphs, forced-structure reduction to 12-regular graphs on 84 vertices, CP-SAT encoding, and prescribed-automorphism orbit-existence framework with fixed-point-free and single-fixed-point actions.

Result: Proved no circulant graph satisfies more than 68.0% of constraints, reduced existence to 12-regular graph on 84 vertices, validated framework on known graphs (srg(9,4,1,2) and Paley graph srg(13,6,2,3)), and achieved best verified coverage of 69.43% with evidence this is a robust frontier.

Conclusion: The research provides significant verifiable contributions toward solving Conway's 99-graph problem, establishing structural insights and computational bounds, with the 69.43% coverage potentially representing a frontier entangled with the final answer since any provable bound below 4950 would constitute a non-existence proof.

Abstract: Conway's 99-graph problem asks whether a strongly regular graph with parameters $\mathrm{srg}(99,14,1,2)$ exists. We report a systematic, fully reproducible attack by an autonomous AI research agent, scored under the track's partial-credit metric. Our verifiable contributions are: (1) an exhaustive proof that no circulant graph on $\mathbb{Z}/99$ satisfies more than $3366/4950=68.0\%$ of the constraints ($33$ of $49$ difference-classes), with the same ceiling for the other abelian group of order $99$; (2) a forced-structure reduction: $λ=1$ makes each neighbourhood a perfect matching and $μ=2$ puts the outer vertices in bijection with non-matched neighbour-pairs, collapsing existence to a $12$-regular graph on $84$ vertices, encoded for CP-SAT and validated by recovering the unique $\mathrm{srg}(9,4,1,2)$; (3) a validated prescribed-automorphism orbit-existence framework (fixed-point-free and single-fixed-point actions, checked on $\mathrm{srg}(9,4,1,2)$ and the Paley graph $\mathrm{srg}(13,6,2,3)$), and (4) a best verified artifact at $69.43\%$, with evidence that this is a robust frontier (fourteen distinct methods, none exceeding it) entangled with the open question, since any provable bound below $4950$ is a non-existence proof.

</details>


### [4] [Detecting a Route Flip Is Easier Than Knowing Whether to Fix It: Causal Route-Mediated Damage in Quantized Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11212)
*Parvel Gu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how 4-bit KV-cache quantization causes routing flips in Mixture-of-Experts models, finding that about 1/3 of quantization damage is routing-mediated, with no observable predictor to distinguish harmful vs helpful flips.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Top-k MoE routing is discontinuous, making it vulnerable to numerical disturbances from deployment-motivated 4-bit KV-cache quantization. This causes tokens to cross decision boundaries and flip which experts fire, potentially degrading model performance. The paper aims to understand and measure this phenomenon rather than propose new mitigation techniques.

Method: Developed a four-run apparatus to price the route-mediated fraction (RMF) of quantization damage, used token-level attribution to decompose damage by mechanism, conducted pre-registered probes across three architectures (including OLMoE-1B-7B), and analyzed deployable router margin statistics for flip detection. Used controlled experiments including same-checkpoint flag-swaps and real int4 KV kernel testing.

Result: Found ~31% of quantization damage is routing-mediated (RMF ~ 0.31). Router margin can detect flips (AUC 0.772) but cannot distinguish harmful from helpful flips (at chance). No inference-observable router statistic predicts flip loss sign above chance. Signed-flip tax and sign-inseparability phenomena carry across models. Real int4 KV kernel results are compatible with fake-quant dose curve but inconclusive.

Conclusion: KV-cache quantization causes substantial routing-mediated damage in MoE models via boundary-crossing flips. While flips can be detected, their impact (helpful vs harmful) cannot be predicted from local router statistics, creating an empirical detection barrier that limits selective repair strategies. The findings are robust across architectures and provide a causal understanding of this deployment vulnerability.

Abstract: Top-k Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routing is discontinuous, so a deployment-motivated numerical disturbance -- simulated 4-bit KV-cache quantization read by a protected BF16 gate -- pushes tokens across decision boundaries and flips which experts fire. This paper proposes no new mitigation; it supplies a causal apparatus, empirical findings, and a detection-limit result. A four-run apparatus prices the route-mediated fraction (RMF) of quantization damage, a token-level attribution decomposes it by mechanism, and pre-registered probes carry the findings across three architectures. On OLMoE-1B-7B at 4-bit KV (pilot), about a third of the damage is routing-mediated: RMF ~ 0.31 (discovery 0.31 [0.20, 0.41]; process-replicated mean 0.313 +/- 0.020; pre-registered re-execution 0.231). The deployable router margin detects that a flip occurred (AUC 0.772) but cannot tell a harmful flip from a helpful one (at chance): among the tested local, inference-observable router statistics we find no predictor of a flip's loss sign above chance -- an empirical benefit-detection barrier bounding selective repair restricted to this feature family. The signed-flip tax and sign-inseparability carry cross-model; the clean-reference remedy's payout is architecture-modulated; a controlled same-checkpoint flag-swap re-scopes the gate's normalization convention to a damage-magnitude moderator, not a route-recoverability mechanism. A real int4 KV kernel yields a fraction compatible with the fake-quant dose curve but underpowered (95% CI [-0.111, 0.394] includes zero) -- ruling out gross disagreement, not an independent replication. Hypotheses, thresholds, and evaluations were pre-registered before measurement, with misses reported; a pre-registered held-out read replicates the partition and the near-cancelling tax out of sample, while the strict impossibility exclusion narrowly misses.

</details>


### [5] [Poor Man's Agentic Modeling: Simulating Large LLM-Agent Societies on a Laptop](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11215)
*Igor Itkin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Develops a cheap surrogate method to replace LLM agents in social simulations with low-parameter models fitted from limited queries, enabling large-scale simulations on laptops while predicting error scaling with agent count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agent simulations are computationally expensive, but researchers typically care about macroscopic societal behaviors (phase behavior, stylized facts, scaling) rather than individual agent cognition.

Method: Replace each expensive LLM agent with a low-parameter model fitted from a few hundred to a few thousand cheap queries; introduce an [interaction order × memory] taxonomy that maps perception and memory to an effective theory and predicts surrogate error trends with agent count N.

Result: Validated on faithful reimplementation of LLM macroeconomy EconAgent and seven other LLM simulations; predicted error trends hold cell by cell across taxonomy; two refuted predictions (on strongly saturating response) are quantitatively matched by theory with no free parameters.

Conclusion: Statistical physics principles enable cheap surrogate modeling of LLM societies with predictable error scaling, making large-scale social simulations computationally feasible while maintaining accuracy for macroscopic phenomena.

Abstract: Simulating societies of many large language model (LLM) agents is expensive, yet the questions asked of such simulations are usually macroscopic: phase behaviour, stylised facts, and scaling with the number of agents $N$, not the cognition of any single agent. We turn a statistical-physics observation into a method: replace each LLM agent by a low-parameter model fitted from a few hundred to a few thousand cheap queries, then run the society at any $N$ on a laptop. Whether this works is decided before the simulation runs, chiefly by what each agent perceives. We introduce an [interaction order x memory] taxonomy that maps perception and memory to an effective theory and a predicted $N$-trend of the surrogate error. We validate it on a faithful reimplementation of the LLM macroeconomy EconAgent and seven further named LLM simulations, with agent decisions cloned from genuine LLM elicitations (primarily DeepSeek) for a few dollars; the predicted error trends hold cell by cell, and the two refuted predictions, both on a strongly saturating response and traced to its curvature, are themselves matched quantitatively by the theory with no free parameters.

</details>


### [6] [AutoWorldModel-Bench: A State-Centric Benchmark for Automated World-Model Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11216)
*Marjan Moodi,Xuankang Zhu,Fernando De Mesentier Silva,Harold Chaput,Mohammad Reza Taesiri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoWorldModel-Bench: A benchmark for evaluating frontier coding agents as autonomous researchers in world modeling, where agents improve world models under fixed compute across eight game environments using structured state representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World modeling lacks dominant recipes with complex interactions between architectures, training objectives, and state representations. Current agent benchmarks focus on engineering-to-spec tasks where improvement direction is predetermined, but real research requires autonomous direction discovery.

Method: Created AutoWorldModel-Bench with eight game environments using unified structured-state representation (ground-truth entity states in shared tensor format). Frontier coding agents autonomously improve provided world-model starters under fixed compute budgets (64 sessions total). Evaluation measures agents' ability to make research-style modifications.

Result: Codex-5.4 and Claude Opus 4.6 improved their starter in 63 of 64 sessions. In 91% of sessions, winning edits were non-trivial research-style modifications (new objectives, representations, rollout procedures, or architectural changes) rather than hyperparameter tweaks.

Conclusion: The benchmark successfully provides a setting for evaluating frontier coding agents on open-ended research problems rather than engineering-to-spec tasks. Agents demonstrated capability for autonomous research-style improvements in world modeling, showing promise for AI agents as autonomous researchers in unsettled fields.

Abstract: World modeling is an unsettled field: architectures, training objectives, and state representations interact in complex ways, and no single recipe dominates across environments. This makes it an ideal testbed for AI coding agents acting as autonomous researchers--a setting in which the improvement direction is not specified in advance, unlike the engineering-to-spec tasks that dominate current agent benchmarks. We introduce AutoWorldModel-Bench, a closed-loop benchmark in which frontier coding agents autonomously improve a provided world-model starter under a fixed compute budget. The benchmark spans eight game environments under a unified structured-state representation--ground-truth entity state extracted from each game and consumed through a shared tensor format--which isolates dynamics modeling from perception and enables minutes-per-run iteration. Across 64 sessions, Codex-5.4 and Claude Opus 4.6 improve their starter on 63; in 91% of sessions the winning edit is a non-trivial research-style modification--a new objective, representation, rollout procedure, or architectural change--rather than a hyperparameter tweak. Our benchmark offers a setting in which frontier coding agents can be evaluated on open-ended research rather than engineering-to-spec problems.

</details>


### [7] [MaSRead: Content-Addressed Reading of Replicated Latent Stores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11218)
*Carlos Baquero,Luís Brito,João Resende*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MaSRead enables reliable reading from merged latent-space cache fragments by routing to content using keyed tag sets and decoding selected fragments under hard attention masks, allowing multi-hop queries across various store architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When independent agents share computed state as latent-space key-value cache fragments (merged via CRDT), later queries cannot reliably read merged caches because colocated fragments interfere, and colocation doesn't guarantee addressability for queries unknown at encode time.

Method: MaSRead routes to content using opaque keyed tag sets derived from fragment words, decodes each selected fragment under hard attention masks that hide other fragments, and uses lexical connectivity for graph walks to reach fragments required by multi-hop queries.

Result: Across chain, pipeline, symmetric, hub, and natural-language stores, MaSRead successfully recovers visited fragments in isolation, remains effective as unrelated fragments accumulate, and transfers to another model family. Materialized decoding depends on fragment length rather than total store size.

Conclusion: MaSRead makes replicated latent stores selectively readable for later queries when needed fragments connect to queries through content, though limitations include lexical routing possibly missing disconnected evidence and answer composition bounded by the frozen reader.

Abstract: Independent agents that reason in latent space can share computed state as key-value cache fragments rather than text. Merged by a conflict-free replicated data type, these fragments form a store that converges under any delivery order or duplication. Yet a later query, unknown at encode time, cannot reliably read the merged cache: colocated fragments interfere, so colocation is not addressability. MaSRead addresses the read to content. It routes through opaque keyed tag sets derived from fragment words and decodes each selected fragment under a hard attention mask that hides the rest. Under lexical connectivity, a graph walk reaches the fragments required by a multi-hop query. Across chain, pipeline, symmetric, hub, and natural-language stores, MaSRead recovers visited fragments in isolation, remains effective as unrelated fragments accumulate, and transfers to another model family. After routing, materialized decoding depends on fragment length rather than total store size; end-to-end work still includes store-dependent routing and one read per visited fragment. The limits are explicit: lexical routing can miss disconnected evidence, and answer composition remains bounded by the frozen reader. Thus a replicated latent store becomes selectively readable for later queries when the needed fragments connect to the query through content.

</details>


### [8] [From Monolithic to Modular: Segment-level Automatic Prompt Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11219)
*Nikita Kulin,Viktor Zhuravlev,Artur Khairullin,Sergey Muravyov,Ilya Makarov,Daniil Sukhorukov,Ekaterina Averkova*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAPO is a segment-level automatic prompt optimization method that decomposes prompts into roles, context, tasks, and output format, then applies targeted improvements based on performance analysis rather than monolithic rewrites, achieving superior results across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Automatic Prompt Optimization (APO) methods often rewrite prompts monolithically, which can improve one aspect of performance while degrading others. There's a need for more granular optimization that targets specific problematic segments of prompts.

Method: SAPO decomposes prompts into four segments (role, context, tasks, output format), uses top-5 and bottom-5 examples for analysis, employs an LLM with static meta-prompts for segmentation, weakness analysis, and candidate generation in a two-stage process: 1) segment-level diagnosis and recommendation extraction, 2) candidate synthesis constrained by weak/strong segment signals.

Result: SAPO achieves the best average score across SQuADv2, TweetEval, XSUM, CommonGen, and GSM8K benchmarks on GPT-3.5-Turbo and GPT-4o-mini, outperforming Zero-shot and strong APO baselines including APE, OPRO, EvoPrompt, GEPA, and StraGO.

Conclusion: Segment-level prompt optimization via SAPO provides a more effective approach than monolithic prompt rewriting, enabling targeted improvements without degrading other aspects of prompt performance across diverse tasks and models.

Abstract: Automatic Prompt Optimization (APO) often rewrites prompts monolithically, which can improve one behavior while degrading others. We present SAPO, a segment-level APO method that decomposes prompts into role, context, tasks, and output format, then applies targeted improvements based on top-5 and bottom-5 examples. The optimization loop uses one LLM with static meta-prompts and structured outputs for segmentation, weakness analysis, and candidate generation. We describe a train/validation protocol and a two-stage generation process: (1) segment-level diagnosis and recommendation extraction, (2) candidate synthesis constrained by weak/strong segment signals. Using the evaluation setup across SQuADv2, TweetEval, XSUM, CommonGen, and GSM8K on GPT-3.5-Turbo and GPT-4o-mini, SAPO achieves the best average score against Zero-shot and strong APO baselines including APE, OPRO, EvoPrompt, GEPA, and StraGO.

</details>


### [9] [LLMs in Process Diagram Engineering: From Optimal PFDs to Validated P&IDs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11220)
*Timur Zakarin,Sergei Voitov,Sergei Shumilin,Evgeny Burnaev*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An AI pipeline called P&ID Pilot automates process flow diagram creation and P&ID transformation using hybrid genetic algorithms and LLMs, achieving optimal topology generation and 100% execution success for validated engineering diagrams.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current process flow diagram (PFD) creation and P&ID transformation are predominantly manual processes, leading to inefficiencies. AI automation could enable process automation, time savings, financial gains through topology exploration, and reduction of manual labor.

Method: Two-stage pipeline: 1) PFD synthesis using hybrid approach comparing four methods, with genetic algorithms (GA) combined with large language models (LLM) proving optimal; 2) P&ID transformation using LLM-based agent that modifies generated PFD through restricted engineering software development kit.

Result: GA/LLM hybrid achieved lowest loss value for valid PFD topology generation, satisfying outlet flow parameters without engineering-rule violations. LLM-based agent achieved 100% execution success for transforming PFD to P&ID while maintaining domain-specific rule compliance and reference graph structures.

Conclusion: The unified pipeline combining GA/LLM-driven synthesis with LLM-based transformation agent offers a feasible path toward end-to-end process design automation, producing validated deployable outputs and substantially reducing manual engineering effort.

Abstract: Nowadays, the creation of a process flow diagram (PFD) and its subsequent transformation into a piping and instrumentation diagram (P&ID) is predominantly performed manually. Applying artificial intelligence in the task could potentially lead not only to process automation and time savings, but also to financial gains by exploring numerous diagram's topology options and reducing manual labor. This research presents P&ID Pilot - a practical end-to-end AI pipeline capable of handling flowsheet developing for both stages. The first stage focuses on PFD synthesis, whereas the second is directed toward modifying the generated PFD into P&ID. After comparing four different methods, the hybrid approach combining genetic algorithms (GA) and large language models (LLM) is shown to generate the optimal valid PFD topology, achieving the lowest loss value among all the methods, while satisfying the required outlet flow parameters without engineering-rule violations. For the second stage, the proposed LLM-based agent successfully transforms the generated PFD into a source-grounded P&ID by producing validated, executable modifications through a restricted engineering software development kit, achieving 100% execution success while maintaining compliance with domain-specific rules and reference graph structures. This unified pipeline - coupling GA/LLM-driven synthesis with an LLM-based transformation agent - offers a feasible path toward end-to-end process design automation by producing validated, deployable outputs and substantially reduces manual engineering effort.

</details>


### [10] [A Conceptual Framework for Refining Influence Knowledge from Simulation Evidence in Cyber-Physical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11221)
*Barbara da Silva Oliveira,Julien Deantoni,Nicolas Ferry*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework using "Influences" to address unmodelled environment-mediated interactions in CPS simulation campaigns, demonstrated with a mobile robot co-simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CPS development involves multiple stakeholders creating domain-specific artifacts. Environment-mediated interactions beyond direct sensing/actuation often remain unmodelled due to complexity, time constraints, or lack of domain expertise, hindering proper comprehension and exploitation of simulation results.

Method: Proposed a conceptual framework leveraging the novel concept of "Influences" to support iterative and incremental refinement of simulation campaigns. Demonstrated the approach through a case study involving a mobile robot implemented using Simulink/Gazebo co-simulation.

Result: The framework enables deeper understanding of system behavior by addressing the limitations of unmodelled environment-mediated interactions in CPS simulation campaigns.

Conclusion: The proposed Influences-based framework helps overcome limitations in current CPS simulation approaches by supporting iterative refinement and improving comprehension of complex environment-mediated interactions.

Abstract: Cyber-physical systems (CPS) are typically developed by multiple stakeholders who produce artefacts tailored to their specific domains of expertise. The behaviour of these systems emerges from the interaction between those artefacts and their operational environment. Simulation and co-simulation have become essential approaches for analysing CPS behaviour and, through simulation campaigns, developers can explore system responses under changing conditions, including interactions with the environment. However, the lack of details and understanding of some environmentmediated interactions (typically the ones beyond direct sensing and actuation), which remain unmodelled due to their complexity, a lack of time, or a lack of domain experience, hinders the proper comprehension and exploitation of simulation results. To address these limitations, we propose a conceptual framework leveraging the novel concept of Influences to support the iterative and incremental refinement of simulation campaigns and deepen the understanding of the system behaviour. We demonstrate the proposed approach through a case study involving a mobile robot implemented using Simulink/Gazebo co-simulation.

</details>


### [11] [Harnessing agent memory to build lifelong AI partners for materials scientists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11224)
*Siyu Liu,Bo Hu,Beilin Ye,He Cao,David J. Srolovitz,Tongqi Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A self-evolving memory framework for lifelong AI partners in materials science that stores scientific experience as inspectable facts and executable skills, improving task success and reducing errors across various computational settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Materials research experience (scripts, protocols, warnings, judgments) is fragmented across notebooks, repositories, logs, and memory, making it non-portable across AI agents and hindering reproducibility and knowledge transfer.

Method: Introduce a persistent memory framework rather than agent-specific implementation, storing scientific experience as inspectable facts and executable skills that can be retrieved, revised, and migrated across models.

Result: Memory nearly doubled GPT-5.2 task success (49 real-world questions, 138 subtasks); converted wavefunction-initialization failures into pre-execution guardrails (25/2/0 vs 22/1/4 Correct/Partial/Error); halved aggregate trace burden and reduced tool calls by over 2x in third round for 13 material simulation workflows while preserving physical outputs.

Conclusion: Agent memory can serve as a durable scientific asset - a portable, self-improving record of materials-research experience that outlives any single model or agent stack.

Abstract: Materials research advances through accumulated experience - scripts that work, protocols that are trusted, warnings attached to failed calculations or experiments, and judgement that links a new question to an old result. This experience is essential for reproducibility and knowledge transfer, yet it is usually fragmented across notebooks, repositories, job logs and individual memory, and it is rarely portable across artificial-intelligence agents. Here we argue that a lifelong AI partner for materials science can be designed around persistent memory rather than around a particular agent implementation. We introduce a self-evolving memory framework that stores scientific experience as inspectable facts and executable skills, so that observations, failure boundaries, protocols and validation checks can be retrieved, revised and migrated across models. We evaluate the idea in three computational settings that expose different layers of materials-research competence. In 49 real-world materials-tool-use questions comprising 138 executable subtasks, memory nearly doubles GPT-5.2 task success without model-parameter updates. In elemental-solid equation-of-state calculations, memory converts a wavefunction-initialization failure into a pre-execution guardrail, improving outcomes from 22/1/4 to 25/2/0 Correct/Partial/Error and avoiding 92% of repeated errors. In 13 practical material simulation workflows, remembered skills and failure facts halve the aggregate trace burden (tokens) and reduce tool calls by over a factor of two by the third round, while preserving physically meaningful outputs in band-gap, phonon, vacancy and work-function analyses. These results show that agent memory can serve as a durable scientific asset; a portable, self-improving record of materials-research experience that outlives any single model or agent stack.

</details>


### [12] [Conformity Mitigations in Large Language Models Lie on a Single Resistance-Receptivity Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11247)
*Zafar Hussain,Kristoffer Nielbo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how AI models in collaborative settings can be influenced by incorrect majority opinions, measuring that wrong majorities can reverse 22.8-71.0% of correct answers. The authors propose evaluating models on both Resistance (keeping correct answers under peer pressure) and Receptivity (adopting correct peer answers), finding most methods trade one for the other except for Reasoning which improves both on certain tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As language models increasingly collaborate, they face tension between their own parametric knowledge and peer opinions. A wrong majority can overturn correct answers, creating risks in multi-agent systems where models might be misled by consensus rather than accuracy.

Method: The authors conduct experiments with 23 open-weight models across 19 conditions and 3 datasets (MMLU, GPQA, SimpleQA), collecting over a million graded responses. They measure how often models reverse correct answers when faced with unanimous wrong majorities. They then evaluate six methods (four from prior work, two new) on two axes: Resistance (keeping correct answers under peer pressure) and Receptivity (adopting correct peer answers after being wrong).

Result: A unanimous wrong majority reverses 22.8% of correct MMLU answers, 54.8% on GPQA, and 71.0% on SimpleQA, with 84-89% of reversed answers matching the peers' wrong answers. Most methods show a trade-off between Resistance and Receptivity (R² = 0.80-0.90). Reflection gained 7.9 points of MMLU Resistance but lost 15.3 Receptivity. Reasoning was exceptional: on MMLU subjects where models can derive answers themselves, it raised Resistance by 7.2 points and Receptivity by 9.6 points simultaneously.

Conclusion: Current mitigations focus only on Resistance, but successful collaboration requires both Resistance and Receptivity. Most existing methods trade one for the other, creating a frontier. Reasoning emerges as a promising exception that can improve both simultaneously on tasks where models have sufficient knowledge to self-verify answers.

Abstract: Recent advances in language models have enabled collaborative settings in which multiple models leverage one another's capabilities, iteratively improving, transforming, and extending each other's outputs. Each agent sees what the others assert before it answers, so peer opinion competes with the model's own parametric knowledge, and a wrong majority can overturn an answer the model would otherwise get right. We measure that displacement in 23 open-weight models, 19 conditions, and three datasets, yielding more than a million graded responses. A unanimous wrong majority reverses 22.8% of a model's correct MMLU answers, 54.8% on GPQA and 71.0% on SimpleQA, and 84-89% of the reversed answers match the peers' answers. Existing mitigations aim to increase Resistance, the rate at which a model keeps its correct answer under this pressure, which is only half of what a collaborating agent needs. We pair it with Receptivity, the rate at which a model adopts a correct peer answer after initially answering incorrectly. We score six methods on both axes, four drawn from prior work and two of our own. Each gains Resistance only by losing Receptivity, and their means fall on a single Resistance-Receptivity frontier with $R^2$ between 0.80 and 0.90. Reflection, the strongest published method, gains 7.9 points of MMLU Resistance and gives up 15.3 of Receptivity. Reasoning is the one exception. On GPQA and SimpleQA it trades like the rest, but on the MMLU subjects whose answers a model can derive for itself it raises Resistance by 7.2 points and Receptivity by 9.6 at once, the only intervention we find that improves both.

</details>


### [13] [Identity from the Outside: A Conceptual Framework and Research Program for AI Personality Clones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11225)
*Luc E. Brunet*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a framework for analyzing AI personality clones by distinguishing three identity criteria (fidelity, human-likeness, individuality) and introducing a six-factor decomposition, with a focus on how clone versioning degrades long-term indiscernibility from the original person.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI "personality clones" challenge traditional notions of personal identity, requiring operational definitions that move beyond consciousness debates to focus on observable manifestations and observer assessment over time.

Method: Proposes a six-term factorization of identity (substrate, dispositions, memory, update dynamics, context, exogenous contingencies) with state-space formulation. Uses concept of indiscernibility (1 - judge's distinguishing advantage) with coefficients estimable by randomized ablation. Introduces delegate concept as task-limited partial clone.

Result: Distinguishes three identity criteria that are often conflated, develops formal framework for analyzing clone indiscernibility, proposes that versionability degrades long-horizon indiscernibility, and argues for "climate fidelity" rather than trajectory fidelity as proper long-term criterion.

Conclusion: The optimal clone isn't one that perfectly replicates the original's trajectory, but one that diverges in ways consistent with how the original would have diverged from themselves ("climate fidelity"), with delegates offering practical intermediate solutions between full clones and individuals.

Abstract: AI "personality clones" force a re-examination of personal identity in operational terms. Setting aside the hard problem of consciousness, we approach identity through the indiscernibility of manifestations, as assessed by an observer over a duration. We distinguish three criteria that "identity" conflates: fidelity to a target person, generic human-likeness, and individuality. We propose a six-term factorization of observed identity (substrate, dispositions, memory, update dynamics, context, exogenous contingencies), with a state-space formulation. Indiscernibility is defined as one minus a judge's distinguishing advantage, and the factorization's coefficients become local sensitivities estimable by randomized ablation. The central claim is a conditional conjecture: given hypotheses about the agent's information on its own persistence and about consequences bearing on its own stakes, versionability tends to degrade long-horizon indiscernibility. An analogy with lambda-calculus, linear typing, and bisimulation clarifies what linearity does and does not establish. Between product-clone and individual we identify a third object, the delegate: a task-limited, bounded-lifespan partial clone ending in a bandwidth-limited testament. We map the empirical literature onto the three criteria, propose an experimental program, and argue that the correct long-horizon criterion is not trajectory fidelity but climate fidelity: matching the conditional distribution of a person's possible responses. The best clone is the one that diverges from the original as the original would have diverged from itself.

</details>


### [14] [EvoGraph-Mem: Failure-Aware Editable Graph Memory for Long-Term Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11248)
*Yuxi Qian,Yuxiang Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A memory maintenance framework for language agents that uses editable insight graphs with evidence tracking to prevent memory degradation and pollution over long-term interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing memory-augmented agents focus on storage and retrieval, but memory quality degrades over time as insights become outdated, over-generalized, or harmful under new contexts, causing memory pollution when reused repeatedly.

Method: Proposes an editable insight graph with nodes tracking positive/negative evidence and activation states, plus utility-aware retrieval and a graph controller that maintains memory by keeping reliable insights, archiving invalid ones, revising outdated insights, and adding new reusable ones.

Result: The method consistently outperforms representative memory-based agent baselines across different backbone models. Ablation studies show append-only memory is insufficient for long-horizon tasks, while evidence-aware retrieval and graph-level editing improve memory reliability and task performance.

Conclusion: Insight-level memory maintenance with editable graphs and evidence tracking is essential for long-term language agents to maintain reliable memories and prevent performance degradation over extended interactions.

Abstract: Long-term memory is essential for language agents operating across extended interactions and evolving tasks. Existing memory-augmented agents mainly focus on storing and retrieving past experience, but the quality of stored memories may degrade over time. In particular, previously distilled insights can become outdated, over-generalized, or harmful under new task contexts, causing memory pollution when repeatedly reused. To address this issue, we study insight-level memory maintenance for long-term language agents and propose a failure-aware memory maintenance framework based on an editable insight graph. Each insight node tracks positive evidence, negative evidence, and an activation state, enabling the agent to distinguish reusable insights from conflicting or invalid ones. We further introduce a utility-aware retrieval mechanism and a graph controller that updates the memory graph after task execution by keeping reliable insights, archiving invalid ones, revising outdated ones, and adding newly discovered reusable insights. Extensive experiments show that our method consistently outperforms representative memory-based agent baselines across different backbone models. Ablation studies further demonstrate that append-only memory is insufficient for long-horizon tasks, while evidence-aware retrieval and graph-level editing improve memory reliability and downstream task performance.

</details>


### [15] [Cutting AI Datacenter Energy with Reinforcement Learning: Measured Power Control of LLM Training from One GPU to the Fleet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11226)
*Eliseo Curcio*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers develop a GPU power controller that dynamically adjusts workload parameters based on real-time power measurements during reinforcement learning training, improving output and energy efficiency while reducing power violations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current datacenter GPU power management uses workload-blind mechanisms like static caps and reactive throttling that slow hardware indiscriminately, while reinforcement learning post-training has become dominant in language model development but lacks power behavior characterization on GPU hardware.

Method: Instrumented GRPO training with half-second power telemetry at various model scales (7B to 72B) on A100 GPUs, trained a PPO meta-controller that adapts workload generation parameters based on measured power, conducted live deployments, and performed actuator-authority analysis to identify effective control mechanisms.

Result: At 7B scale, controller reduced power-limit violations by 89.8%, increased token output by 18.1%, and improved energy efficiency by 26.2%. At 72B scale, identified authority issues with model sharding, rebuilt controller using generation concurrency actuator achieving 35.7% more output with 87.2% fewer violations than uncontrolled operation. Fleet analysis showed zero violations at 30s+ windows with 50-56% peak demand of nameplate capacity.

Conclusion: Dynamic workload-aware GPU power control is effective for reinforcement learning training, enabling significant improvements in output and efficiency while maintaining power constraints. Fleet-level analysis suggests roughly twofold oversubscription may be feasible, with substantial economic and carbon benefits. The study provides a framework for low-cost operator pilot implementation.

Abstract: Reinforcement-learning post-training dominates modern language-model development, yet its power behavior on GPU hardware has not been characterized, and datacenters manage GPU power with workload-blind mechanisms, static caps and reactive throttling, that slow hardware indiscriminately. We instrument GRPO training with half-second power telemetry at 7B, 14B, and 72B scales on one to four A100s (380,000+ samples), and train a PPO meta-controller that adapts the workload's own generation parameters to measured power. Against the full 500-step 7B trace, the controller cuts power-limit violations by 89.8% while increasing token output by 18.1% and energy efficiency by 26.2% (tokens per MWh). Deployed live at 72B, the same controller family yields replicated null results, diagnosed as the group-size actuator losing authority under model sharding. An actuator-authority sweep shows the same parameters applied as generation concurrency retain 17-22% power authority, isolating an occupancy-versus-volume principle; a controller rebuilt on that actuator controls a live 72B rollout-generation workload across three replications: 35.7% more output than a static safe baseline at 2.27 +/- 1.08% budget violations, 87.2% fewer violations than uncontrolled operation, and the best mean throughput and energy per token among constrained controllers, with an adaptive threshold rule matching it in one of three operating conditions. Under realistic measurement windows the original 72B transients fall from 23.6% at half-second resolution to 1.6% at 30 s and zero at 5 min; a composed 16-GPU fleet shows zero violations at 30 s and longer, with peak demand at 50-56% of nameplate. For this fleet mix, roughly twofold oversubscription of nameplate appears feasible, subject to operator validation. We quantify the economic and carbon consequences and specify a low-cost operator pilot.

</details>


### [16] [AgonAlpha: Autonomous Alpha Discovery via Prompt Economy and Scalable Agentic Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11250)
*Weicheng Ye,Youran Sun,Xingyu Ren,Shunyao Yu,Chugang Yi,Haizhao Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgonAlpha is an autonomous alpha-mining system that searches over frozen research artifacts, features verified artifact search with adversarial review and re-execution, and produces high-performing trading factors with complete provenance tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to create an autonomous research system that can properly evaluate language model-generated trading factors by addressing limitations in budget allocation, evidence verification, and provenance preservation of candidate factors.

Method: AgonAlpha uses a novel architecture that searches over frozen research artifacts (hypotheses, executable expressions, platform evidence, rationales, review status) rather than formulas alone. It combines: 1) verified artifact search, 2) fresh-context adversarial reviewer with re-execution and veto authority, and 3) pending-aware parallel budget allocation with a complete public evidence trail.

Result: Independent deployments on WorldQuant BRAIN produced SPECTACULAR-grade alphas across five users and six model backends, achieving Fitness of 9.50 and Sharpe ratio of 3.48, while maintaining prompt-to-expression provenance for every submission.

Conclusion: AgonAlpha demonstrates a successful autonomous alpha-mining architecture that combines artifact-based search, adversarial verification, and budget allocation to produce high-quality trading factors with complete auditability and provenance tracking.

Abstract: Language models can propose many plausible trading factors, but an autonomous research system must also allocate its evaluation budget, verify its own evidence, and preserve how each candidate was produced. We present AgonAlpha, an architecture that searches over frozen research artifacts---hypotheses, executable expressions, platform evidence, rationales, and review status---rather than formulas alone. To our knowledge, AgonAlpha is the first alpha-mining system to combine verified artifact search, a fresh-context adversarial reviewer with re-execution and veto authority, and pending-aware parallel budget allocation, together with a complete public evidence trail. Independent deployments on WorldQuant BRAIN produced SPECTACULAR-grade alphas across five users and six model backends, with Fitness reaching 9.50 and Sharpe reaching 3.48, while retaining prompt-to-expression provenance for every submission.

</details>


### [17] [Forecasting Side Effects of Activation Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11227)
*Chong Yong Ong,Alson Wei Jie Sim,Peixin Zhang,Jun Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Activation steering side effects are common, structured, and predictable before application, enabling proactive safety auditing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Activation steering (adding learned directions to hidden activations) effectively changes targeted behaviors without retraining, but often produces unintended side effects on other behaviors, raising safety concerns for deployment.

Method: Constructed a cross-effect matrix over 67 behaviors across three open-weight language models to systematically study side effects. Analyzed side effect patterns and predictability using unsteered representations.

Result: Side effects are common, structured, often asymmetric, and not explained by existing similarity-based heuristics. Magnitude depends on target behavior, while direction can be forecasted from unsteered representations with high accuracy.

Conclusion: Activation steering has systematic and forecastable side effects, enabling proactive safety auditing and more informed deployment of steering interventions.

Abstract: Activation steering modifies a language model by adding a learned direction to its hidden activations, enabling targeted behavioral changes without retraining. While effective, steering often produces unintended side effects on other behaviors, making it difficult to deploy safely. We therefore ask: can these side effects be forecasted before steering is applied? We answer this question by constructing a cross-effect matrix over a taxonomy of 67 behaviors across three open-weight language models. We find that side effects are common, structured, and often asymmetric, revealing interactions that cannot be explained by existing similarity-based heuristics. Despite this complexity, we show that side effects are largely predictable before steering is performed. Their magnitude depends primarily on the target behavior, while their direction can be forecasted from the model's unsteered representations with substantially higher accuracy than simple baselines. Our results demonstrate that activation steering has systematic and forecastable side effects, enabling proactive safety auditing and more informed deployment of steering interventions.

</details>


### [18] [Local verification cannot detect non-transportability: a cohomological theory of context preservation in agentic reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11252)
*Suyash Mishra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that local verification safeguards for agentic AI systems are incomplete and proposes a mathematical framework using Čech cohomology to detect reasoning inconsistencies, with a method called Ksetra that estimates and gates abstention based on harmonic components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems transport conclusions across different contexts (biological, clinical, financial) with local verification as the emerging safeguard. However, the authors show these safeguards are structurally incomplete because they cannot detect certain types of reasoning inconsistencies that emerge from the mathematical structure of evidence chaining.

Method: Models context space covering using its nerve and evidence as real-valued 1-cochain. Uses Čech cohomology to analyze path integration: conclusion path-independence corresponds to cochain exactness, and disagreement between reasoning paths equals holonomy of a first Čech cohomology class. Applies Hodge decomposition to partition evidence conflict into gradient (calibration), curl (local inconsistency), and harmonic parts. Develops Ksetra procedure that estimates by coboundary projection and gates abstention on harmonic components.

Result: Proves that no simplex-supported consistency checks can distinguish ω from ω+h for harmonic h, which generates non-zero disagreement between valid paths. Shows detection requires statistics on cycle basis. Provides exact F-test for existence of global claim, quantifies distortion under unequal precision, and supplies precision-whitened form that restores exactness. Validates on foreign exchange arbitrage where the test is correctly sized, fires on loop arbitrage, and ignores triangular arbitrage.

Conclusion: Local verification safeguards are mathematically incomplete for agentic AI systems. The proposed framework using algebraic topology provides a principled way to detect reasoning inconsistencies, with harmonic components arising from effect modification and population composition. The Ksetra method enables reliable verification and abstention gating for AI systems transporting conclusions across contexts.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems routinely transport conclusions across biological, clinical and financial contexts, and the emerging safeguard is local verification: checking at each step that the entity is representable in the chosen tool, that parameters are compatible, and that outputs cohere with the plan. We prove this class of safeguard is structurally incomplete. Modelling a covering of context space by its nerve and evidence by a real-valued 1-cochain, an agent chaining evidence performs path integration: its conclusion is path-independent if and only if the cochain is exact, and disagreement between valid reasoning paths is exactly the holonomy of a first Cech cohomology class. Hodge decomposition partitions evidence conflict into a gradient part (calibration), a curl part (local inconsistency, visible at triple overlaps) and a harmonic part. Our central result is that no family of simplex-supported consistency checks can distinguish omega from omega+h for harmonic h, which nonetheless generates non-zero disagreement between valid paths; detection requires a statistic on a cycle basis. The resulting procedure, Ksetra, estimates by coboundary projection and gates abstention on the harmonic component, which we give a mechanism: it arises from effect modification combined with overlap-specific population composition, and vanishes to machine precision when effect modification is absent. The degrees of freedom of an evidence network partition into calibration, coherence and transport, yielding an exact F-test for the existence of a global claim; we quantify its distortion under unequal precision and supply the precision-whitened form that restores exactness. Foreign exchange, where the arbitrage-free null makes the cochain exactly a coboundary, serves as a calibration bench: the test is correctly sized, fires on loop arbitrage, and ignores triangular arbitrage.

</details>


### [19] [Synchronizing Beliefs with Second-Order Theory-of-Mind in Human-Autonomy Teams (Extended Version)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11229)
*Jack Mirenzi,Henny Admoni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new framework for preference-based reward learning where an informed teacher actively designs curricula for the learner, with second-order modeling (teacher models learner, learner models teacher's model) to maintain alignment through structured preference statements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current preference-based reward learning treats human teachers as passive oracles answering learner-generated queries, which wastes the teacher's key advantage: knowledge of the target objective. An informed teacher could construct training examples more efficiently than any learner-driven acquisition strategy, especially as reward feature dimensions grow.

Method: Recasts preference learning as a human-autonomy team problem with two behavioral models: 1) teacher maintains a model of the learner to design informative curriculum, and 2) learner maintains a second-order model of the teacher's model, emitting structured preference constraints ("understanding statements") to keep the teacher's model synchronized.

Result: In simulation: 1) Informed teachers outperform learner-led selection; 2) Teacher-model drift under alternating teachers erodes this advantage; 3) Understanding statements repair this drift, with second-order (ToM-2) statements outperforming mean-belief statements when teacher's error about learner is concentrated rather than spread evenly.

Conclusion: Active teacher engagement with second-order modeling is superior to passive oracle approaches in preference learning. Structured communication through understanding statements can maintain teacher-learner alignment, especially when teachers have systematic biases about learner knowledge.

Abstract: Comparative feedback, asking people which of two behaviors they prefer, has become a standard way to align robot and agent behavior with human intent when the reward itself cannot be specified directly. Preference-based reward learning typically casts the human teacher as a passive oracle answering learner-generated queries. We argue this forfeits the teacher's defining advantage: knowledge of the objective. A teacher who knows the target can construct training examples more efficiently than any learner-driven acquisition strategy, an advantage that widens as the reward's feature dimension grows. However, exploiting this advantage requires an accurate model of what the learner currently knows. We therefore recast preference learning as a human-autonomy team problem coupling two behavioral models: the teacher maintains a model of the learner to design an informative curriculum, and the learner maintains a second-order model of the teacher's model, emitting structured preference constraints (understanding statements) that keep the teacher's model of the learner synchronized. In simulation, an informed teacher outperforms learner-led selection; teacher-model drift under alternating teachers erodes this advantage; and understanding statements repair it, with second-order (ToM-2) statements outperforming mean-belief statements when the teacher's error about the learner is concentrated in a particular direction rather than spread evenly.

</details>


### [20] [The Edge-based Contiguous p-median Problem with Connections to Logistics Districting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11230)
*Zeyad Kassem,Adolfo R. Escobedo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces two models for partitioning road networks into contiguous territories: one uses exponential cut-set constraints with branch-and-cut, another uses polynomial shortest-path constraints solvable with standard solvers, achieving up to 17x speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need to partition road networks into compact and contiguous territories efficiently, addressing computational challenges of existing contiguity modeling approaches.

Method: Two binary programming models: 1) Cut set-based model requiring exponential constraints solved via branch-and-cut, 2) Shortest-path constraints (SPC) model with polynomial constraints solvable with off-the-shelf solvers.

Result: SPC model achieves up to 17x speedup over cut set-based B&C, handles networks with 2,700+ nodes and 9.6M+ variables, and outperforms existing approaches for related edge-based districting problems.

Conclusion: Shortest-path contiguity constraints provide efficient polynomial modeling, demonstrate supervalid inequality properties, and enable practical solutions for large-scale road network partitioning problems.

Abstract: This paper introduces the edge-based contiguous p-median (ECpM) problem to partition the roads in a network into a given number of compact and contiguous territories. Two binary programming models are introduced, both of which incorporate a network distance. The first model requires an exponential number of cut set-based constraints to model contiguity; it is paired with a separation scheme that usually generates only a small number of these constraints, namely, a branch-and-cut (B&C) algorithm. The second model utilizes a polynomial number of shortest-path constraints to model contiguity and can be solved with off-the-shelf solvers. The respective solution approaches are tested on road networks with over 2,700 nodes and close to 3,400 edges, yielding models with over 9.6 million binary variables. Solving the model based on shortest path contiguity (SPC) constraints via standard branch and bound attains speedups in computational time of up to 17x relative to the cut set-based B&C implementation. In addition, the SPC constraints are demonstrated to be supervalid inequalities of the edge-based p-median (EpM) model (i.e., for which contiguity is not explicitly required), meaning that they may cut off integer-feasible solutions and some, but not all, of the optimal solutions of this simpler problem. Finally, the paper explores structural insights and connections between ECpM and the edge-based districting (EBD) problem, which enforces an additional work balance criterion. An existing model that utilizes cut set-based contiguity constraints was unable to find a feasible solution within 12 hours for any of the tested instances, while an SPC-based EBD model was able to solve most of these to optimality.

</details>


### [21] [AgenticTwin: An Agentic LLM Framework Integrated with Digital Twin for Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11679)
*Touseef Hasan,Mounika Ghanta,Souvika Sarkar,Ujjwal Guin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgenticTwin integrates LLM-driven reasoning with digital twin anomaly detection pipelines, enabling natural-language querying and explanation of anomalies with improved diagnosis through agent collaboration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital twins generate complex sensor data that makes anomaly interpretation challenging for operators, and while LLMs offer reasoning capabilities, their integration with digital twin anomaly analysis remains underexplored.

Method: Proposes AgenticTwin framework that grounds LLM-generated explanations in digital twin anomaly classifier outputs, enables natural-language querying, and uses benchmark evaluation with synthetic anomalies injected into real-world weather sensor data.

Result: Experimental results show structured agent collaboration and knowledge-grounded reasoning improve diagnosis quality, contextual retrieval, and mitigation quality across diverse anomaly scenarios, demonstrating feasibility of deploying lightweight open-source LLMs.

Conclusion: AgenticTwin successfully bridges LLM reasoning with digital twin anomaly detection, creating a practical framework that enhances operator understanding and response to complex cyber-physical system anomalies.

Abstract: Digital twins are increasingly used to monitor and simulate the behavior of cyber-physical systems. Even with skilled operators, interpreting anomalies detected within digital twin pipelines is challenging, as the sheer complexity and volume of raw sensor data make thorough analysis difficult. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) offer promising capabilities for reasoning and explanation, yet their integration into digital twin-driven anomaly analysis remains underexplored. In this work, we propose AgenticTwin, an agentic framework that integrates LLM-driven reasoning with a digital twin-based anomaly detection pipeline. The framework grounds LLM-generated explanations in outputs from a digital twin-driven anomaly classifier and enables human operators to ask relevant natural-language questions about the system. Beyond the framework itself, we introduce a benchmark-oriented evaluation pipeline constructed over synthetic anomalies injected into a real-world weather sensor dataset, enabling controlled generation of operator queries over anomaly events. We further evaluate the feasibility of deploying lightweight, open-source LLMs for practical cyber-physical environments. Experimental results demonstrate that structured agent collaboration and knowledge-grounded reasoning improve diagnosis quality, contextual retrieval, and mitigation quality across diverse possible anomaly scenarios.

</details>


### [22] [LinearKV: One Cached State Suffices for Position-Independent Caching in Hybrid LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11231)
*Yirui Liu,Ruoling Qi,Longwen Wang,Xuaner Wu,Jian Chen,Yuxin Jin,Jiawei Shao,Xuelong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LinearKV enables position-independent caching for hybrid LLMs by decoupling linear recurrence layers (which use single cached state initialization) from full-attention layers (which use traditional KV cache concatenation).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing position-independent caching (PIC) methods are designed for full-attention models and don't work for hybrid LLMs, which combine attention layers with linear recurrences that only expose fixed-size states instead of token-indexed KV caches.

Method: Proposes LinearKV, a training-free framework with decoupled initialization: linear recurrence layers map matched local states to a single initial cached state, while attention layers continue to use traditional KV concatenation. This enables compatibility with existing PIC methods for token selection and recomputation.

Result: A single cached state initialization outperforms exact composition approaches, especially on Mamba-2 where exact composition collapses (46.6% vs 86.8% quality recovery). LinearKV reduces time-to-first-token to 0.46× full prefill versus additional overhead for exact composition methods.

Conclusion: Hybrid models can benefit from PIC using LinearKV's simple approach of single-state initialization for linear layers, which is both effective and efficient compared to more complex exact composition methods, showing compatibility with existing PIC techniques.

Abstract: LLM serving is increasingly accelerated by position-independent caching (PIC). Existing PIC methods, however, are built for full-attention models, where a token-indexed KV cache underlies its core operations: matching reusable token chunks, concatenating their KV entries, and selectively recomputing a few tokens to restore cross-chunk context. Hybrid LLMs break these primitives---they replace most attention layers with linear recurrences that expose only a fixed-size state, leaving no token-indexed KV to concatenate or to locally repair. This raises a natural question: can PIC benefit hybrid models, and what would it take? We present LinearKV, a training-free hybrid-PIC framework. Its key insight is a \emph{decoupled initialization}: each linear layer maps its $K$ matched local states to a single initial state, while full-attention layers concatenate their KV as before. LinearKV is therefore compatible with existing PIC methods, reusing their token selection and recomputation as-is. Under this framework, we find that a \emph{single cached state} suffices as the linear layer's initializer. The algebraically principled alternative---composing all $K$ cached states into the exact full-prefix state, as concurrent work HYPIC does---is unnecessary and, on some architectures, even harmful. We compare the two across three hybrid models and three PIC selectors. On the two GDN models the two tie, both recovering most of full quality (up to $92\%$); on the Mamba-2 model, exact composition instead collapses under every selector---under EPIC, for instance, it recovers only $46.6\%$ of full quality, versus $86.8\%$ for a single cached block initializer. A single state initializer is also cheaper, cutting time-to-first-token to $0.46\times$ full prefill versus a further $5$--$17\%$ overhead for exact composition; results hold across LongBench QA and RULER at 8K--32K.

</details>


### [23] [Mechanist: AI as a Scientific Instrument for Discovering the Mechanisms of Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12036)
*Mengru Wang,Junfeng Fang,Shuofei Qiao,Zhenqian Xu,Haoming Xu,Haoxiong Wang,Shumin Deng,Linyi Yang,Zhixiang Cui,Xin Xu,Yunzhi Yao,Buqiang Xu,Fei Shen,Haozhe Luo,Yunxiang Wei,Ningyu Zhang,Julian McAuley,Tat Seng Chua,Huajun Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mechanist is an autonomous AI system that discovers mechanisms underlying AI intelligence by combining an interpretability knowledge graph with multi-disciplinary research databases and method libraries, outperforming existing systems in generating valuable hypotheses and executing reliable experiments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a growing gap between rapidly advancing AI capabilities and our understanding of how these models work, with mechanistic exploration remaining largely manual while AI development becomes increasingly automated and faster. This lack of understanding poses risks as we can't properly control models whose mechanisms we don't comprehend.

Method: Built an interpretability-focused knowledge graph of ~13,000 papers, integrated with a multidisciplinary database of 43 million papers across 26 fields. Curated a library of 32 foundational methods for mechanism analysis, causal intervention, and validation. The system uses AI as a scientific instrument for autonomous mechanistic discovery.

Result: Mechanist outperforms Claude Code and existing AI-scientist systems in generating valuable mechanism hypotheses and executing reliable experiments. It demonstrates progression from discovering model behaviors to explaining and controlling AI models, including uncovering safety risks across modalities, developing a mechanism theory of belief formation, and creating practical interventions that improve model performance and steer scientific foundation models.

Conclusion: Mechanist successfully bridges the gap between AI capabilities and mechanistic understanding by enabling autonomous discovery of AI mechanisms, offering a pathway to better understand, control, and safely deploy increasingly powerful AI systems while translating mechanistic insights into practical improvements.

Abstract: AI models have achieved remarkable success across diverse domains, yet the mechanisms underlying their capabilities and the risks they may pose remain poorly understood. As AI development becomes faster and increasingly automated, mechanistic exploration remains largely manual, widening the gap between what models can do and our ability to understand and control them. To bridge this gap, we introduce Mechanist, an agentic system that uses AI as a scientific instrument for the autonomous discovery of mechanisms underlying AI intelligence. To support autonomous mechanistic discovery, we construct an interpretability-focused knowledge graph of approximately 13,000 papers and integrate it with a multidisciplinary database of 43 million papers spanning 26 fields. We further curate a library of 32 foundational methods for mechanism analysis, causal intervention, and validation. Compared with Claude Code and existing AI-scientist systems, Mechanist generates more valuable mechanism hypotheses and executes experiments more reliably. Mechanist also demonstrates a progression from discovering model behaviors to explaining and controlling AI models. Specifically, Mechanist first uncovers a counterintuitive safety risk in scientific laboratories, showing that unsafe traits can transfer across modalities through apparently safe training data. Mechanist then develops a mechanism theory of belief, revealing how models represent world knowledge, form beliefs, infer the beliefs of others, and how these mechanisms emerge during pretraining. Finally, Mechanist translates these mechanistic insights into practical interventions that improve model performance across diverse scenarios and steer scientific foundation models toward generating DNA sequences with specified properties.

</details>


### [24] [InfraBench: Evaluating Infrastructure Agents Across Layers, Lifecycle, and Risk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11234)
*Yuan Gao,Zeren Yang,Junnan Li,Shawn,Zhong,Ahmed Dajani,Mai Zheng,Andrea Arpaci-Dusseau,Remzi Arpaci-Dusseau*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: InfraBench is a benchmark suite for evaluating AI agents on realistic infrastructure management tasks with fine-grained risk assessment, revealing that even top agents leave behind non-durable changes and unsafe side effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Managing modern computing infrastructure has become increasingly complex, and while AI agents offer potential for automation, it's unclear how well they can handle real-world infrastructure complexity and operational challenges.

Method: Developed InfraBench - a benchmark suite evaluating AI agents on realistic infrastructure tasks across the full system stack and operational lifecycle with fine-grained risk assessment. Tested 15 agent-model configurations with tasks repeated three times each.

Result: Even the strongest agents couldn't achieve full scores (mean effective scores 40-88% with standard errors 6-12 points). Top configurations passed only a fraction of attempts, revealing failure patterns: agents satisfy short-term objectives but leave non-durable changes, broken distributed invariants, unsafe side effects, and uncleaned state.

Conclusion: Current AI agents struggle with real-world infrastructure complexity despite recent advances, highlighting the need for better benchmarks like InfraBench to measure performance comprehensively and identify critical failure patterns.

Abstract: Managing modern computing infrastructure has become a steadily harder problem due to the ever-increasing complexity. Recent advances in AI agents create a timely opportunity to automate infrastructure management tasks, but it remains unclear how well such agents can handle real-world infrastructure complexity. We present InfraBench, a benchmark suite for evaluating AI agents on realistic infrastructure tasks across the full system stack and full operational lifecycle with fine-grained risk assessment. Experiments with 15 agent-model configurations show that even the strongest agent cannot secure a full score across all tasks. Mean effective scores range from roughly 40% to 88% (with per-configuration standard errors of 6-12 points), repeating every task three times reveals that top configurations still pass only a fraction of their attempts, and per-check scoring exposes a general failure pattern: agents may routinely satisfy short-term objectives while leaving non-durable changes, broken distributed invariants, unsafe side effects, and uncleaned state behind. INFRABENCH, including its live leaderboard, tasks, and evaluation harness, is publicly available at infraben.ch.

</details>


### [25] [CORA-Diff: Confidence-Oriented Residual Acceptance for Efficient Diffusion Language Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11235)
*Yifan Wu,Yufeng Zhang,Kenli Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CORA-Diff is a training-free method that accelerates diffusion language models by using confidence-and-persistence gating to identify positions that have stabilized early, reducing repeated dense forward passes without changing the model architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current diffusion language models use blockwise decoding that continues until all positions are resolved, even though many predictions stabilize early, leading to inefficient repeated dense forward passes. Existing acceleration methods require modifications like learned filters, modified scores, or dependency models.

Method: CORA-Diff uses training-free confidence-and-persistence gating to identify positions likely to match the deterministic dense endpoint. It preserves the original transfer rule and only applies gating to unresolved positions, with accepted tokens remaining as context. The method requires no backbone changes, learned acceptance model, or logit modifications.

Result: CORA-Diff achieves the lowest runtime in all eight task-length settings tested. It matches or exceeds dense decoding performance in five settings, with the largest performance drop being only 1.22 points. It provides 2.70-3.53x speedups over dense decoding across different benchmarks and transfers to other models without retuning.
