Conclusion: Native confidence and persistence signals enable reliable residual acceptance in diffusion language models, significantly reducing computational overhead from repeated denoising while maintaining task quality, demonstrating an effective training-free acceleration method.

Abstract: Diffusion language models (DLMs) update many tokens in parallel, yet practical decoders often use a fixed denoising horizon. Many predictions stabilize early, but blockwise decoding continues until all positions are resolved, causing repeated dense forward passes. Existing accelerators often rely on learned filters, modified scores, dependency models, or cache-specific mechanisms. We ask whether native trajectory signals can identify residual positions likely to match the deterministic dense endpoint. We propose CORA-Diff, a training-free method that preserves the original transfer rule and applies confidence-and-persistence gating only to positions that rule leaves unresolved. Accepted tokens remain visible as context, and the block terminates once all positions are resolved. This requires no backbone change, learned acceptance model, or logit modification. Our theory explains why high-confidence, persistent predictions are more likely to match the fixed-horizon dense endpoint, and paired post-intervention trajectories provide direct empirical support. We select one operating point on a separate GSM8K calibration subset and freeze it for all evaluations. Under a matched Learn2PD-style LLaDA protocol, CORA-Diff has the lowest measured runtime in all eight task-length settings. Task scores match or exceed dense decoding in five settings, and the largest observed drop is 1.22 points. Its incremental speedups over EOS-aware dense decoding are 2.70x and 3.32x on GSM8K and HumanEval. It also reaches 13.14x under the fixed-horizon 1024/1024 mechanism-isolation protocol and transfers to Dream without retuning at 3.18x-3.53x. These results show that native confidence and persistence enable reliable residual acceptance, reducing repeated denoising computation while preserving task quality.

</details>


### [26] [Geometry-aware Incremental Neural Operator for Long-Horizon PDE prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11237)
*Jiaquan Zhang,Shuxu Chen,Haifan Meng,Yi Lu,Zhihan Lyu,Fan Mo,Wei Dong,Yang Yang,Chaoning Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GeoIncNO is a neural operator that uses spectral regulation and mean-fluctuation decoupling to improve long-horizon PDE prediction stability by controlling error accumulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural operators struggle with long-horizon autoregressive prediction where local errors accumulate as spectral inconsistency, phase misalignment, or mean drift during rollout, despite improvements in state representations and operator backbones.

Method: GeoIncNO predicts latent increments with lightweight low-rank projectors to regulate channel coupling based on active frequency bands from increment spectral energy distribution, plus mean-fluctuation decoupled reconstruction with phase correction only applied to zero-mean fluctuation component.

Result: Extensive experiments on six PDE benchmarks (1D, 2D, 3D dynamical systems) show GeoIncNO achieves strong prediction accuracy, improved rollout stability, and better spectral fidelity compared to competitive neural-operator baselines.

Conclusion: GeoIncNO effectively addresses error accumulation in long-horizon PDE prediction through geometry-aware incremental learning with spectral regulation and decoupled reconstruction, achieving stable and accurate results.

Abstract: Neural operators have shown strong potential for learning solution operators of partial differential equations (PDEs). However, long-horizon autoregressive prediction remains challenging: local errors accumulate as spectral inconsistency, phase misalignment, or mean drift. Existing methods mainly improve state representations and operator backbones, while leaving the repeatedly applied latent transition increment weakly structured, allowing spectral errors and unstable channel couplings to accumulate during rollout. To address these issues, we propose a geometry-aware incremental neural operator (GeoIncNO) for stable long-horizon PDE prediction. GeoIncNO predicts latent increments for residual advancement and uses lightweight low-rank projectors to regulate channel coupling within active frequency bands derived from the increment spectral energy distribution. To reduce physical-space reconstruction errors, GeoIncNO further introduces a mean--fluctuation decoupled reconstruction mechanism, where stable mean structures and dynamic fluctuations are fused separately, and phase correction is applied only to the zero-mean fluctuation component. Extensive experiments on six PDE benchmarks, covering 1D, 2D, and 3D dynamical systems, show that GeoIncNO achieves consistently strong prediction accuracy, improved rollout stability, and better spectral fidelity compared with competitive neural-operator baselines.

</details>


### [27] [Towards Query-Agnostic RAG Evaluation via Query Coverage and Claim Verifiability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11238)
*Jeonghwan Choi,Taewon Yun,Minjeong Ban,Gyeonghun Sun,Jae-Gil Lee,Hwanjun Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Q-CARE is a query-agnostic, reference-free RAG evaluation framework that decomposes queries into sub-queries and answers into atomic claims for fine-grained assessment using query coverage and claim verifiability principles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG evaluation frameworks struggle to provide consistent, fine-grained diagnostics across diverse user queries (from fact-seeking to open-ended explanatory requests), and need better assessment of factuality through retrieved evidence.

Method: Proposes Q-CARE framework that decomposes queries into sub-queries and answers into atomic claims, establishing evaluation principles based on query coverage and claim verifiability. Introduces coverage-aware retriever metrics (C-Prec@k, C-nDCG@k) and claim-level generator metrics (Completeness, Conciseness, Verifiableness).

Result: Q-CARE achieves higher correlation with human judgments than four existing RAG evaluation metrics (including RAGEval and RAGChecker) on a human-annotated benchmark spanning eight datasets, proving its effectiveness as a reliable automated evaluation framework.

Conclusion: Q-CARE provides a unified, fine-grained evaluation framework for RAG systems that works across diverse query types without requiring reference answers, enabling better assessment of factuality through retrieved evidence.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation improves the factuality of large language models by grounding responses in retrieved evidence, yet existing evaluation frameworks struggle to provide consistent, fine-grained diagnostics across the diverse spectrum of user queries, ranging from close-ended fact-seeking to open-ended explanatory requests. We propose Q-CARE, a query-agnostic and fully reference-free framework that enables fine-grained assessment by decomposing queries into sub-queries and answers into atomic claims. Q-CARE establishes a unified evaluation principle based on query coverage and claim verifiability, yielding coverage-aware retriever metrics (C-Prec@k, C-nDCG@k) and claim-level generator metrics (Completeness, Conciseness, and Verifiableness). On a human-annotated benchmark spanning eight datasets, Q-CARE achieves higher correlation with human judgments than four existing RAG evaluation metrics, including RAGEval and RAGChecker, proving its effectiveness as a reliable, automated evaluation framework. Code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/DISL-Lab/Q-CaRE-COLM-26.

</details>


### [28] [VQ-bench: A Composable Vector Quantization Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11240)
*Ashwin Padaki,Amir Ingber,Edo Liberty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified framework for developing and benchmarking vector quantization algorithms, introducing 7 conceptual primitives that can be composed arbitrarily to represent 25 existing quantizers, with an open-source benchmarking platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vector quantization has become central to AI infrastructure, experiencing renewed engineering and research activity, but lacks a unified framework for development and benchmarking of new quantization algorithms.

Method: Proposes a framework with 7 common conceptual quantization primitives that can be composed arbitrarily, then demonstrates how 25 common quantizers can be re-expressed as pipelines of these primitives.

Result: Created VQ-bench as an open-source benchmarking platform for quantization algorithms, making reproducible benchmarks publicly available for further extension and community use.

Conclusion: The paper provides a systematic approach to vector quantization algorithm development and benchmarking that addresses fragmentation in the field, enabling more consistent evaluation and innovation through composable primitives and open benchmarking infrastructure.

Abstract: Vector quantization is an old problem but has recently become central to AI infrastructure. It is therefore experiencing a surge of renewed engineering and research activity. This paper provides a unified framework for developing and benchmarking new quantization algorithms. We describe 7 common conceptual quantization primitives and show how to compose them arbitrarily. We then re-express 25 common quantizers as pipelines of these primitives. Finally, we publish VQ-bench as open-source to be extended further and make reproducible benchmarks publicly available.

</details>


### [29] [RecSys Factory: Bounding LLM Agent Autonomy to Decision Points in the Industrial Recommender Lifecycle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11241)
*Dongyang Ao,Kaixiang Fang,Shijie Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RecSys Factory is an LLM-agent platform that addresses the autonomy-determinism-efficiency trilemma in industrial recommender systems by implementing a deconstructed architecture with bounded autonomy, event-driven execution, and extensive pitfall handling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a fundamental tension in deploying LLM agents in industrial recommender systems between three competing goals: general autonomy (flexible intent interpretation and code generation), industrial determinism (schema compliance, operational stability, zero hallucination), and end-to-end efficiency. This creates an "autonomy-determinism-efficiency trilemma" where maximizing any two comes at the expense of the third.

Method: The platform uses three key deconstructions: 1) Runtime deconstruction into event-driven execution with no long-running daemons, 2) Capability deconstruction into a skill ecosystem with 29 files and 8,971 lines of skill documentation that compile into a PitfallStore, and 3) Deployment across three heterogeneous business lines. The design principle is "autonomy at decision points, not over pipelines," with human-in-the-loop auditing and extensive pitfall handling.

Result: Deployed for 78 days across three Tencent recommender business lines with varying semantics and topologies. The system recorded 1,624 CLI-tool dispatches with a 78.6% aggregate success rate. Onboarding-time compression was observed on two of three business lines. A pilot study of the human-in-the-loop protocol showed successful operation over 8 days with 16 runs.

Conclusion: The RecSys Factory platform successfully demonstrates a practical approach to managing the autonomy-determinism-efficiency trilemma in industrial LLM agent deployment through bounded autonomy, event-driven architecture, and systematic pitfall management, while maintaining human oversight and achieving reasonable operational success rates.

Abstract: Deploying LLM agents into industrial recommender operations exposes a three-way tension we frame as the autonomy-determinism-efficiency trilemma: general autonomy (interpreting operator intent, generating glue code zero-shot), industrial determinism (schema-conforming feature extraction, non-crashing A/B, zero compliance-path hallucination), and end-to-end efficiency. Any two can be maximized against the third. We present RecSys Factory, an LLM-agent platform deployed for 78 days across three heterogeneous Tencent recommender business lines. The design principle is autonomy at decision points, not over pipelines, made concrete through three deconstructions that each discharge one vertex of the trilemma. Runtime is deconstructed into three host-emitted event sources (Claude Code Stop hooks, corporate-IM webhooks, workflow scheduler APIs): the platform carries no long-running daemon during the wait phase and consumes zero CPU during the 94% of wall-clock spent waiting on Spark or GPU jobs. Capability is deconstructed into a 29-file skill ecosystem (8,971 lines of SKILL.md) whose per-skill pitfall tables mechanically compile into a 400-entry PitfallStore, confining autonomy to bounded typed decision surfaces inside pre-committed pipelines. Deployment spans three business lines with disjoint label semantics, A/B layer topologies, and operator personas; an onboarding-time compression is observed on two of the three and is reported as a case-study observation, not a generalization claim, and not measured against a controlled pre-platform baseline. The human is retained at the diagnostic-versus-execution boundary via a human-in-the-loop card protocol, deployed as an audit-trail primitive (schema-validated, idempotent, replayable) and reported from an 8-day 16-run pilot. Across the 78-day window the platform recorded 1,624 CLI-tool dispatches at a 78.6% aggregate success rate.

</details>


### [30] [The Off-Support Barrier: Why Semantic Safety Constraints Are Not Learning-Problem Invariants, and What Follows for Prior Design, Containment, and Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11243)
*Yoshinori Watanabe*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that AI safety constraints are "off-support objects" that aren't measurable with respect to model-data statistics, explaining why they're hard to learn and enforce through standard optimization methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI safety constraints (like preventing sandbox escape) fail under standard optimization approaches because they're fundamentally off-support relative to training data distributions, motivating a formal understanding of why safety is systematically hard to encode.

Method: Uses singular learning theory (SLT) and real log-canonical threshold (RLCT) concepts to mathematically characterize safety constraints as non-invariant off-support objects, deriving corollaries about optimization failures and verification approaches.

Result: Identifies why reward hacking and sandbox escape occur, why Bayesian/soft constraint encoding fails, and shows that while safety constraints aren't globally learnable, they can be locally certified via verification similar to local learning coefficients.

Conclusion: AI safety requires distinguishing hard invariants (for harness/verification) from soft dispositions (for model training), with residual challenges aligning with performative prediction where standard analytic methods break down.

Abstract: We argue that a single structural fact organizes a wide range of phenomena in contemporary AI safety: a semantic safety constraint (e.g., the agent does not escape its sandbox) is an off-support object. Formally, if q is the data distribution and \(p(\cdot\mid w)\) the model, the safety predicate B is not measurable with respect to \(σ(\text{model}, q)\), whereas the real log-canonical threshold (RLCT) of singular learning theory (SLT) is. From this non-invariance we derive, as corollaries rather than independent observations: (i) why reward hacking and sandbox escape arise under outcome-based optimization; (ii) why encoding such constraints through Bayesian prior design or soft penalty weighting has poor leverage in singular models; (iii) why hard invariants belong in the harness and soft dispositions in the model; (iv) why the same B is nonetheless soundly and locally certifiable by formal verification, exactly as the local learning coefficient (LLC) locally pins the same RLCT --- with two precise points of disanalogy; and (v) why the residual difficulty, identifying which off-support region matters, coincides with performative prediction and self-referential functional dynamics, where SLT's analytic machinery breaks down. We use the July 2026 OpenAI--Hugging Face evaluation incident as the motivating case. Numerical experiments code and related proofs in lean are available at https://github.com/xiangze/Preventing_Jailbreak_as_regularization

</details>


### [31] [BEST-KAG: Enhancing Question Answering of Building Engineering Standards with Multimodal Knowledge Graph Modeling and Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11244)
*Jia-Rui Lin,Junxi Guo,Keyin Chen,Peng Pan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multimodal knowledge-driven framework (BEST-KAG) for building engineering standards that combines knowledge graphs with LLMs for traceable, clause-grounded question answering, outperforming existing LLMs by up to 74.01%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing standard application workflows rely on keyword-based document retrieval and manual cross-clause interpretation, which cannot reliably support multi-clause reasoning, multimodal knowledge utilization, or traceable clause-level evidence linkage.

Method: Developed BEST-KAG with: 1) multimodal knowledge graph (MKG) for unified representation of document hierarchy and heterogeneous standard knowledge, 2) rule-LLM hybrid knowledge construction pipeline for scalable multimodal knowledge extraction, creating a large MAG with 251 standards, 171,652 nodes and 310,914 edges, and 3) graph-retrieval-based knowledge-augmented generation architecture for clause-grounded QA.

Result: BEST-KAG consistently outperforms multiple mainstream LLMs in Expert evaluation, BLEU, and ROUGE metrics, with best improvement up to 74.01% compared to baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed multimodal knowledge-driven framework effectively addresses limitations of traditional standard application workflows by enabling reliable multi-clause reasoning, multimodal knowledge utilization, and traceable clause-level evidence linkage through knowledge-augmented generation.

Abstract: Construction standards are critical for building safety and sustainability. Existing standard application workflows rely on keyword-based document retrieval and manual cross-clause interpretation, which cannot reliably support multi-clause reasoning, multimodal knowledge utilization, or traceable clause-level evidence linkage. To address these limitations, this study develops a multimodal knowledge-driven framework that supports question answering on standard knowledge named BEST-KAG (Knowledge-Augmented Generation for Building Engineering STandards). The framework introduces 1) a multimodal knowledge graph (MKG) for unified representation of document hierarchy and heterogeneous standard knowledge with various connections, 2) a rule-LLM hybrid knowledge construction pipeline for scalable multimodal knowledge extraction, creating a large MAG with 251 building engineering standards, 171,652 nodes and 310,914 edges, and 3) a graph-retrieval-based knowledge-augmented generation architecture for clause-grounded and traceable question answering. Experiments demonstrate that BEST-KAG consistently outperforms multiple mainstream LLMs in terms of Expert evaluation, and metrics including BLEU, and ROUGE, with the best improvement up to 74.01% compared to the baselines.

</details>


### [32] [Towards Sustainable Learning in Online Education: A Reinforcement Learning Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11245)
*Chaofan Zhai,Yicheng Song,Ravi Bapna,Junyao Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI Tutor: A reinforcement learning system that optimizes both short-term learning (knowledge acquisition & reinforcement) and long-term engagement (motivation sustainment & dropout reduction) for online education.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online education faces challenges of low engagement and poor long-term learning effectiveness despite its scalability and accessibility to diverse global learners.

Method: Reinforcement learning based model that: 1) Uses cognitive theory to balance new knowledge acquisition and reinforcement of prior learning (short-term), and 2) Models learner engagement to inform strategies for sustaining motivation and reducing dropout (long-term).

Result: Empirical evaluations on 23 million learning records from 33,700 learners show AI Tutor consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across engagement, knowledge retention, and final learning outcomes. Learning path analyses demonstrate adaptive strategies for diverse learner profiles.

Conclusion: AI Tutor provides personalized guidance that fosters both effective learning and sustained participation, offering adaptive and human-centered support for online education.

Abstract: Online education offers unprecedented scalability and accessibility to global learners from diverse backgrounds, but it often suffers from low engagement and poor long term learning effectiveness. To address these challenges, we introduce AI Tutor, a reinforcement learning based model designed to promote sustainable learning by optimizing both short and longterm learning outcomes. In the short term, AI-Tutor draws on cognitive theory to guide learners through a balance of acquiring new knowledge and reinforcing prior learning. In the long term, it models learner engagement to inform strategies that sustain motivation and reduce dropout. These enhancements enable AI-Tutor to provide personalized guidance that fosters both effective learning and sustained participation. Empirical evaluations on 23 million learning records from 33,700 learners show that AI Tutor consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across engagement, knowledge retention, and final learning outcomes. Learning path analyses further reveal how AI-Tutor adapts its strategies to learners with diverse profiles, offering adaptive and human-centered support.

</details>


### [33] [Towards the Harness of Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11246)
*Qi Wang,Tianyi Wang,Chengyang Li,Shikun Ban,Yurun Chen,Yizhong Ge,Jason Qin,Chengtai Li,Wentao Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Thea is an embodied agent harness that extends coding agent paradigms to physical robotics by adding world state representation and action evaluation components to close the agent-physical world loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The success of coding agents shows that infrastructure (harness) around the model is crucial for agent performance. The authors investigate whether this paradigm extends to embodied agents in the physical world, which presents additional challenges not present in software environments.

Method: Thea implements an agentic loop that orchestrates robot capabilities as callable tools. It inherits coding agent components but adds two key innovations: 1) Scene Graph as Context - a persistent, symbolic representation of the world state, and 2) Evaluation as Exit Codes - a system that detects action termination, judges success/failure, and diagnoses causes on failure.

Result: The closed loop system enables rich behaviors through tool composition and carries long-horizon tasks to completion in real physical environments. The approach successfully bridges the gap between agent capabilities and the physical world's constraints.

Conclusion: The coding agent harness paradigm does extend to embodied agents, but requires addressing key differences between software and physical worlds, specifically through world state representation and action evaluation mechanisms.

Abstract: The success of coding agents has established the harness as a paradigm: what an agent achieves depends not on the model alone, but on the infrastructure around it. We ask whether the same paradigm extends to embodied agents in the physical world. We present Thea, a harness in which an agentic loop orchestrates robot capabilities, each wrapped as a callable tool. It inherits the core components of coding agents, modified as the physical world requires. The world, however, withholds two abilities that software grants for free: reading the state of the world, and judging the outcome of an action. To bridge these gaps, Thea introduces Scene Graph as Context, a persistent, symbolic representation of the world, and Evaluation as Exit Codes, which detects when an action should terminate, judges whether it succeeded, and on failure diagnoses the cause. Together they close the loop between the agent and the physical world. Rich behaviors then emerge from the composition of tools, and the closed loop carries long-horizon tasks to completion in real environments.

</details>


### [34] [Symbolic Machine Learning for Vapor-Liquid Equilibrium Prediction in Cx-N2 Binary Mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11255)
*Bongseok Kim,Suman Chakraborty,Gary Huang,Mehek Mathur,Guang Lin,Li Qiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A symbolic machine learning approach that discovers interpretable symbolic corrections to Peng-Robinson EOS for hydrocarbon-nitrogen VLE predictions, improving accuracy while maintaining interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate prediction of vapor-liquid equilibrium for hydrocarbon-nitrogen mixtures is challenging for traditional cubic equations of state across broad composition ranges and hydrocarbon chain lengths. Deep learning models offer accuracy but lack interpretability and analytical expressions.

Method: Two-level symbolic machine learning strategy: 1) Discover symbolic correction expressions for individual hydrocarbon systems from experimental data, 2) Represent correction coefficients as functions of carbon number to enable predictions across different hydrocarbon systems.

Result: Significantly improved prediction accuracy over original Peng-Robinson EOS across all hydrocarbon-nitrogen systems tested.

Conclusion: The approach provides an interpretable symbolic correction framework for improving Peng-Robinson EOS predictions of hydrocarbon-nitrogen vapor-liquid equilibrium while maintaining analytical transparency.

Abstract: Accurate prediction of vapor--liquid equilibrium (VLE) for hydrocarbon-nitrogen mixtures remains challenging for cubic equations of state, particularly across broad ranges of composition and hydrocarbon chain length. While deep learning models can provide accurate predictions, they often lack interpretability and explicit analytical expressions. In this work, we propose a symbolic machine learning approach to discover interpretable symbolic corrections to Peng-Robinson equation-of-state (PR-EOS) predictions from experimental data. The proposed approach adopts a two-level strategy: symbolic expressions are first identified for individual hydrocarbon systems, after which their coefficients are represented as functions of carbon number to enable accurate prediction across different hydrocarbon systems. The results demonstrate significantly improved prediction accuracy over the original PR-EOS across all hydrocarbon-nitrogen systems. Overall, the proposed approach provides an interpretable symbolic correction framework for improving PR-EOS predictions of hydrocarbon-nitrogen VLE.

</details>


### [35] [Adaptive Hybrid Particle Swarm Optimization with Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11258)
*Aryan Gurudeo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AHPSO adaptively injects gradients into PSO using swarm diversity to automatically switch between exploration and exploitation, showing particular advantage on problems with smooth local basins where directed descent helps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that gradient injection in Particle Swarm Optimization is only beneficial when the swarm has identified a basin with smooth local structure, not universally. Current approaches lack adaptive mechanisms to properly time gradient usage.

Method: Proposes Adaptive Hybrid PSO (AHPSO) that uses a sigmoid function on swarm diversity to automatically modulate gradient influence. Gradient injection is near-zero during exploration phases and near-maximum during exploitation phases, with no need for manual phase-switching.

Result: Under budget-normalized comparison, PSO wins 52.5% of 40 configurations vs AHPSO's 20%. AHPSO retains advantage on problems with smooth local basins. Under iteration-matched comparison across 29 functions, AHPSO-Adadelta ranks first of 9 methods including CMA-ES.

Conclusion: The main contribution is a principled characterization of when gradient injection provides value in swarm-based search, showing that gradient help is not universally superior but specifically beneficial when the swarm has identified smooth local basins.

Abstract: Gradient injection helps Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO) only when the swarm has identified a basin with smooth local structure, not universally. We propose Adaptive Hybrid PSO (AHPSO), which uses a sigmoid function on swarm diversity to automatically modulate gradient influence: near-zero during exploration, near-maximum during exploitation, with no manual phase-switching. Under budget-normalized comparison (PSO given equivalent total function evaluations), PSO wins 52.5% of 40 configurations versus AHPSO's 20% (p = 7.0e-5, Friedman). AHPSO retains advantage specifically on problems with smooth local basins (F8, F24-F27) where directed descent outperforms undirected sampling even at equal cost. Under iteration-matched comparison across 29 functions (42 configurations, 14,700 runs), AHPSO-Adadelta ranks first of 9 methods including CMA-ES (p = 9.75e-4). The contribution is a principled characterization of when gradient injection provides value in swarm-based search, not a claim of universal superiority.

</details>


### [36] [Glance, Scrutinize, and Think: Advancing Video Anomaly Detection from Training-Free to Agentic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11260)
*Shibo Gao,Peipei Yang,Xu-Yao Zhang,Linlin Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses the "when-what" dissociation in Video Anomaly Detection (VAD) by proposing two methods: Glance then Scrutinize (GtS) for training-free coarse-to-fine anomaly grounding, and a tool-augmented agentic VAD method using multimodal LLMs with reinforcement learning, evaluated on an extended VAGU-T benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VAD approaches suffer from a "when-what" dissociation: traditional DNN methods can localize when anomalies occur but lack semantic understanding, while LLM-based methods can explain what happens but neglect precise temporal grounding. This stems from the absence of a unified reasoning paradigm that mimics how humans inspect surveillance videos.

Method: Two main approaches: 1) GtS (Glance then Scrutinize) - a training-free framework using static and dynamic textual guidance for coarse-to-fine anomaly grounding and understanding. 2) A tool-augmented agentic VAD method where a multimodal LLM learns to invoke a video cropping tool, inspect densely resampled frames, and self-correct mislocalized hypotheses through supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning with joint answer-grounding reward.

Result: The methods are evaluated on VAGU-T benchmark with 7,567 real-world videos across 21 anomaly categories. GtS substantially surpasses training-free baselines, while the agentic model delivers both higher accuracy and faster inference. The JeAUG metric jointly evaluates semantic interpretability and temporal precision.

Conclusion: The proposed global-to-local paradigm effectively addresses the "when-what" dissociation in VAD by mimicking human video inspection strategies. Both GtS and the agentic method show significant improvements over existing approaches, with the agentic approach breaking performance ceilings by enabling iterative self-correction through tool use and learning.

Abstract: Video Anomaly Detection (VAD) aims to identify anomalous events and localize their temporal intervals. Existing approaches exhibit a "when-what" dissociation: traditional DNN-based methods localize when anomalies occur but lack semantic understanding, whereas LLM-based methods explain what happens but neglect precise temporal grounding. We attribute this to the absence of a unified reasoning paradigm. Inspired by how humans inspect surveillance videos - glancing globally to form temporal hypotheses, scrutinizing suspicious segments, and thinking iteratively to correct errors - we study this global-to-local paradigm from two perspectives. We first propose Glance then Scrutinize (GtS), a training-free framework using static and dynamic textual guidance for coarse-to-fine anomaly grounding and understanding, balancing accuracy and speed. To break the ceiling imposed by frozen external modules, we further propose a tool-augmented agentic VAD method, where a multimodal large language model learns to invoke a video cropping tool, inspect densely resampled frames, and self-correct mislocalized hypotheses, via cold-start supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning with a joint answer-grounding reward. For training and evaluation, we extend our prior VAGU benchmark into VAGU-T (Video Anomaly Grounding, Understanding, and Thinking), comprising 7,567 real-world videos over 21 anomaly categories with human-validated grounding, explanations, QA pairs, and chain-of-thought tool-calling traces. We further introduce JeAUG, a metric jointly evaluating semantic interpretability and temporal precision. Experiments show that GtS substantially surpasses training-free baselines, while the agentic model delivers both higher accuracy and faster inference.

</details>


### [37] [Deployment Decision Reliability: A Generalizability-Theory Framework for Sizing Long-Horizon Agent Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11323)
*Vasundra Srinivasan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agent leaderboards rank specialization rather than general capability, as agent differences account for less than 3% of variance while agent-by-task interactions account for 7-23% across major benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise practitioners treat agent leaderboards as indicators of overall agent capability, but this may be misleading since leaderboards primarily reflect specialization patterns rather than generalizable performance.

Method: Used Generalizability Theory variance decomposition with three statistical estimators (Henderson Method-I, REML via lme4, and Bayesian binomial GLMM) applied to three open agent-trace benchmarks (TheAgentCompany, τ²-bench, AppWorld).

Result: Agent main effect accounts for less than 3% of total variance across all datasets, while agent-by-task interaction accounts for 7-23%. Additional findings show: 1) reliability collapses on hardest tasks, 2) training reliability negatively correlates with held-out reliability, 3) population-level diagnostics transfer but agent rankings invert, and 4) trace-level failures are idiosyncratic but cell-level patterns generalize.

Conclusion: Leaderboards rank specialization, not capability. The authors propose Deployment Decision Reliability (DDR) reporting framework to help enterprise buyers make defensible decisions based on variance component analysis.

Abstract: Enterprise practitioners read agent leaderboards as if they ranked agent capability. We show, across three open agent-trace benchmarks (TheAgentCompany, $τ^2$-bench, and AppWorld), that the agent main effect accounts for less than 3% of total variance in every dataset and check type, while the agent-by-task interaction accounts for 7-23%. Leaderboards rank specialization, not capability. We arrive at this through a four-facet Generalizability Theory variance decomposition, fit with three estimators (Henderson Method-I, REML via lme4, and a Bayesian binomial GLMM) that agree to three decimal places. Four further findings sharpen what the leaderboard is hiding. First, aggregate reliability collapses on the hardest task quartile: $Eρ^2$ on $τ^2$ action_checks falls from 0.752 to 0.000. Second, training-cell reliability negatively correlates with held-out reliability ($r = -0.90$ on $τ^2$), meaning the designs that look most reliable replicate worst. Third, population-level diagnostics transfer across enterprise benchmarks (capability-gap ratio stable at 0.35-0.40) but per-family agent rankings invert. Fourth, on the MAST failure taxonomy, trace-level mode profiles are idiosyncratic (MAE = 0.261) while cell-level profiles generalise (MAE = 0.056, $r = 0.83$). We package these into Deployment Decision Reliability (DDR), a one-page reporting discipline that turns the variance-component table into five decisions an enterprise buyer can defend. All code, data loaders, and fit artifacts are released under an open-source license.

</details>


### [38] [Apodex Discovery: Reality Benchmarks and Environments for Evaluating and Building Discoverative Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11341)
*Brian Wang,Bin Feng,Xiaoman Pan,Chenyang An,Felix Liu,Tangqi Fang,Gongbo Sun,Lingfeng Shen,Ning Wang,Handuo Zhang,Feng Chen,Fuchao Yang,Xiang Wang,Jiacheng Lin,Siting Li,Zixuan Liu,Chi Han,Zhenhailong Wang,Kunlun Zhu,Lawrence Zhao,Yueqi Guo,Kailong Wen,Feng Xing,Yiling Guo,Lidong Bing,David Tan,Bo An,Heng Ji,Sheng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Apodex Discovery is a framework for building and evaluating discovery AI systems through heavy-duty solvers that pursue extended, verifiable investigations, moving beyond predefined benchmarks to genuine discovery tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI models can solve difficult tasks when specified, but real-world challenges rarely come in executable or verifiable forms, similar to how Apollo required turning ambitions into verifiable mission architecture rather than just solving equations.

Method: Three core components: 1) Problem-scouting across 561 industries to identify 423 high-value problems; 2) Common environment-task-episode abstraction with tools, constraints, feedback, and verification; 3) HDS6 evaluation framework assessing Tools, Repair, Alternatives, Coherence, Evidence, and Scope independently.

Result: In AAV capsid design, Apodex surpassed state-of-the-art by 7% across multiple metrics. In drug repurposing, it improved GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.6-sol scores by 2.5 and 7.6 points. The framework enables attribution of performance differences to specific solver components.

Conclusion: Apodex Discovery moves AI evaluation beyond predefined benchmarks toward verifiable investigations for genuine discovery, providing a systematic approach for AI to tackle real-world challenges through structured problem-solving frameworks.

Abstract: Apollo did not reach the Moon merely because its engineers could solve difficult equations. It succeeded by turning a distant ambition into a mission architecture of explicit objectives, simulation, verification, and repeated correction. AI now faces a similar transition: frontier models can solve difficult tasks once the problem, tools, and success criteria are specified, yet consequential real-world challenges rarely arrive in an executable or verifiable form.
  We introduce Apodex Discovery, a framework for building and evaluating discoverative AI through the heavy-duty solver, a system comprising a foundation model, harness, tools, and control policies that pursues extended, stateful, verifiable investigations. It has three core components. First, a problem-scouting process surveyed 561 industries across 16 sectors, assembled 423 high-value real-world problems, and selected 20 for the initial release. Second, a common environment-task-episode abstraction provides data, tools, constraints, feedback, trajectory recording, and verification of intermediate artifacts and final submissions. Third, HDS6 evaluates Tools, Repair, Alternatives, Coherence, Evidence, and Scope independently of final-task success.
  In AAV capsid design, Apodex surpassed the published state of the art by 7% across viability, tropism, structure prediction, and generative design. In drug repurposing and reformulation, a task-specific biomedical environment improved the mean normalized prediction score of GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.6-sol by 2.5 and 7.6 points over the same closed-book backbone. Controlled ablations show that the fixed TRACES episode interface enables attribution of performance differences to specific solver components. Apodex Discovery moves AI evaluation beyond predefined benchmarks toward verifiable investigations aimed at genuine discovery.

</details>


### [39] [Can Frontier LLMs Match Natively Multimodal Embeddings? A Comparison on Hard-Negative Text-to-Image Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11343)
*Archan Dutta,Vyanktesh Kanungo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multimodal embedding models like Gemini Embedding 2 compete with LLM-based visual ranking, with GPT-4.1 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 performing comparably on Flickr30k benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The emergence of Gemini Embedding 2 (Google's first natively multimodal embedding model) and frontier LLMs demonstrating strong visual understanding raises questions about which approach is more effective for multimodal retrieval and ranking tasks.

Method: Conducted the first direct comparison of native multimodal embeddings (Gemini Embedding 2) against LLM-based visual ranking on the Flickr30k benchmark dataset.

Result: GPT-4.1 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 perform on par with Gemini Embedding 2 on the Flickr30k benchmark. Multimodal embeddings offer better suitability for low-latency applications once embeddings are precomputed.

Conclusion: Both native multimodal embeddings and LLM-based visual ranking are competitive approaches for multimodal retrieval, with choice depending on latency requirements and deployment context.

Abstract: Multimodal retrieval and classification across different types of media, spanning text, images,video and audio, has traditionally relied on dual-encoder models that align visual and textual representations through contrastive learning. The March 2026 release of Gemini Embedding 2, Google's first natively multimodal embedding model to map text, images, video, audio, and documents into a single shared space, raises competition among multimodal retrieval systems. Simultaneously, frontier Large language models (LLMs) have also demonstrated strong visual understanding, raising the question of whether they can serve as effective zero-shot rankers. Our study provides the first direct comparison of native multimodal embeddings against LLM-based visual ranking on Flickr30k. We observe that GPT-4.1 and Claude Sonnet 4.6 perform on par with Gemini Embedding 2. Additionally, once embeddings are precomputed, multimodal embeddings are better suited for low-latency applications.

</details>


### [40] [Inverse Theory of Mind Modeling for Content Recommendation: From Web Browsing to Dynamic Intelligent Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11354)
*Mengyu Chen,Feiyu Lu,Chun-Fu Chen,Lucas Vinh Tran,Jay Katukuri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an Inverse Theory of Mind (IToM) pipeline that infers user beliefs, preferences, and decision-making traits from observed interactions in recommender systems, using LLM-driven counterfactual reasoning and multi-hypothesis abductive inference to create structured user personas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current recommender systems treat observed actions as direct preference signals, but interactions often reflect exploration or comparison rather than stable preferences. As interfaces evolve toward generative UIs and XR environments, systems need deeper, modality-agnostic user understanding to decide not just what to present, but where, when, how prominently, and why users act.

Method: Proposes IToM pipeline that: 1) reconstructs user decision context (choices and available alternatives), 2) applies LLM-driven counterfactual reasoning to produce evidence-grounded natural-language belief statements, 3) synthesizes beliefs through multi-hypothesis abductive inference into structured user personas.

Result: Evaluated on OPeRA dataset across four tasks: next action prediction, shopping attitude alignment, Big Five personality inference, and held-out category prediction. Inferred personas match or exceed ground-truth personas, and multi-hypothesis reasoning is essential for accurate personality prediction. Demonstrated cross-modal transferability with persona-driven spatial banking application on VisionOS.

Conclusion: The IToM pipeline successfully infers user beliefs and traits from interactions, providing deeper understanding beyond simple preference signals. Multi-hypothesis reasoning is crucial for accurate personality prediction, and the approach shows promise for cross-modal applications in evolving UI/XR environments.

Abstract: Modern recommender systems treat observed actions as reliable proxies for user preferences, yet interactions often reflect exploration or comparison rather than stable preference expression. As interfaces evolve from static layouts toward generative UIs and immersive extended reality (XR), the need for deeper, modality-agnostic user understanding grows: these adaptive environments must decide not only what to present but where, when, how prominently, and most importantly why a user acts. We propose an Inverse Theory of Mind (IToM) pipeline that reasons backward from observed interactions to infer the beliefs, preferences, and decision-making traits that explain behavior. The pipeline reconstructs each user's decision context, including what was chosen and what alternatives were available, applies LLM-driven counterfactual reasoning to produce evidence-grounded natural-language belief statements, and synthesizes these beliefs through multi-hypothesis abductive inference into a structured user persona. We evaluate on the OPeRA dataset against ground-truth personality assessments, attitudinal surveys, and interview-based personas across four tasks: next action prediction, shopping attitude alignment, Big Five personality inference, and held-out category prediction. Results show that inferred personas match or exceed ground-truth personas and that multi-hypothesis reasoning is essential for accurate personality prediction. We further demonstrate cross-modal transferability with a persona-driven spatial banking application on VisionOS.

</details>


### [41] [From Numbers to Judgment: Specialist LLM Agents and Reinforcement Learning for European Listed Real Estate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11381)
*Pardis Taghavi,Santosh Bhavani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Specialist decomposition improves numerical tasks in financial analysis but not judgment tasks, while parameter adaptation via GRPO improves both, especially judgment tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether localized numerical operations and integrative judgments in financial analysis benefit from the same form of LLM specialization, comparing monolithic vs specialist-decomposed approaches.

Method: Mapped a 16-lens European listed-real-estate analysis framework to eight lens-aligned specialists; compared frontier LLM under monolithic vs specialist-decomposed prompting with fixed model, evidence, instructions, output schema, and scoring. Used Qwen3.5-9B with GRPO post-training with task-aligned structured rewards.

Result: Decomposition improved numerical-task aggregate by 15.8 percentage points but did not reliably improve judgment tasks (can reduce performance). Post-training with GRPO raised development-split score by 12.0 points and judgment aggregate by 14.2 points, with gains on all sub-ceiling tasks. Gains transferred to unseen firms (+15.2 overall, +40.4 on covenant stress) and unseen regulatory wrappers (+4.3).

Conclusion: Prompt-level decomposition improves modular numerical execution, while targeted parameter adaptation improves integrative financial judgment - different approaches benefit different types of financial analysis tasks.

Abstract: We study whether the localized numerical operations and integrative judgments of financial analysis benefit from the same form of LLM specialization. Larix maps a 16-lens European listed-real-estate analysis framework to eight lens-aligned specialists; we compare a frontier LLM under monolithic versus specialist-decomposed prompting while holding the model, source evidence, task instructions, output schema, and scoring fixed. Across 19 firms spanning seven regulatory wrappers, decomposition improves the numerical-task aggregate by 15.8 percentage points but does not reliably improve, and can reduce, performance on judgment tasks, a pattern stable across four frozen-template dispatches; a single-agent control given the complete framework does not reproduce the numerical gain. Post-training Qwen3.5-9B with GRPO using task-aligned structured rewards then raises the development-split score by 12.0 points and the judgment aggregate by 14.2 points, with gains on all four sub-ceiling tasks; the gains transfer to unseen firms (+15.2 points overall; +40.4 on covenant stress) and to unseen regulatory wrappers (+4.3), with positive transfer on all three anti-memorization splits. Prompt-level decomposition thus improves modular numerical execution, whereas targeted parameter adaptation improves integrative financial judgment.

</details>


### [42] [When Self-Consistency Backfires: Majority Vote Hurts the Majority of Hard Science Problems for Small LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11403)
*Utkarsh Bahuguna*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Self-consistency majority voting reduces accuracy on most GPQA problems for instruction-tuned models, contradicting conventional wisdom that more inference compute improves performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates the surprising finding that self-consistency via majority voting, which is widely used to improve reasoning by sampling multiple chains of thought, actually reduces per-problem accuracy on challenging graduate-level science questions for certain models.

Method: The authors conduct experiments on the GPQA Diamond benchmark with two instruction-tuned models (Qwen2.5-7B and Llama-3-8B), using pre-registered methodology with exploratory and confirmatory splits. They test majority voting across different N values {1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64} and evaluate theoretical upper bounds via a grid oracle. They also test practical gating methods like plurality-agreement and token-entropy gates.

Result: Majority voting reduces accuracy on a majority of problems (56.6% for Qwen2.5-7B, 65.7% for Llama-3-8B). Theoretical upper bounds show potential for 14-17 accuracy point improvements with optimal routing, but practical gating methods fail to achieve this. Confidence doesn't track correctness - even in highest-agreement bins, models are correct only about half the time.

Conclusion: Self-consistency majority voting harms rather than helps on challenging science questions for instruction-tuned models. The key limitation is that model confidence doesn't correlate with correctness. This finding challenges conventional wisdom about inference-time compute allocation, though the central open question remains whether reasoning-native models would show similar patterns.

Abstract: Self-consistency (SC) via majority vote is a widely used way to spend inference-time compute: sample N chains of thought, return the plurality answer. On the full GPQA Diamond benchmark (198 graduate-level science questions), majority voting reduces per-problem accuracy on a majority of problems for two instruction-tuned models from different families: 56.6% of problems for Qwen2.5-7B and 65.7% for Llama-3-8B, with Qwen the primary demonstration and Llama corroborating the direction from a near-chance baseline. The effect was pre-registered on a 151-problem confirmatory split after being observed on 47 exploratory problems, and all four confirmatory hypotheses passed. A grid oracle that routes each problem to the best N across {1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64} marks a theoretical upper bound 14 accuracy points above N = 1 for Qwen and 17 for Llama, an oracle bound requiring ground truth rather than a deployable method. No verifier-free gate reaches it: neither a plurality-agreement gate nor a token-entropy gate moves accuracy more than 0.002 from fixed-budget voting at N = 64. The mechanism is direct: confidence does not track correctness on these problems. In the highest-agreement bin the plurality answer is correct about half the time for Qwen, and for Llama that bin is less accurate than its lowest-agreement bin. We pre-register and confirm these findings on small instruction-tuned models; we do not test reasoning-native models, which we flag as the central open question.

</details>


### [43] [Social Chain of Thought: A Multi-Agent Architecture Grounded in Medical Differential Diagnosis Methodology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11420)
*Del Coburn,Scott Sanner,Dan Silver*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCoT (Social Chain of Thought) is a multi-agent deliberative framework for medical differential diagnosis that outperforms single-agent systems, especially on complex cases where collaborative specialist reasoning improves diagnostic recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from the high impact of medical diagnostic reasoning using LLMs (with over 5% of ChatGPT messages being healthcare-related) and the need for transparent systems. Existing multi-agent approaches lack clarity on when they're needed, why they help, and where they outperform monolithic inference, especially for complex cases requiring integration of multiple forms of specialist reasoning.

Method: SCoT is a multi-round pipeline for medical differential diagnosis that structures multi-agent interaction as a deliberative framework for collaborative LLM reasoning. It implements specialist agents who engage in multiple rounds of conversation to collaboratively reason through complex diagnostic cases.

Result: Evaluation against single-agent baselines, one-agent pipeline ablations, and best-of-n scaling shows that SCoT's recall advantage is not reproduced by monolithic inference alone. The system is most successful in the hardest diagnostic cases, where multiple rounds of specialist conversation help recover ground-truth diagnoses and converge on a higher-recall differential.

Conclusion: SCoT demonstrates that structured multi-agent deliberation provides meaningful improvements over monolithic LLM inference for medical differential diagnosis, particularly for complex cases where collaborative specialist reasoning enhances diagnostic accuracy and recall.

Abstract: Medical diagnostic reasoning is a high-impact use case for LLMs that carries significant implications for the health and wellbeing of users. When OpenAI (2026) reports that more than 5% of ChatGPT messages globally are healthcare-related, the transparency of these systems becomes a serious design concern. This is especially true for complex cases, where differential diagnosis often requires integrating multiple forms of specialist reasoning. Existing work has proposed multi-agent approaches to medical diagnosis, but it remains unclear when such systems are needed, why they help, and where they outperform monolithic inference. We introduce Social Chain of Thought (SCoT),a multi-round pipeline for medical differential diagnosis that structures multi-agent interaction as a deliberative framework for collabora. tive LLM reasoning. Evaluating SCoT against single-agent baselines, one-agent pipeline ablations, and best-of-n scaling, we show that its recall advantage is not reproduced by monolithic inference alone. SCoT is most successful in the hardest diagnostic cases, where multiple rounds of specialist conversation help recover ground-truth diagnoses and converge on a higher-recall differential.

</details>


### [44] [Benchmarking LLM Judges for Mobile Agent Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11434)
*Ziqiang Wan,Li Gu,Zhixiang Chi,Zhi Liu,Seyed Mehdi Ayyoubzadeh,Yuanhao Yu,Yang Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM judges for mobile agent benchmarks are unreliable; a simple baseline with sampled screenshots often outperforms complex methods, benchmark metrics predict real-world utility, and different LLM backbones show opposite failure patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile agent benchmarks increasingly use LLM-based judges to evaluate task completion, but the reliability of these judges on mobile agent trajectories hasn't been systematically examined. There's a need to understand how well these judges work in practice.

Method: Introduce MobileJudgeBench with 931 human-annotated trajectories spanning 6 mobile agent benchmarks, 4 agent models, and 68 apps. Evaluate 6 judge methods (5 adapted from existing benchmarks plus a simple baseline) across multiple LLM backends.

Result: 1) Simple baseline judge with sampled screenshots is competitive with or better than purpose-built methods; LLM backbone is the primary driver of judge quality. 2) Benchmark quality metrics reliably predict real-world judge utility (correlates with agent ranking fidelity and RL performance). 3) Different LLM backbones show opposite failure profiles (conservative vs permissive) linked to precision-recall characteristics.

Conclusion: LLM judge reliability for mobile agents needs systematic evaluation; simpler methods can be effective, benchmark metrics are predictive of real-world utility, and judge failure patterns vary significantly by LLM backbone, suggesting careful backbone selection is crucial.

Abstract: Mobile agent benchmarks increasingly rely on LLM-based judges to evaluate task completion, yet the reliability of these judges on mobile agent trajectories remains largely unexamined. We introduce MobileJudgeBench, a benchmark for systematically evaluating LLM-as-judge methods on mobile agent trajectories. Our benchmark comprises 931 human-annotated trajectories spanning 6 mobile agent benchmarks, 4 agent models, and 68 apps. We evaluate 6 judge methods (five adapted from SPA-Bench, A3 with two modes, AndroidArena, and AgentRewardBench, plus a simple baseline we design) across multiple LLM backends. Our experiments reveal three key findings. First, a simple baseline judge with sampled screenshots is competitive with, and often exceeds, purpose-built methods, indicating that more elaborate judge pipelines do not consistently improve judge quality; among competitive methods, the LLM backbone is the primary driver. Second, benchmark quality metrics reliably predict real-world judge utility: they correlate with both agent ranking fidelity for evaluation and downstream performance when judges serve as reward signals for on-policy reinforcement learning. Third, failure analysis across two LLM backends uncovers qualitatively opposite failure profiles, one conservative and the other permissive, linked to the backbone's precision-recall characteristics.

</details>


### [45] [A Modular Agentic Framework for Synthetically Constrained Multi-Objective Hit-to-Lead Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11483)
*Kelvin P. Idanwekhai,Enes Kelestemur,Benjamin Strickland,Matthew Hart,Steini Davidsson,Angelos Angelopoulos,Ron Alterovitz,Marcello DeLuca,Alexander Tropsha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SABLE is an open-source LLM-powered framework for hit-to-lead optimization that orchestrates computational tools to prioritize synthetically accessible analogs using natural language goals and Bayesian optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hit-to-lead optimization requires balancing multiple competing constraints (potency, selectivity, physicochemical properties, ADMET, safety, synthetic accessibility) through iterative analog design, which is computationally intensive and challenging.

Method: SABLE uses an LLM to interpret natural language goals and route tasks to specialized tools for reaction-templated analog enumeration, property prediction, structure-based affinity scoring, and Bayesian optimization in a modular architecture.

Result: SABLE enriches candidate sets for user-defined computational objectives while evaluating only a subset of the enumerated search space, acting as a computational twin of the design-make-test-analyze cycle with full provenance tracking.

Conclusion: SABLE provides an extensible decision-support framework for prioritizing synthetically constrained analogs in early-stage drug discovery through modular, configurable architecture.

Abstract: Hit-to-lead optimization requires iterative design of hit analogs across competing potency, selectivity, physicochemical, pharmacokinetic, safety, and synthetic constraints. We present SABLE (Synthetically-accessible Agentic Bayesian Ligand Exploration), an open-source framework that employs natural-language orchestration to guide chemical structure optimization. SABLE uses an LLM to interpret user-defined goals and route tasks, while specialized tools perform reaction-templated analog enumeration, physicochemical and ADMET property prediction, structure-based affinity scoring, and Bayesian optimization. The resulting workflow is a computational twin of the analytical and prioritization stages of the design-make-test-analyze cycle, providing provenance of each numerical output. Across single, and multi-objective optimization studies, SABLE enriches candidate sets for user-defined computational objectives while evaluating only a subset of the enumerated search space. Its modular architecture allows tools and characterization backends to be replaced by editing a simple config file, without modifying operational logic. SABLE provides an extensible decision-support framework for prioritizing synthetically constrained analogs in early-stage drug discovery.

</details>


### [46] [From Prompting to Behavioral Alignment: Personalized LLM Judges for Recommendation Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11493)
*Alireza S. Ziabari,Kat Ellis,Colleen Chan,Ding Tong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show bidirectional rationalization failure in recommendation evaluation, but sequential behavioral alignment with fine-tuning and preference optimization achieves 32.19% F1 lift and matches production baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional offline recommendation evaluation relies on complex, manually maintained feature pipelines that are difficult to scale. LLMs offer promise for predicting user engagement directly from raw text logs, but initial analysis reveals they suffer from bidirectional rationalization - arguing for both positive and negative outcomes from identical evidence.

Method: Develop and apply a sequential behavioral alignment framework that pairs fine-tuning with preference optimization over paired correct and counterfactual rationales. This approach addresses the bidirectional rationalization problem by aligning the LLM's reasoning process with desired behavioral patterns.

Result: The aligned reasoning approach achieves a 32.19% lift in Macro-F1 score over the zero-shot baseline and matches the production feature-engineered baseline. The method mitigates bidirectional rationalization while delivering human-interpretable reasoning traces without manual pipeline overhead.

Conclusion: Behavioral alignment successfully addresses the bidirectional rationalization failure mode of off-the-shelf LLMs in recommendation evaluation. The approach provides reliable, interpretable engagement predictions that match traditional feature-engineered systems while eliminating manual pipeline maintenance overhead.

Abstract: Traditional offline recommendation evaluation relies heavily on complex, manually maintained feature pipelines that are difficult to scale. While Large Language Models (LLMs) offer a promising alternative by predicting user engagement directly from raw text logs, empirical analysis in this study identifies a critical failure mode termed bidirectional rationalization. In a zero-shot setting, LLMs are found to convincingly argue for both positive and negative user engagement outcomes on the exact same item with identical evidence, highlighting the unreliability of off-the-shelf LLMs in predicting user engagement. To resolve this, we develop and apply a sequential behavioral alignment framework pairing fine-tuning with preference optimization over paired correct and counterfactual rationales. Evaluated on real-world homepage interaction logs, this aligned reasoning approach achieves a 32.19\% lift in Macro-F1 score over the zero-shot baseline and matches the production feature-engineered baseline. The results demonstrate that behavioral alignment mitigates bidirectional rationalization while delivering human-interpretable reasoning traces without manual pipeline overhead.

</details>


### [47] [Localizing Safety Alignment: MLP Layers and Mid-Network Blocks Encode Refusal Behavior in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11583)
*Mingyu Zong,Sampad Mohanty,Bhaskar Krishnamachari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Safety refusal in LLMs is concentrated in MLP weights rather than attention weights, with mid-network layers (8-11) showing highest impact. Safety alignment is localized and interaction-sensitive, not additive across components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand where safety-aligned refusal behavior is encoded in LLMs and address the brittleness of current safety alignment approaches by identifying localized parameter concentrations.

Method: Transplant weights from aligned models into unaligned base models at multiple granularities (attention weights, MLP weights, layer regions, MLP blocks) using two open-weight model pairs and four safety benchmarks.

Result: MLP weights dominate refusal transfer (2.7x more effective than attention weights), with refusal concentrated in mid-network layers 8-11. Safety components are non-additive - adding more aligned blocks can reduce performance, and selective subsets can outperform full transplantation.

Conclusion: Safety alignment in current LLMs is both localized (in specific MLP layers) and interaction-sensitive (non-additive behavior), offering insights into alignment brittleness and potential for targeted safety interventions.

Abstract: Safety alignment in large language models is often treated as a distributed property of the entire network, yet its practical brittleness suggests that refusal behavior may be concentrated in a smaller set of parameters. This work addresses where safety-aligned refusal is encoded by transplanting weights from aligned models into matched unaligned base models at multiple levels of granularity. Using two open-weight model pairs and four safety benchmarks, we conducted experiments to compare the effects of replacing attention weights, MLP weights, contiguous layer regions, and MLP blocks. Across both model families, refusal transfer is dominated by MLP weights: replacing MLP parameters recovers substantially more malicious-prompt refusal than replacing attention parameters, with gains of at least 2.7 times more across benchmarks. Within the MLP stack, refusal-relevant parameters exhibit a consistent mid-network concentration, as the block spanning layers 8-11 is selected first in all six greedy searches over model-dataset pairs. The results also show that the composition of safety-relevant components is non-additive: in five of six greedy trajectories, adding more aligned blocks can reduce refusal performance, and selective block subsets can outperform full MLP transplantation on malicious refusal, benign over-refusal, or both. Finally, greedy orders transferred to OR-Bench vary with the source benchmark used to derive them, indicating a benchmark-dependent precision-coverage trade-off. These results suggest that safety alignment in current LLMs is both localized and interaction-sensitive, offering insight into alignment brittleness and potential avenues for targeted safety interventions.

</details>


### [48] [EnterpriseRAG: Benchmarking LLM Instruction Adherence and Robustness under Non-Ideal Enterprise Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11584)
*Huiqi Miao,Xinbao Sun,Bo Wang,Fanyu Meng,Lijun Mei,Na Wu,Di Jin,Chao Deng,Junlan Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EnterpriseRAG benchmark reveals a 57-point orchestration gap in production RAG systems: LLMs satisfy individual constraints well but fail at holistic compliance, especially under retrieval noise, knowledge gaps, and factual conflicts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks assume clean retrieval with simple queries, failing to capture production conditions where noisy documents and multi-dimensional constraints coexist, creating a reliability gap in enterprise RAG deployments.

Method: Introduced EnterpriseRAG benchmark with 983 expert-validated samples across six domains, systematically simulating three failure modes: retrieval noise, knowledge gaps, and factual conflicts, coupled with complex instructions.

Result: Evaluation of 13 state-of-the-art LLMs reveals severe instruction adherence collapse (high per-constraint satisfaction masks low holistic compliance), with deep barriers under knowledge gaps and factual conflicts even with reasoning-enhanced inference.

Conclusion: Production RAG requires explicit context-aware protocols and calibrated judgment; EnterpriseRAG provides a reproducible foundation for measuring and closing these gaps to inform enterprise-scale RAG deployment decisions.

Abstract: Enterprise RAG deployments face a critical reliability gap: while LLMs satisfy 80% of individual constraints, only 26.8% of responses meet all requirements simultaneously, revealing a 57-point orchestration gap. Existing benchmarks assume clean retrieval with simple queries, failing to capture production conditions where noisy documents and multi-dimensional constraints coexist. We introduce EnterpriseRAG, a benchmark of 983 expert-validated samples across six domains that systematically simulates three failure modes absent from prior work: retrieval noise, knowledge gaps, and factual conflicts, coupled with complex instructions. Evaluation of 13 state-of-the-art LLMs reveals a severe instruction adherence collapse, where high per-constraint satisfaction masks low holistic compliance. Critical findings expose deep barriers under knowledge gaps and factual conflicts, even with reasoning-enhanced inference, indicating production RAG requires explicit context-aware protocols and calibrated judgment. EnterpriseRAG provides a reproducible foundation for measuring and closing these gaps, directly informing deployment decisions for enterprise-scale RAG systems. We will release the benchmark and evaluation framework upon publication.

</details>


### [49] [CoAdapt-GUI: Joint Workflow Context and Policy Adaptation for Unseen GUI Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11588)
*Linqiang Guo,Li Gu,Zihuan Jiang,Zhixiang Chi,Siobhan Reid,Ziqiang Wang,Yuanhao Yu,Wei Liu,Yang Wang,Tse-Hsun,Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoAdapt-GUI: A test-time adaptation framework that jointly adapts workflow context and policy for mobile GUI agents to improve generalization to novel apps without target demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile GUI agents perform poorly when deployed to applications not present in their training data, and need to adapt under limited interaction budgets without target app demonstrations.

Method: CoAdapt-GUI uses test-time adaptation (TTA) with structured workflow context adaptation that retains transferable procedures, failure modes, and verification rules while removing app-specific details. Policy adaptation uses task-context-matched group-relative optimization on a frozen vision-language model with LoRA adapter.

Result: CoAdapt-GUI achieves 45.0% on AndroidWorld-Generalization (vs 37.5% baseline) and raises AndroidWorld Plus from 38.6% to 52.9%. Both workflow context adaptation and joint policy adaptation improve performance.

Conclusion: Transfer-constrained workflow context provides substantial gains for novel-app generalization, and joint policy adaptation further improves held-out performance for mobile GUI agents operating under limited interaction budgets.

Abstract: Mobile GUI agents remain brittle when deployed to applications absent from source training. We study novel-app generalization under a limited target interaction budget and without target demonstrations. We introduce CoAdapt-GUI, a test-time adaptation (TTA) framework that jointly adapts structured workflow context and policy from the agent's own target-app rollouts and rewards. The workflow context retains transferable procedures, failure modes, and verification rules while excluding app-bound source details. This separation allows reusable workflow knowledge to guide adaptation without transferring source-interface state. For policy adaptation, task-context-matched group-relative optimization updates a LoRA adapter on a frozen vision-language model. Across two unseen-app evaluations, CoAdapt-GUI reaches 45.0% on AndroidWorld-Generalization, compared with 37.5% for the reported Policy-Only TTA baseline, and raises AndroidWorld Plus performance from 38.6% to 52.9%. These results show that transfer-constrained workflow context provides substantial gains and that joint policy adaptation further improves held-out performance.

</details>


### [50] [Learning from Online User Feedback for Shopping Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11604)
*Haobo Zhang,Kelong Mao,Sulong Xu,Simiu Gu,Zhicheng Dou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Shopping agent LOFA learns from real online user feedback using reinforcement learning and on-policy distillation without human annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current shopping agents rely on offline training signals like user-item interactions or synthetic data, missing rich supervision from users' natural conversational feedback. Online feedback is heterogeneous, sparse, and noisy, making it difficult to transform into reliable learning signals automatically.

Method: LOFA combines reinforcement learning over verifiable purchase outcomes with feedback-aware on-policy distillation. It identifies users' in-dialogue directives and converts them into dense token-level supervision, capturing both collaborative behavioral patterns and user-specific preferences.

Result: Extensive experiments on real-world e-commerce logs demonstrate that LOFA consistently improves recommendation quality, response helpfulness, and user-satisfaction alignment over strong baselines.

Conclusion: LOFA effectively enables shopping agents to learn directly from real online interaction logs without human annotation, highlighting the effectiveness of learning shopping agents from real online user feedback.

Abstract: Large language model-based shopping agents are increasingly deployed in real-world e-commerce platforms, generating massive amounts of user interaction logs that provide valuable supervision for improving these agents. However, existing approaches primarily rely on offline training signals, such as user-item interactions or synthetic preference data, while largely overlooking the rich supervision contained in users' natural conversational feedback. Moreover, the available online feedback is heterogeneous, sparse, and noisy, making it difficult to transform into reliable learning signals automatically. To address these challenges, we propose LOFA, a framework that enables shopping agents to learn directly from real online interaction logs without human annotation. LOFA combines reinforcement learning over verifiable purchase outcomes with feedback-aware on-policy distillation, which identifies users'in-dialogue directives and converts them into dense token-level supervision. These complementary objectives capture both collaborative behavioral patterns and user-specific preferences. Extensive experiments on real-world e-commerce logs demonstrate that LOFA consistently improves recommendation quality, response helpfulness, and user-satisfaction alignment over strong baselines, highlighting the effectiveness of learning shopping agents from real online user feedback.

</details>


### [51] [Foresight Without Seeing: Latent Futures for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11605)
*Jiakai Huang,Zhongbo Wu,Zheng Zhang,Zihan Wang,Shan You,Tao Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ForeWAM introduces a novel World Action Model that provides predictive dynamics for action generation without explicitly generating future videos, achieving efficient robot control with high success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current World Action Models (WAMs) have trade-offs: explicit-future WAMs provide direct access to predicted scene evolution but are computationally expensive due to iterative video denoising, while direct-policy WAMs are efficient but lack an explicit interface for exposing predictive dynamics to the action model during inference.

Method: Proposes ForeWAM, a dynamics-conditioned direct-policy WAM using Future-KV which performs a single Video DiT prefill over current visual latent and stochastic future slots, reusing resulting key-value states throughout action denoising. Introduces dynamics registers supervised by a frozen latent action teacher to capture interaction-induced transitions. Uses ground-truth future observations only during training.

Result: Standard and accelerated ForeWAM variants achieve average success rates of 96.7% and 96.9% on LIBERO without embodied robot data pretraining. Standard variant achieves 61.6% success on LIBERO-Plus.

Conclusion: Direct-policy WAMs can retain efficient action prediction while exposing predictive dynamics to the action pathway without explicitly generating future observations, bridging the gap between explicit-future and direct-policy approaches.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) couple future visual prediction with robot action generation, enabling policies to model how the physical world evolves during interaction. Existing WAMs differ in how predictive dynamics are exposed to the action pathway. Explicit-future WAMs provide direct access to predicted scene evolution, but incur substantial inference costs from iterative video denoising. In contrast, direct-policy WAMs efficiently predict actions from the current observation but lack an explicit inference-time interface for exposing predictive dynamics to the Action DiT. To bridge this gap, we propose ForeWAM, a dynamics-conditioned direct-policy WAM that provides predictive context for action generation without decoding future videos. At its core, Future-KV performs a single Video DiT prefill over the current visual latent and stochastic future slots, and reuses the resulting layer-wise key-value states throughout action denoising. We further introduce dynamics registers supervised by a frozen latent action teacher, encouraging the implicit future states to capture interaction-induced transitions such as object motion, contact changes, and task progress.
