Ground-truth future observations and the teacher are used only during training; deployment requires neither and performs no future video generation. Without embodied robot data pretraining, the standard and accelerated variants of ForeWAM achieve average success rates of 96.7% and 96.9% on LIBERO, respectively. The standard variant further achieves 61.6% success on LIBERO-Plus. These results demonstrate that direct-policy WAMs can retain efficient action prediction while exposing predictive dynamics to the action pathway without explicitly generating future observations.

</details>


### [52] [MBA: Multimodal Benchmark and Agents for Real-World Business Ideation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11616)
*Hojun Choi,Jaeyo Shin,Suin Lee,Hyunjung Shim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MBA-Bench is the first multimodal benchmark for training and evaluating business ideation agents, featuring 30K samples across six domains, with novel methods for blind and known criteria optimization that significantly outperform baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing business ideation approaches using LLMs are confined to text-only paradigms, but real-world business contexts are inherently multimodal with visual cues that text alone cannot fully convey. There's a need for multimodal evaluation benchmarks for business ideation agents.

Method: Created MBA-Bench with 30K multimodal samples across six domains. Automatically captioned images and used GPT-4o to generate reference ideas via retrieval query generation, market evidence retrieval, and evidence-augmented synthesis. Developed MBA-b (blind) and MBA-k (known) variants with different reward objectives. Trained via LoRA-based supervised fine-tuning followed by group relative policy optimization with setting-specific rewards (creativity/feasibility for MBA-b, plus six additional disclosed criteria for MBA-k).

Result: MBA-b and MBA-k outperformed caption-only baselines by 63.9% and 77.1%, respectively, and multimodal baselines by 25.6% and 35.8%. The multimodal baselines approached closed-source performance on several metrics.

Conclusion: The MBA-Bench benchmark successfully addresses the multimodal nature of business ideation and provides effective training frameworks that significantly outperform existing approaches. The proposed methods demonstrate substantial improvements in both blind and known criteria settings, validating the importance of multimodal integration for business ideation tasks.

Abstract: Agentic systems powered by large language models (LLMs) have opened new opportunities for business ideation. Yet existing approaches remain confined to a text-only paradigm, despite the inherently multimodal nature of real-world contexts. We thus introduce MBA-Bench, the first multimodal benchmark for training and evaluating business ideation agents, comprising 30K samples across six domains, each domain characterized by distinct visual cues not fully conveyed by text alone. Concretely, we automatically caption images and employ GPT-4o to generate five reference ideas for each of three business questions through retrieval query generation, market evidence retrieval, and evidence-augmented synthesis. Following prior work, we evaluate agents across six business-oriented criteria using MLLM-as-a-Judge. To consider settings where criteria are hidden or disclosed, we present MBA-b and MBA-k for blind and known, respectively. We train both with two novel reward objectives---creativity and feasibility---while MBA-k further optimizes the six disclosed criteria for eight in total. Both are trained via LoRA-based supervised fine-tuning followed by group relative policy optimization with these setting-specific rewards. For extensive experiments on MBA-Bench, we set up two baselines accommodating either captions only or multimodal inputs, with the latter nearing closed-source performance on several metrics. MBA-b and MBA-k outperform caption baselines by 63.9% and 77.1%, and multimodal baselines by 25.6% and 35.8%, respectively.

</details>


### [53] [Making AI-Generated Feedback Matter: From Provision to Student Enactment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11625)
*Omar Alsaiari,Nilufar Baghaei,Jason M. Lodge,Dragan Gaševi'c,Naomi Winstone,Hassan Khosravi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI feedback systems need more than just generating feedback - they require workflows that actively engage students in feedback literacy processes to be educationally effective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Feedback is crucial for learning but faces two challenges: providing quality, timely, personalized feedback at scale, and helping students productively interpret and act on that feedback. While AI can address the first challenge (provision), student uptake of AI-generated feedback remains limited.

Method: Large-scale quasi-experimental sequential cohort study comparing three AI-mediated feedback workflows across 13,037 students and 51,296 student-authored resources: 1) Directed Feedback (students receive AI feedback without structured support), 2) Self-Directed Feedback (optional AI-supported dialogue), and 3) Enacted Feedback (students select feedback, evaluate relevance, and engage in targeted AI dialogue).

Result: Enacted Feedback had significantly higher AI feedback uptake (26.2% probability) vs Directed (14.1%) and Self-Directed (0.1%). It also led to higher self-assessment confidence and submitted-work quality compared to both control conditions.

Conclusion: Educational value of AI-generated feedback depends not just on comment quality but on workflows that structure students' enactment of feedback literacy processes. AI access alone is insufficient; purposeful workflow design that positions learners as active participants in judgement, dialogue, and improvement is central to productive feedback use.

Abstract: Feedback processes strongly influence student learning, yet their educational value depends on addressing two distinct challenges: providing high-quality, timely, and individualised feedback at scale, and supporting students to interpret, evaluate, and act on that feedback productively. Generative AI offers a credible means of addressing the provision challenge, but students' uptake of AI-generated feedback remains limited. We conducted a large-scale quasi-experimental sequential cohort study comparing three AI-mediated feedback workflows across 13,037 students and 51,296 student-authored resources. In Directed Feedback (n = 3,723), students received AI-generated feedback comments without structured support. In Self-Directed Feedback (n = 3,951), students could initiate optional AI-supported dialogue. In Enacted Feedback (n = 5,363), students were prompted to select feedback suggestions, evaluate their relevance, and engage in targeted AI-supported dialogue anchored to those selections. Enacted Feedback was associated with significantly higher uptake of AI-generated feedback, with an estimated probability of 26.2%, compared with 14.1% for Directed Feedback and 0.1% for Self-Directed Feedback. It was also associated with significantly higher self-assessment confidence and submitted-work quality than both comparison conditions. These findings suggest that the educational value of AI-generated feedback depends not only on the quality of feedback comments, but also on workflows that actively structure students' enactment of feedback literacy processes. The results have implications for the design of AI feedback systems that position learners as active participants in judgement, dialogue, and improvement rather than passive recipients of comments. Overall findings show that AI access alone is insufficient; purposeful workflow design is central to productive feedback use.

</details>


### [54] [CLAIM: Leading Open-domain Active Clarification of Large Language Models with Uncertainty Measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11631)
*Kuangzhao Yang,Ziliang Zhao,Zhicheng Dou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CLAIM is an uncertainty-driven framework for active clarification learning that uses model disagreement entropy to identify when and what to clarify in ambiguous LLM queries, eliminating the need for costly human annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In open-domain human-computer interactions, LLMs often encounter ambiguous or incomplete user queries. Directly answering such queries leads to overgeneralized or erroneous responses, while asking clarifying questions improves interaction quality. Existing methods rely on expensive manual annotations or preference alignment to determine when clarification is needed and what aspects to clarify, limiting generalization and incurring high costs.

Method: CLAIM quantifies query uncertainty through entropy from answer disagreements across multiple models, using this uncertainty signal to construct synthetic data. It employs an entropy-driven synthetic data generation pipeline combining entropy-based uncertainty estimation, semantic clustering, and reasoning-based judgments. The framework trains a unified clarification decision model using supervised fine-tuning (SFT) combined with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO), formulating clarification as a structured decision generation problem.

Result: Experimental results show that CLAIM learns stable and generalizable clarification strategies without relying on manually labeled data. The framework demonstrates effectiveness in proactive understanding for real-world open-domain LLM interactions, offering a low-cost robust solution.

Conclusion: CLAIM provides a practical, annotation-free approach to improve LLM interaction quality by intelligently determining when and how to clarify ambiguous queries, reducing dependency on costly human annotations while maintaining effectiveness in open-domain settings.

Abstract: In open-domain human-computer interaction scenarios, large language models (LLMs) frequently encounter user queries that are ambiguous or incomplete. In such cases, directly producing an answer often leads to overgeneralized, erroneous, or low-information responses. In contrast, asking clarifying questions can substantially improve interaction quality. However, existing approaches still rely heavily on manually annotated data or preference alignment to address two fundamental challenges: when clarification is necessary, and which aspect of the query should be clarified. This reliance incurs high annotation costs and limits generalization. To address these challenges, we propose CLAIM, an uncertainty-driven framework for active clarification learning in open-domain settings. CLAIM eliminates the need for explicit human preference annotations by quantifying query uncertainty through the entropy induced by answer disagreements across multiple models. This uncertainty signal is then used to construct high-quality synthetic data, enabling the training of a unified clarification decision model through a combination of supervised learning and reinforcement learning. Specifically, we propose an entropy-driven synthetic data generation pipeline that integrates entropy-based uncertainty estimation with semantic clustering and reasoning-based judgments, enabling reliable automatic annotation of clarification requirements. To train CLAIM, we formulate the clarification process as a structured decision generation problem and adopt a training paradigm that combines supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experimental results demonstrate that CLAIM can learn stable and generalizable clarification strategies without relying on manually labeled data, offering a low-cost and robust solution for proactive understanding in real-world open-domain interactions with LLMs.

</details>


### [55] [XBridge: Entity-Grounded Latent Bridge for Heterogeneous LLM Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11676)
*Wooseong Yang,Wei-Chieh Huang,Weizhi Zhang,Yu Wang,Philip S. Yu,Junhyun Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: XBRIDGE enables efficient cross-architecture communication between different LLM agents by combining lexical anchors with latent enrichment, solving entity grounding problems and outperforming text-based communication with lower latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous multi-agent LLM systems outperform homogeneous configurations but face communication challenges: text-based protocols discard internal representations, while latent-level transfer requires architectural homogeneity. The entity grounding problem emerges when transferring continuous representations across different LLM families, leading to rare-token compression collapse where entity identity is lost.

Method: XBRIDGE introduces a decode-free communication protocol with two key mechanisms: 1) Lexical Anchor Mapping (LAM) maps sender's original context tokens to receiver's vocabulary to provide discrete entity anchors, 2) Latent Enrichment Bridge (LEB) allows receiver to query sender's hidden states for contextual enrichment. The entity anchors ground contextual signals to specific entities through the receiver's self-attention.

Result: Across three model families (Llama, Qwen, Mistral), seven benchmarks, and both communication directions, XBRIDGE outperforms text-based communication on all seven tasks for each model pair while achieving 11x lower latency. In same-architecture settings, it exceeds KV-sharing baseline on six of seven tasks. The approach requires only 264M trainable parameters (3.8% of receiver), trained on small balanced samples, and adds negligible inference overhead.

Conclusion: XBRIDGE successfully addresses the entity grounding problem in cross-architecture communication between heterogeneous LLM agents, enabling efficient latent-level transfer without requiring architectural homogeneity. The method provides substantial performance improvements over text-based communication while maintaining low latency and minimal parameter overhead.

Abstract: Heterogeneous multi-agent LLM systems, where agents are powered by different model families, can outperform homogeneous configurations by reducing redundant reasoning patterns. Yet existing communication protocols either operate through text, discarding the sender's internal representations, or require architectural homogeneity for latent-level transfer. We identify the entity grounding problem in cross-architecture communication: cross-attention bridges that transfer continuous representations across different LLM families suffer from rare-token compression collapse, where entity identity is lost in the continuous bottleneck (bridge-only F1 ~30%). We propose XBRIDGE, a decode-free communication protocol that addresses this through two mechanisms. Lexical Anchor Mapping (LAM) maps the sender's original context tokens to the receiver's vocabulary, providing discrete entity anchors. A Latent Enrichment Bridge (LEB) lets the receiver query the sender's hidden states for contextual enrichment. The entity anchors ground the bridge's contextual signals to specific entities through the receiver's own self-attention. Across three model families (Llama, Qwen, and Mistral), seven benchmarks, and both communication directions, XBRIDGE outperforms text-based communication on all seven tasks for each model pair while achieving 11x lower latency, and in a same-architecture setting it also exceeds a KV-sharing baseline on six of seven tasks. LEB requires only 264M trainable parameters (3.8% of the receiver), is trained on a small balanced sample set, and adds negligible inference overhead.

</details>


### [56] [FrontierFinance: A Challenging Benchmark for Measuring Frontier Intelligence of Finance Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11683)
*Yuhao Zhang,O. Ozan Koyluoglu,Thejas Venkatesh,Richard Diehl Martinez,Vishank Bhatia,Arash Alidoust,Ashwin Paranjape*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FrontierFinance is a comprehensive open benchmark for AI investment agents with 220 expert queries and 11,543 source-attributed rubrics covering six investor workflow use cases, revealing that tool harness design and open-weight models can compete with proprietary systems at significantly lower costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for AI investment agents are too narrow, focusing mainly on financial data extraction which current models have saturated. Reference-based metrics and generic LLM judging fail to properly evaluate the open-ended, long-form answers required for real analyst work. There's a need for a benchmark that captures the full complexity of the investor workflow.

Method: Created FrontierFinance benchmark with 220 expert-crafted queries and 11,543 source-attributed rubrics spanning six crucial use cases across the complete investor workflow. Evaluated frontier models and agent systems under a common harness restricted to publicly available data to measure quality and efficiency.

Result: Samaya's in-house system leads with 56.0% performance, ahead of strongest frontier model (Claude Fable 5 at 49.2%) at ~2.2x lower cost. Best open-weight model (Kimi K3 at 46.4%) nearly matches best proprietary model at 4.5x lower cost. Screening & Discovery and Sector, Industry & Macro remain hardest use cases with best systems reaching only 33% and 39% respectively.

Conclusion: The tool harness design strongly influences AI investment agent performance more than the model alone. Open-weight models can compete with proprietary systems at significantly lower costs, making high-quality investment research more accessible. The benchmark reveals persistent challenges in certain complex investment use cases that require further research.

Abstract: AI agents are increasingly deployed for professional investment research, yet no benchmark captures the complexity of the full investor workflow. Existing benchmarks mainly target financial data extraction, a narrow slice that current models have largely saturated, while reference-based metrics and generic LLM-as-a-judge scoring fall short on the open-ended, long-form answers that real analyst queries demand. We introduce FrontierFinance, a fully open benchmark of 220 expert-crafted queries and 11,543 source-attributed rubrics spanning six crucial use cases across the full investor workflow. FrontierFinance is both broader and harder than existing public finance benchmarks. Evaluating frontier models and agent systems under a common harness restricted to publicly available data, we find that the tool harness, not the model alone, strongly shapes quality and efficiency; that Samaya's in-house system leads at 56.0%, ahead of the strongest frontier model (Claude Fable 5, 49.2%) at roughly 2.2x lower cost; and that the best open-weight model (Kimi K3, 46.4%) nearly matches the best proprietary model at 4.5x lower cost. Screening & Discovery and Sector, Industry & Macro remain the hardest use cases across all systems, where even the best systems reach only 33% and 39%. We make the dataset and grading code publicly available.

</details>


### [57] [HUGIN: Enhancing Vision-Language Planning for Autonomous Logistics Sorting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11692)
*Xikai Sun,Cangtian Zhou,Kebin Liu,Ke Ma,Xu Wang,Zaishu Chen,Haotian Wang,Li Liu,Yunhao Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HUGIN framework improves vision-language models for joint multi-scene understanding in autonomous logistics sorting by using endogenous data augmentation and global context ranking, achieving significant accuracy improvements on a new industrial benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous logistics sorting systems require joint planning across disjoint camera views, but existing vision-language models struggle with scarce cross-scene supervision and attention dispersion caused by long visual contexts in joint multi-scene understanding tasks.

Method: Proposes HUGIN framework with two components: 1) Endogenous Data Augmentation that recombines verified atomic facts under operating constraints, and 2) Global Context Ranking that aligns instruction representations more strongly with complete visual context than partial context. Also constructs SortingBench dataset from four layouts of autonomous logistics sorting systems.

Result: HUGIN consistently outperforms baselines across five open VLMs; Qwen3-VL-8B accuracy improved from 63.6% to 78.8% on SortingBench. Deployment tests with over 15,000 packages confirmed practical viability. Additional experiments verified each component's effectiveness and spillover benefits in embodied tasks.

Conclusion: HUGIN framework successfully addresses challenges in joint multi-scene understanding for autonomous logistics sorting, enabling vision-language models to effectively handle complex multi-scene planning tasks with practical industrial applications.

Abstract: Autonomous logistics sorting systems (ALSS) are an important industrial application of embodied AI, which requires joint planning over spatially disjoint camera views. We formulate this setting as Joint Multi-Scene Understanding (JMSU). With open-world visual understanding and task-planning capabilities, vision-language models (VLMs) are promising candidates for JMSU. However, directly applying existing VLMs to JMSU is non-trivial due to scarce cross-scene supervision and attention dispersion caused by long visual context in JMSU. To address these challenges, we propose HUGIN, a training framework with two complementary components. Endogenous Data Augmentation recombines verified atomic facts under operating constraints, while Global Context Ranking aligns the instruction representation more strongly with the complete visual context than with a partial visual context. To support ongoing research, we construct a high-quality industrial sorting dataset and benchmark named SortingBench from four layouts of autonomous logistics sorting systems. Across five open VLMs, HUGIN consistently outperforms matched baselines; for example, the accuracy on SortingBench of Qwen3-VL-8B increases from 63.6% to 78.8%. Additional experiments verify the effectiveness of each component and JMSU's spillover benefits in embodied tasks. Deployment tests involving more than 15,000 packages support the practical viability of VLM-based planning for autonomous logistics sorting.

</details>


### [58] [Making Your LLMs More Objective: Stabilizing LLM Safety Behavior Across Traits with Trait-Invariant Safety Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11705)
*Lang Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show inconsistent safety behavior based on system prompt traits - same request may be refused or accepted depending on assigned traits. Proposed methods measure and fix this vulnerability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aligned LLMs should provide consistent safety decisions regardless of system prompt traits, but current models show trait-induced safety variation where the same request gets different safety responses based on assigned traits.

Method: 1) Introduce refusal-based metrics (Trait-Induced Deviation and Trait-Induced Flip Rate) to measure safety variation. 2) Analyze representation-level mechanisms showing traits perturb safety representations in low-dimensional subspace. 3) Propose Trait-Invariant Safety Tuning (TIST) self-distillation framework. 4) Develop Trait-Subspace Neutralization (TraSN) instantiation enforcing invariance only in trait subspace.

Result: TraSN improves trait-invariant safety while strengthening harmful-request safety and preserving general capabilities. Successfully addresses trait-induced safety variations through subspace-specific interventions.

Conclusion: Traits are a critical factor in LLM safety that can cause inconsistent model behavior. The proposed methods successfully achieve trait-invariant safety while maintaining model capabilities, highlighting the need to consider traits in safety alignment.

Abstract: Aligned large language models (LLMs) are expected to exhibit safety behavior based on the content of the user request: they should refuse unsafe requests and comply with safe ones. However, we show that the same request can elicit substantially different safety decisions under different traits assigned in the system prompt, a failure mode we call trait-induced safety variation. To measure this failure, we introduce refusal-based metrics: Trait-Induced Deviation measures dataset-level deviation from the no-trait baseline, while Trait-Induced Flip Rate measures whether the same request receives different safety decisions across traits. We then provide a representation-level analysis of the mechanism behind trait-induced safety shifts and find that traits perturb the model's safety representations within a low-dimensional subspace. To achieve trait-invariant safety, where safety behavior remains stable across traits, we introduce Trait-Invariant Safety Tuning (TIST), a simple yet effective self-distillation framework that aligns an LLM's trait-conditioned behavior with its no-trait behavior. Guided by our analysis, we further propose Trait-Subspace Neutralization (TraSN), an instantiation of TIST, which enforces invariance only within the identified trait subspace. Experiments show that TraSN improves trait-invariant safety and strengthens harmful-request safety while preserving general capability. Our results highlight traits as an important factor in LLM safety and robust model behavior.

</details>


### [59] [Proportional Analogies on Probability Distributions via Bayesian Updating](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11724)
*Pierre-Alexandre Murena*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new framework for proportional analogies extended to probability distributions using Bayesian updating, with applications to exponential family distributions and Gaussian mixture approximations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While proportional analogies have been well-studied for Boolean, symbolic, and real-valued domains, their extension to probability distributions remains largely unexplored, creating a gap in the formal understanding of analogical reasoning in probabilistic contexts.

Method: The approach builds on the idea that two distributions are related when one can be transformed into the other through Bayesian updating induced by suitable observations. The framework is investigated for standard members of the exponential family and extended to arbitrary probability distributions using Gaussian mixture approximations.

Result: The paper develops a novel notion of proportional analogy for probability distributions based on Bayesian updating, providing a mathematical framework for analogical reasoning in probabilistic domains across various distribution families.

Conclusion: This work successfully extends the proportional analogy framework from traditional domains to probability distributions, establishing a foundation for analogical reasoning in probabilistic settings through Bayesian updating principles.

Abstract: Analogies are quaternary relations of the form "A is to B as C is to D". Among the various formalizations of analogical reasoning, proportional analogies provide an important axiomatic framework by characterizing valid analogies through a set of postulates. While proportional analogies have been extensively studied over Boolean, symbolic, and real-valued domains, their extension to probability distributions remains largely unexplored. In this paper, we introduce a notion of proportional analogy for probability distributions based on Bayesian updating. Our approach builds upon the idea that two distributions are related whenever one can be transformed into the other through Bayesian updating induced by a suitable set of observations. We investigate this framework for several standard members of the exponential family and discuss how it naturally extends to arbitrary probability distributions through Gaussian mixture approximations.

</details>


### [60] [Harness-IF: Evaluating Instruction Following Across Instruction Surfaces in Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11727)
*Zining Huang,Haoran Que,Hong Zeng,Ge Zhang,Zuo Wang,Jin Chen,Haodong Wang,Zhongfei Hou,Changxin Pu,Shen Yan,Wenhao Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Harness-IF is a new benchmark for evaluating coding agents' rule compliance using execution evidence and Against-Prior Accuracy to separate genuine compliance from coincidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing instruction-following benchmarks cannot distinguish between genuine rule compliance and agents simply doing what they would have done anyway, especially for coding agents where current benchmarks focus only on final task success.

Method: Introduces Harness-IF with 60 realistic multi-turn coding items from a 642-rule library, evaluating 256 rules across five configurable surfaces. Uses Against-Prior Accuracy (AP-Acc) to score only rules that oppose unprompted defaults by re-running tasks with rules withheld across nine probe builds.

Result: Across 12 frontier models: accuracy 72.1-85.9%, AP-Acc 66.1-78.6%. Every model performs worse on against-prior rules by 3.6-7.4 points (mean 5.81). Top build unchanged after prior control, but three adjacent rank pairs exchange. Pooled precedence analysis shows system prompts, project files, and user instructions take precedence over tool and skill descriptions.

Conclusion: Aggregate scores overstate compliance by model-specific margins; genuine rule compliance measurement requires accounting for agents' prior defaults, and prompt precedence hierarchy reveals that system/project/user instructions override tool/skill descriptions.

Abstract: When a coding agent obeys a rule, it may simply have been going to do that anyway. Existing instruction-following benchmarks cannot tell the difference: they concentrate rules in the user turn, while coding-agent benchmarks emphasize final task success. We introduce Harness-IF, which scores operational rules one at a time from execution evidence: 60 realistic multi-turn coding items drawn from a 642-rule library, 256 rules receiving verdicts, placed on the five configurable surfaces a deployed agent reads. To separate compliance from coincidence we introduce Against-Prior Accuracy (AP-Acc), which scores only rules labeled as opposing unprompted defaults, observed by re-running tasks with the rule withheld across nine probe builds and curated otherwise. Across 12 frontier models, accuracy spans 72.1-85.9% and AP-Acc 66.1-78.6%; every model is worse on against-prior rules, by 3.6 to 7.4 points (mean 5.81), and the direction survives a common-support analysis with item-clustered intervals. Aggregate scores therefore overstate compliance by a model-specific margin: prior control leaves the top build unchanged and exchanges three adjacent rank pairs. A counterbalanced conflict pilot on nine separate builds adds a second result: pooled precedence does not follow prompt depth, with system prompts, project files, and user instructions ahead of tool and skill descriptions.

</details>


### [61] [HyperANFIS: Enhancing Rule Representation and Interpretability in Adaptive Neuro-Fuzzy Systems via Hyperbolic Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11768)
*Haoran Pei,Zhao Su,Zetao Lin,Haoran Li,Jun Shen,Qi Zhu,Lan Guo,Qingguo Zhou,Binbin Yong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hyperbolic ANFIS (HyperANFIS) extends the adaptive neuro-fuzzy system to hyperbolic space, improving predictive performance while maintaining interpretable fuzzy rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ANFIS models operate in Euclidean space, which limits their representational capacity and predictive performance, despite their strength in generating interpretable IF-THEN rules.

Method: Hyperbolic ANFIS preserves the fuzzy semantics and core architecture of ANFIS but performs rule-prototype learning, rule activation, and consequent aggregation in hyperbolic space instead of Euclidean space.

Result: HyperANFIS consistently outperforms standard ANFIS and various ANFIS variants across all datasets, while generating higher-quality fuzzy rules with improved predictive accuracy, inter-rule collaboration, and rule credibility.

Conclusion: Extending ANFIS to hyperbolic geometry strengthens fuzzy inference without sacrificing interpretability, offering a superior approach that leverages hyperbolic space's representational properties for better performance and rule quality.

Abstract: The adaptive neuro-fuzzy inference system (ANFIS) is an interpretable reasoning framework capable of generating explicit IF-THEN fuzzy rules, making it suitable for tasks requiring transparent reasoning. However, existing ANFIS models generally construct rule antecedents and perform inference in Euclidean space, limiting their representational capacity and predictive performance. To address this issue, we propose Hyperbolic ANFIS (HyperANFIS), a hyperbolic extension of ANFIS. HyperANFIS preserves the fuzzy semantics and core architecture of conventional ANFIS while performing rule-prototype learning, rule activation, and consequent aggregation in hyperbolic space. It also retains the ability to generate interpretable IF-THEN rules. By exploiting the representational properties of hyperbolic geometry, HyperANFIS strengthens the fuzzy inference process, thereby improving predictive accuracy, inter-rule collaboration, and the credibility of its interpretable rules. Experimental results show that HyperANFIS consistently outperforms the standard ANFIS baseline and various ANFIS variants across all datasets, while also generating higher-quality fuzzy rules.

</details>


### [62] [The Sleeping Agent: What Gist-Based Context Compression Loses and Why](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11775)
*Nicholas E. Kyrkewood*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Gist compression improves multi-hop reasoning but hurts temporal questions due to lost date/time information; a targeted prompt modification fixes temporal issues without affecting other memory types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Gist-based context compression is widely used in long-horizon language model agents, but its effects on different types of memory retrieval (multi-hop reasoning, factual, temporal) are poorly understood. The researchers aim to identify when compression helps versus hurts performance.

Method: Used Salience-Weighted Consolidation (SWC), a biologically-inspired compression framework, as a diagnostic probe. SWC scores conversation history by salience, partitions into priority tiers, and applies structured gist abstraction to mid-priority content. Evaluated four conditions on all ten LoCoMo conversations (1,935 matched text-only questions, with 1,501 used in primary analysis after excluding adversarial questions) at temperature 0.

Result: Found a consistent task-type interaction: gist compression substantially outperforms truncation on multi-hop reasoning and single-hop factual questions, but temporal questions remain substantially harder under compression. Identified the failure mechanism: gist abstraction preserves relational and event structure while discarding dates and times. A one-sentence prompt modification increased temporal expression preservation from 3.05% to 62.39% (20-fold improvement) while barely changing named entity (x1.02) and event preservation (x1.11). This fix recovered +0.314 judge accuracy on temporal questions.

Conclusion: Gist compression has nuanced effects depending on question type, excelling at reasoning tasks but failing at temporal questions due to systematic omission of date/time information. The issue can be precisely fixed with targeted prompt modifications, demonstrating that gist compression's failures are addressable and providing insights for optimizing long-context language agents.

Abstract: Gist-based context compression---summarising older conversation history into compact representations---is a common approach in long-horizon language model agents, yet its effect on different types of memory retrieval is poorly understood. We use Salience-Weighted Consolidation (SWC), a biologically-inspired compression framework motivated by sleep-based memory consolidation, as a diagnostic probe to study when gist compression helps and when it hurts. SWC scores conversation history by salience, partitions it into priority tiers, and applies structured gist abstraction to mid-priority content. Evaluating four conditions on all ten LoCoMo conversations---1,935 matched text-only questions in total, 1,501 used in the primary aggregate after excluding Category 5 (adversarial) questions---at temperature 0, we find a consistent task-type interaction: gist compression substantially outperforms truncation on multi-hop reasoning and single-hop factual questions, but temporal questions remain substantially harder under compression, with compressed conditions scoring well below the full-context reference on the conversations where both are evaluated. We trace this failure to a specific mechanism: the gist abstraction prompt preserves relational and event structure while discarding dates and times. A preservation analysis across all ten conversations confirms the mechanism: an approximately 20-fold increase in temporal expression preservation (3.05% to 62.39%) with a one-sentence prompt modification, while named entity and event preservation rates barely change (x1.02 and x1.11), demonstrating that the fix is a precision instrument. The prompt modification recovers +0.314 [0.254, 0.375] judge accuracy on category-2 (temporal) questions in the matched set. Code and results: https://github.com/kyrkewood/sleeping-agent.

</details>


### [63] [Agent Skills Can Be Harmful: An Empirical Study of Skill-Induced Failures in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11888)
*Gen Dong,Yanjie Gao,Liqun Li,Tianyin Xu,Yu Hua,Fan Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how agent skills can cause failures and efficiency regressions in LLM agents, introducing a differential analysis framework to attribute failures to specific skills and identifying that seemingly relevant skills often cause implementation errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent skills are widely used to extend LLM agents, but prior work shows mixed results: some skills improve success rates while others cause failures, increase costs, or reduce performance. There's a need to systematically understand why skills sometimes harm agent performance.

Method: Introduces a differential analysis framework that compares skill-guided runs against no-skill or semantically matched skill reference runs to attribute failures to specific skills. Instantiates the framework on SkillsBench and SWE-Skills-Bench datasets and builds SkillTriage, a taxonomy-guided attribution tool that normalizes cases, extracts differential evidence, and generates triage reports.

Result: Identified 307 skill-induced failures: 125 functional failures and 182 efficiency regressions. Key findings: 1) Functional failures are rarely from obviously irrelevant skills but from seemingly relevant ones causing incorrect implementation or omissions; 2) Efficiency regressions aren't explained by prompt length alone; 3) Excessive verification and heavy implementation pipelines are major sources of efficiency issues.

Conclusion: Skills often turn validation checklists and construction recipes into mandatory work that harms efficiency. The analysis provides insights for safer, more cost-aware skill reuse and suggests research directions for improving skill design and tooling.

Abstract: Agent skills are the de facto mechanism for extending LLM agents with reusable guidance. A skill can shape the agent's task execution, including planning, tool use, problem-solving, and validation. Prior work reported mixed results of agent skills: some skills improve task success rates, while others have no effect, increase token use and execution time, and even reduce success rates. This paper presents a comprehensive analysis of skill-induced agent failures by attributing task failures and cost regressions to specific loaded skills. We introduce a differential analysis framework that attributes a failure or regression to a skill by comparing a target skill-guided run against a no-skill or semantically matched skill reference run that solves the same task, or solves it more cheaply. We instantiate this framework on SkillsBench and SWE-Skills-Bench, yielding 307 skill-induced failures, including 125 functional failures and 182 efficiency regressions. We also build SkillTriage, a taxonomy-guided attribution tool that normalizes paired cases, extracts differential evidence, and produces triage reports. Our major findings include: (1) Skill induced functional failures are rarely caused by obviously irrelevant skills; instead, seemingly relevant skills often make the agent incorrectly implement or omit task-required implementation elements. (2) Skill-induced efficiency regressions are not explained by prompt length alone. (3) The largest sources within Excessive Procedure are excessive verification and heavy implementation pipelines, contributing 67 and 30 cases, respectively. This shows that skills often turn validation checklists and construction recipes into mandatory work. Based on our findings, we propose research topics and tooling improvements for safer and more cost-aware skill reuse.

</details>


### [64] [Policy-as-logic for robust reasoning over rules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11905)
*Rahul Nair,Bastian Lipka,Elizabeth Daly*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hybrid symbolic approach combining LLMs for fact extraction and answer set solvers for formal reasoning outperforms policy-as-prompt and policy-as-code methods with 10x token reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many generative AI applications require responses to respect written policies/rules (tax rules, airline baggage allowances). Current approaches need better interpretability, auditability, accuracy, and robustness under input perturbations.

Method: Policies expressed in formal logic; at inference time uses language models for fact extraction to ground predicates, then answer set solvers for reasoning. Separates extraction and reasoning steps.

Result: Outperforms policy-as-prompt and policy-as-code methods in most cases with ~10x reduction in token usage. Provides interpretable, auditable, and robust responses.

Conclusion: Structured reasoning and symbolic solvers combined with generative models enable robust decisions involving objective criteria, highlighting value of hybrid symbolic-neural approaches.

Abstract: In many practical applications of generative AI systems, from tax rules to airline baggage allowance, responses to natural language queries must respect written policies or rules. We present a hybrid symbolic approach that expresses policies in formal logic and at inference time exploits the representation power of language models for fact extraction to ground predicates, and an answer set solver for reasoning such that responses are interpretable, auditable, and as we show, accurate and robust under input perturbations. Specifically, we show this separation of extraction and reasoning steps outperforms policy-as-prompt and policy-as-code methods in most cases with ~10x reduction in token usage. The results point to the value of structured reasoning and symbolic solvers in conjunction with generative models to make robust decisions involving objective criteria.

</details>


### [65] [OEIS Open: How many conjectures can language models turn into theorems?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11941)
*Tom Adamczewski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OEIS Open benchmark with 492 mathematical conjectures from OEIS, formalized in Lean, shows LMs can autonomously resolve open research conjectures at modest cost, achieving 30% success rate with $50 budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To create an open-source benchmark for evaluating language models' ability to solve open mathematical conjectures, moving beyond proprietary/bespoke agent systems and enabling secure, reproducible evaluation of any LM.

Method: Constructed OEIS Open benchmark using 492 open mathematical conjectures from OEIS formalized in Lean; created open-source evaluation code that runs any LM securely against these conjectures; used minimal tool-equipped LMs with budget constraints ($50-$200 per attempt); tested with and without access to arXiv mathematical literature.

Result: LMs with minimal tools resolved 147 conjectures (30%) with $50 budget; best current LM scores 44% on OEIS Open Lite subset with $200 budget; access to arXiv literature (476,000 papers) did not improve performance; more sophisticated agent loops also showed no benefit.

Conclusion: While the mathematical significance of the solved conjectures is uncertain, results demonstrate that LMs can autonomously resolve open research conjectures at modest cost, establishing OEIS Open as a practical benchmark for evaluating LM mathematical reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: We construct OEIS Open, a benchmark based on 492 open mathematical conjectures from the OEIS, formalized in Lean by Tsoukalas et al. Whereas these conjectures had previously been attempted only with a bespoke agent, our open-source evaluation code runs any generic language model (LM) against them, and is secure against LM cheating attempts. We find that LMs equipped with a minimal set of tools resolve 147 of these conjectures with a budget of \$50 per attempt, scoring 30% on OEIS Open. OEIS Open Lite is a random subset of 100 conjectures for cheaper evaluation. When evaluated with a budget of \$200 per attempt, the best current LM scores 44% on OEIS Open Lite. Giving LMs access to the mathematics literature via 476,000 papers from arXiv did not increase performance on OEIS Open Lite, and nor did using more sophisticated agent loops. The conjectures covered in this work are of uncertain mathematical significance, and most have likely received little previous attention. Nevertheless, our results show that LMs can resolve open research conjectures autonomously and at modest cost.

</details>


### [66] [ExRole: From Team Trajectories to Executable Roles in Multi-Agent Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11949)
*Zhou Liu,Chaoyang Han,Zewei Pan,Zeli Su,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ExRole is a trajectory-to-role framework that learns executable role prototypes from team traces to improve multi-agent reasoning, achieving significant gains over single-agent and other multi-agent approaches on complex QA benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multi-agent systems treat roles as hand-written prompt labels disconnected from learned behavior and parameter updates. The authors argue that useful roles should be executable control variables that summarize behavior predictive of future utility, guide interaction, and identify trainable capacity responsible for that behavior.

Method: ExRole learns future-aware role prototypes from prefix-local team traces, resolves them into readable instructions and token-aligned role markers, and optionally routes shared LoRA rank slots with turn-aligned credit.

Result: On MuSiQue and 2WikiMultiHopQA, ExRole improves over single-agent search by 15.0/14.4 and 13.5/16.1 EM/F1 points respectively. Against strongest non-ExRole controls, gains remain 11.5/11.6 and 7.7/9.7 points. Trajectory-induced role conditioning consistently outperforms role-free, manual, random, and shuffled alternatives. Role-Agent-Turn interventions show induced roles capture transferable behavioral specialization.

Conclusion: ExRole demonstrates that learned, executable roles significantly enhance multi-agent reasoning capabilities compared to static role assignments, enabling behavioral specialization that transcends fixed agent identities or turn positions, making roles more than just labels but actual control variables for improved coordination.

Abstract: Roles provide an interpretable interface for organizing language-model agents, yet most multi-agent systems treat them as hand-written prompt labels disconnected from learned behavior and parameter updates. We argue that a useful role should instead be an executable control variable: it should summarize behavior predictive of future utility, guide subsequent interaction, and identify the trainable capacity responsible for that behavior. We introduce ExRole, a trajectory-to-role framework that learns future-aware role prototypes from prefix-local team traces, resolves them into readable instructions and token-aligned role markers, and optionally routes shared LoRA rank slots with turn-aligned credit. Across MuSiQue and 2WikiMultiHopQA, ExRole improves over single-agent search by 15.0/14.4 and 13.5/16.1 EM/F1 points, respectively. Against the strongest non-ExRole controls, the corresponding gains remain 11.5/11.6 and 7.7/9.7 points. Across both benchmarks, the controlled results consistently favor trajectory-induced role conditioning over role-free, manual, random, and shuffled alternatives. Role-Agent-Turn interventions further show that the induced roles capture transferable behavioral specialization beyond fixed agent identities or turn positions.

</details>


### [67] [Retry, Switch, or Abstain? Learning Strategy-Aware Tool-Use Policies via Controlled Error Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11977)
*Chaoran Chen,Vy Nguyen,Ziji Zhang,Abhinav Gullapalli,Ziyi Wang,Yuxuan Lu,Dakuo Wang,Jing Huang,Zhou Yu,Jin Lai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BENCH2ROBUST framework converts failure-free tool-use benchmarks into stochastic environments with controlled solvability, enabling study of recovery behaviors. Combining Bayesian Tool Memory (context intervention) with curriculum RL improves robustness up to 45.5% while preserving failure-free performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM tool-use agents are trained in environments where tools always succeed, but real-world deployments face transient, persistent, or silent tool failures. Robust recovery requires more than simple retries - agents need to know when to retry, switch to alternatives, or stop when no viable paths remain.

Method: Developed BENCH2ROBUST framework to convert standard tool-use benchmarks into stochastic environments with controlled solvability scenarios. Studied two complementary interventions: 1) Bayesian Tool Memory (BTM) providing structured runtime recovery context, and 2) curriculum-controlled reinforcement learning. Evaluated across 7 models from 4 families using two multi-turn benchmark families.

Result: Tool failures create a near-universal robustness gap across all tested models. On Retail tasks, BTM improves robustness by up to 16.8 percentage points without retraining. RL learns complementary recovery behavior that remains effective without BTM. Combining both approaches reaches 40.8-45.5% robustness under failure injection while maintaining failure-free performance.

Conclusion: Robust tool use benefits from combining environment-specific recovery knowledge (BTM) with learned recovery behavior (RL). The proposed interventions demonstrate complementary strengths, suggesting that both memory-based context and learned policies are necessary for effective failure recovery in real-world LLM tool-use deployments.

Abstract: Tool-using LLM agents are commonly trained and evaluated in environments where tool calls succeed reliably, yet deployed tools can fail transiently, persistently, or silently. Robust recovery therefore requires more than repeated retries: an agent may need to retry the same path, switch to an alternative, or recognize that no viable path remains. We present BENCH2ROBUST, a framework that converts failure-free tool-use benchmarks into controlled stochastic environments with scenario-controlled solvability, where episodes explicitly require retrying, switching, or stopping after available paths are exhausted. We use BENCH2ROBUST to study two complementary interventions: structured runtime recovery context through Bayesian Tool Memory (BTM), and curriculum-controlled reinforcement learning. Across 7 models from 4 families and two multi-turn benchmark families, tool failures produce a near-universal robustness gap. On held-out Retail tasks, BTM improves robustness by up to 16.8 percentage points without retraining, while RL learns complementary recovery behavior that remains beneficial without inference-time BTM. Combining the two reaches 40.8-45.5% under injection while preserving failure-free performance. These results suggest that robust tool use benefits from combining environment-specific recovery knowledge with learned recovery behavior.

</details>


### [68] [Claim-Level Reliability Assessment for Efficient Test-Time Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11994)
*Sen Xu,Wei Wang,Shixi Liu,Jixin Min,Yingwei Dai,Zhibin Yin,Yirong Chen,Junlin Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CLR is a training-free framework that improves reasoning accuracy by focusing computational resources on verifying critical claims rather than generating multiple solutions, exploiting the asymmetry between constructing correct solutions and refuting incorrect claims.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current test-time scaling approaches like self-consistency rely on generating multiple solutions and majority voting, but this wastes compute on routine tokens and suffers from signal dilution where decisive errors in reasoning traces are obscured. Whole-trace evaluation is inefficient because it treats all tokens equally rather than focusing on critical decision points.

Method: Claim-Level Reliability Assessment (CLR): 1) Condenses each reasoning trace into a compact set of decision-critical claims (logical anchors), 2) Focuses computational resources on targeted verification of these claims rather than generating additional solutions, 3) Employs semantic falsification - searching for negative evidence to refute incorrect claims rather than trying to construct perfect solutions, exploiting the fundamental asymmetry that refuting an incorrect claim only requires finding one decisive flaw.

Result: Across four LLMs and four reasoning benchmarks with matched compute budgets, CLR consistently outperforms both pass@1 and self-consistency. Notable results: On GPT-OSS-20B/CMIMC25, CLR exceeds pass@1 by 27.15 percentage points and raises self-consistency accuracy from 77.50% to 82.19% while using 37.0% fewer tokens.

Conclusion: Claim-level falsification is an effective principle for test-time scaling that reallocates compute from solution sampling to targeted verification. By isolating logical anchors and focusing on semantic falsification, CLR systematically compresses the survival space of incorrect high-confidence traces, effectively suppressing erroneous consensus through nonlinear reliability scoring, making reasoning more accurate and efficient.

Abstract: We propose claim-level falsification as a principle for test-time scaling and instantiate it through Claim-Level Reliability Assessment (CLR), a training-free framework that reallocates test-time compute from additional solution sampling to targeted verification. Since whole-trace evaluation often obscures decisive errors due to signal dilution from routine tokens, CLR condenses each reasoning trace into a compact set of decision-critical claims, thereby isolating its logical anchors. Furthermore, recognizing the inherent difficulty of generating entirely correct solutions under fixed model capabilities, CLR shifts the focus to semantic falsification. This approach exploits a fundamental asymmetry between solution construction and claim refutation. Constructing a valid solution requires a flawless reasoning path, whereas refuting an incorrect claim requires identifying only a single decisive flaw. This targeted search for negative evidence systematically compresses the survival space of high-confidence incorrect traces, effectively suppressing erroneous consensus via nonlinear reliability scoring. Across four LLMs and four reasoning benchmarks under matched budgets, CLR generally improves upon pass@1 and self-consistency. On GPT-OSS-20B/CMIMC25, for instance, CLR exceeds pass@1 by 27.15 percentage-points and raises self-consistency accuracy from 77.50\% to 82.19\% with 37.0\% fewer tokens.

</details>


### [69] [CTBench: Evaluating Troubleshooting Capabilities of AI Agents in Realistic Telecom Network Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12002)
*Xingyu Yan,Tingting Dai,Antonio De Domenico,Mohamed Sana,Nicola Piovesan,Changchang Li,Bowen Liu,Kun Jiang,Mengjie Zhang,Dingcheng Shan,Jing-Cheng Pang,Chenwei Wu,Sijie Wu,Lianying Chao,Haoran Cai,Jiantao Ye,Xubin Li,Simon Mark Lucas,Xin Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CTBench is a public benchmark for evaluating telecom troubleshooting agents, focusing on root cause analysis and path restoration with expert-constructed tasks and evidence-based evaluation metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations for network operation agents fail to accurately model real telecom network characteristics and don't properly assess agents under partially observable environments with diverse vendors, devices, protocols, and interfaces. There's a need for better benchmarks that assess whether agents behave like competent telecom troubleshooting engineers.

Method: CTBench is introduced as a public benchmark with expert-constructed tasks annotated with rich metadata including golden evidence steps. It uses expert-grounded metrics that evaluate both final answers and diagnostic evidence. Experiments are conducted with representative harness-model combinations to assess agent performance.

Result: State-of-the-art agents perform well at identifying endpoints in path-restoration tasks but underperform in root cause analysis. Agents particularly struggle with interface state, link-layer, service-management, and operational faults. Even when producing plausible/correct final answers, agents often fail to provide evidence-grounded diagnoses. Path restoration is more resource expensive, but larger resource usage doesn't necessarily lead to better diagnosis.

Conclusion: There's a significant gap in agent capabilities for telecom troubleshooting, especially in evidence-based diagnosis and root cause analysis. The CTBench benchmark reveals critical weaknesses in current agents and provides a foundation for more realistic evaluation of telecom operation automation systems.

Abstract: Agents are increasingly considered for automating network operations and maintenance, where engineers must diagnose network faults, optimize configurations to enhance services, and reduce operational costs while acting under strict constraints. However, existing evaluations fail to accurately model real network characteristics or assess agents under partially observable telecom environments with diverse vendors, devices, protocols, and interfaces. In this paper, we introduce CTBench, a public benchmark for assessing whether an agent behaves like a competent telecom troubleshooting engineer. CTBench focuses on root cause analysis and path restoration. Each task is constructed by experts and annotated with rich task metadata, including golden evidence steps. CTBench uses expert-grounded metrics that evaluate both final answers and the diagnostic evidence. Experiments with representative harness-model combinations show that state-of-the-art agents perform very well at identifying endpoints in path-restoration tasks but, more generally, underperform in root cause analysis. In particular, agents struggle with interface state, link-layer, service-management, and other operational faults. Most importantly, even when agents produce plausible or correct final answers, they often fail to provide the evidence-grounded diagnoses required in operational practice. Our results further show that path restoration is generally more resource expensive, yet larger resource usage does not necessarily translate into better diagnosis.

</details>


### [70] [Graph-Structured Rubrics: Compiling Rubrics into Typed Evaluation Graphs for LLM Judges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12097)
*Xi Chen,Jie Mu,Mo Xuan,Qun Shao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Graph-Structured Rubrics (GSR) transform traditional rubrics into typed evaluation graphs that explicitly define criterion composition through transformation, reduction, and gating operators, improving evaluation accuracy and agreement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional rubric-based evaluators treat rubrics as simple prompt context or flat criteria, leaving implicit how different criteria should be composed together, even when natural-language rules specify this composition. This leads to suboptimal evaluation performance.

Method: GSR compiles rubrics into response-independent typed evaluation graphs before observing responses. The graph includes criterion nodes for judgments, transformation/reduction/gating operators for composition through named ports, and a task-specific Readout mapping to convert the unique sink into scores or preferences. The system supports both pointwise (separate dimension judgments before aggregation) and pairwise (reusing graphs for multiple candidates) evaluation modes.

Result: Using GPT-OSS-120B, GSR improves exact score agreement by 0.62-6.75 percentage points over Prometheus-style scoring on four pointwise datasets. It also achieves the numerically highest end-to-end pairwise accuracy on two preference benchmarks under native tie and abstention policies.

Conclusion: Graph-Structured Rubrics provide a more structured and explicit approach to rubric-based evaluation by compiling rubrics into typed evaluation graphs, which leads to improved evaluation accuracy and agreement across both pointwise scoring and pairwise preference tasks.

Abstract: Rubric-based evaluators commonly treat rubrics as prompt context or flat criteria: they specify what to judge but leave criterion composition implicit, even when natural-language rules state it. We introduce Graph-Structured Rubrics (GSR), which compiles a rubric into a response-independent typed evaluation graph before observing responses. Criterion nodes elicit judgments; transformation, reduction, and gating operators compose them through named ports; and a task-specific output mapping, termed Readout, converts the unique sink into a score or preference. Compilation rejects malformed or type-incompatible graphs. Pointwise evaluation judges rubric dimensions separately before graph aggregation; pairwise evaluation reuses the graph with one judgment for each candidate under every criterion. Under GPT-OSS-120B, GSR improves exact score agreement by 0.62--6.75 percentage points over Prometheus-style scoring on four pointwise datasets and achieves the numerically highest end-to-end pairwise accuracy on two preference benchmarks under native tie and abstention policies.

</details>


### [71] [GUIDE: Governed Unified Intelligence for Document-to-Artifact Generation in Enterprise Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12133)
*Shivali Dalmia,Sumukha Thoppanahalli,Mohammadreza Sediqin,Abhishek Mukherji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GUIDE is a governed multi-agent framework that automates enterprise guideline processing, reducing manual work from 2-3 days to 40-125 minutes with 96% success rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise guideline documents are multimodal (text, tables, images) and complex. Existing LLM/VLM systems suffer from hallucinations, table structure degradation, and lack governed workflows. Manual processing takes 2-3 days per document.

Method: GUIDE is a governed multi-agent framework with six specialized agents: parsing, VLM-driven extraction, consistency checking, evaluation, HITL escalation, and persona-tailored artifact synthesis. Built on shared versioned rule store with schema-validated inter-agent contracts and end-to-end provenance tracking.

Result: Evaluated on 120 real-world enterprise guideline documents: 96% document success rate, extracted 3,896 rules (71.4% auto-approved), produced 812 deployment-ready artifacts, reduced turnaround to 40-125 minutes per document (vs. 2-3 days manually).

Conclusion: GUIDE successfully addresses enterprise guideline processing challenges through a governed multi-agent approach with validation and provenance tracking, achieving high automation rates and significant time savings while maintaining quality through specialized agents and human oversight mechanisms.

Abstract: Enterprise guideline documents are heterogeneous and multimodal, combining narrative text, complex tables, and embedded images. Existing LLM and VLM systems face hallucinated content, table structure degradation, and lack governed workflows extending beyond extraction to validation and artifact generation. This leaves enterprises to perform this manually, consuming 2-3 days per document. To address this, we introduce GUIDE, a governed multi-agent framework built on a shared versioned rule store with schema-validated inter-agent contracts and end-to-end provenance tracking. Six specialized agents handle parsing, VLM-driven extraction, consistency checking, evaluation, human-in-the-loop (HITL) escalation, and persona-tailored artifact synthesis. Evaluated on 120 real-world enterprise guideline documents, GUIDE achieves 96% document success, extracts 3,896 rules with 71.4% auto-approved, produces 812 deployment-ready artifacts, and reduces turnaround to 40-125 minutes per document.

</details>


### [72] [Who Thinks Best Depends on How Long You Let Them: Budget-Dependent Rankings in LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12150)
*Rodrigo Guedes de Souza,Alison R. Panisson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM rankings are unstable across different token generation budgets, with model performance showing non-monotonic behavior and rankings reversing as budgets change, necessitating budget-aware evaluation protocols.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper challenges the standard assumption that LLM rankings remain stable across different inference conditions, particularly token generation budgets. Current evaluation practices assume consistent model performance regardless of budget constraints, but this may not reflect real-world deployment scenarios where computational budgets vary.

Method: The researchers varied token generation budgets across seven levels (64-4,096 tokens), evaluating four models on three reasoning benchmarks (56,476 total inferences). They analyzed non-monotone behavior, model ranking reversals using McNemar tests, conducted oracle analysis for optimal model selection, and developed budget-aware routing systems.

Result: Key findings: (1) 3-19% of items show non-monotone behavior (accuracy decreases with more budget) that is model-specific; (2) Model rankings reverse across budgets on all benchmarks; (3) Oracle analysis reveals significant model complementarity (up to +27.8 percentage points), especially at constrained budgets; (4) Budget-aware routing captures 14.1% of oracle gap cross-domain, with budget features helping within-domain but hurting transfer.

Conclusion: Standard LLM evaluation protocols assuming stable model rankings across inference conditions are inadequate. Token generation budgets significantly impact model performance and rankings, requiring budget-conditioned evaluation practices that account for how different models perform under varying computational constraints.

Abstract: Standard evaluation of large language models assumes stable model rankings across inference conditions. We challenge this assumption by varying the token generation budget, i.e., the maximum tokens a model may produce, across seven levels (64--4,096), evaluating four models on three reasoning benchmarks (56,476 inferences). We report four findings: (i) 3--19% of items exhibit non-monotone behavior (accuracy decreasing with more budget), even after controlling for truncation, and this phenomenon is model-specific (cross-model overlap: 6--14%). (ii) Model rankings reverse across budgets on all benchmarks ($p {<} 0.01$, McNemar). (iii) Oracle analysis reveals model complementarity up to $+27.8$pp, most pronounced at constrained budgets. (iv) A budget-aware router captures 14.1% of the oracle gap cross-domain; budget features help within-domain ($+1.6$ to $+5.7$pp) but are domain-specific and hurt transfer ($-1.2$pp). These results argue for budget-conditioned evaluation protocols.

</details>


### [73] [How to Spend Your Oracle Budget: Practical Guidance for Protein Structure Prediction Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12192)
*Aleksandra Kalisz,Jack Simons,Krisztina Sinkovics,Noam Ghenassia,Shikha Surana,Henry Moss,Paul Duckworth*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Benchmark study of oracle-guided protein structure prediction methods (FK-steering, DPO, Best K-of-N, O3) reveals different methods excel at different oracle budgets, with O3 best for low budgets and FK-steering/DPO better for high budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models for protein structure prediction are unreliable on certain targets, and while external oracles can correct failures, biological oracles are expensive. Existing guidance methods differ in how they spend oracle budget, but there's no systematic comparison to guide method selection.

Method: Benchmark FK-steering, DPO, Best K-of-N sampling, and Optimisation Over Outputs (O3) methods. Extend O3 to protein structure prediction models and evaluate on two protein targets: calmodulin (1CLL) and E. coli aspartate transcarbamoylase (9EEH).

Result: No single method consistently dominates across all budgets and oracles. O3 is most effective at low oracle budgets, while FK-steering and DPO show improved performance as budget increases. Results vary across different protein targets and oracle constraints.

Conclusion: The study provides the first practical reference for oracle budget-aware guidance in protein structure prediction, offering actionable recommendations for practitioners operating under real-world oracle-budget constraints.

Abstract: Foundation models for protein structure prediction remain unreliable on certain targets. External oracles can flag and correct these failures, but biological oracles are expensive, making oracle budget a critical constraint. Existing guidance methods, such as FK-steering, DPO, and Best K-of-N sampling, differ in how they spend this budget, yet no systematic comparison exists to guide method selection. To bridge this gap, we benchmark these methods alongside the recently proposed Optimisation Over Outputs (O3), which applies off-the-shelf optimisers within a generative model's latent subspace. We extend the usage of O3 to protein structure prediction models. Overall, our work provides the first practical reference for oracle budget-aware guidance. Our evaluation on two protein targets, calmodulin (1CLL) and E. coli aspartate transcarbamoylase (9EEH), reveals that no single method consistently dominates across all budgets and oracles. Specifically, O3 proves most effective at low oracle budgets, while FK-steering and DPO demonstrate improved performance as the budget increases. We distil these findings into actionable recommendations for practitioners operating under real-world oracle-budget constraints.

</details>


### [74] [An Agentic Workflow for Legacy HPC Modernization: Converting the Two-Electron-Integral Core of GAMESS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12249)
*Yuzhong Shen,Masha Sosonkina,Peng Xu,Mark S. Gordon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic workflow automates modernization of legacy Fortran 77 to Fortran 2008 for GAMESS quantum chemistry package, achieving bit-for-bit correctness across 56K lines and 612 test runs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modernizing legacy Fortran codebases is critical for computational science but often neglected due to enormous scale - transformations are routine but codebases are massive, requiring automation at production scale.

Method: Three prompt-specialized AI agent roles operate under a version-controlled specification they authored/revised, with human gates. Uses exact verification oracle from domain (bit-for-bit reproduction of energies as merge criterion). Applied to convert GAMESS quantum chemistry package's two-electron-integral routines from Fortran 77 to Fortran 2008.

Result: Successfully converted 12 source files (56,448 lines, 225 subroutines). All files pass 51-test validation battery including GAMESS standard tests, with zero chemistry-relevant differences across 612 test runs. Every file passes Jenkins continuous integration tests.

Conclusion: Agentic workflows can safely modernize legacy scientific code at production scale when paired with exact verification oracles, with safety boundary defined by where the oracle stops seeing. The approach successfully automated transformation of critical scientific code while maintaining strict numerical correctness.

Abstract: Modernizing legacy Fortran is a problem of volume: the transformations are individually routine, but the codebases can be enormous, and across much of computational science the work simply goes undone. We propose an agentic workflow that takes this work on at production scale, and we set out to measure how far such delegation can reach. In this work, three prompt-specialized agent roles operate under a version-controlled specification that the agents themselves authored and revised, while humans hold a small number of gates. The arrangement is kept safe by an exact verification oracle inherited from the domain, and the boundary of safe delegation lies exactly where that oracle stops seeing.
  We apply the proposed workflow in a case study, converting the two-electron-integral routines of GAMESS (General Atomic and Molecular Electronic Structure System), a mature quantum-chemistry package with a 48-year development history, from fixed-form Fortran 77 to free-form Fortran 2008. The scope of this work was twelve source files, 56,448 lines, and 225 subroutines for computing electron repulsion integrals. The agents ran as three Claude Code roles in isolated worktrees, and the work spanned four Claude model generations. Because the GAMESS group ships a standard test suite whose printed energies its user community treats as canonical, we could adopt bit-for-bit reproduction of those energies as the merge criterion, where a deviation in the twelfth decimal place counts as a failure rather than drift. All twelve source files pass a 51-test validation battery comprising the 49 standard GAMESS tests and two additional calculations, and across 612 test runs the number of chemistry-relevant differences is zero, and every file also passes the Jenkins tests that are used for continuous integration.

</details>


### [75] [VAKRA: Evaluating Multi-Hop Reasoning Across APIs and Retrieval Under Tool-Use Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12282)
*Ankita Rajaram Naik,Anupama Murthi,Benjamin Elder,Siyu Huo,Raavi Gupta,Abhinav Jain,Praveen Venkateswaran,Abdulhamid Adebayo,Danish Contractor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VAKRA is a benchmark for evaluating agents' ability to reason across structured APIs and document collections, testing API interaction, multi-hop reasoning, and multi-source reasoning with policy constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks evaluate API reasoning and document retrieval in isolation, lacking integrated evaluation of agents that must operate across both structured APIs and document collections in enterprise settings.

Method: Created VAKRA benchmark with over 8,000 executable APIs across 62 domains, testing three difficulty settings: diverse API interaction styles, multi-hop reasoning over APIs, and multi-source reasoning with natural-language policy constraints. Uses ReAct harness to isolate model capabilities from architecture, verifying correctness by re-executing predicted tool calls against live APIs.

Result: Even best models achieve only 70.4% on single-hop tasks, dropping to 50-51% on compositional APIs. Performance degrades over 50% with increasing reasoning depth. Policy-constrained questions expose severe failures (as low as 2.4% on unanswerable queries). Trace analysis shows failures concentrate at language-mediated reasoning rather than tool invocation mechanics.

Conclusion: VAKRA reveals significant gaps in current models' ability to reason across APIs and documents, particularly in multi-hop reasoning and policy-constrained scenarios, highlighting the need for improved language-mediated reasoning capabilities.

Abstract: Agents deployed in enterprise settings must reason across structured APIs and document collections, yet existing benchmarks evaluate these capabilities in isolation. We introduce VAKRA (e\textbf{V}aluating \textbf{A}PI and \textbf{K}nowledge \textbf{R}etrieval \textbf{A}gents), a benchmark of over $8{,}000$ executable APIs across $62$ domains with tasks spanning three settings of increasing difficulty: diverse API interaction styles, multi-hop reasoning over structured APIs, and multi-source reasoning with natural-language tool-use policy constraints. Correctness is verified by re-executing predicted tool calls against live APIs, accommodating multiple valid paths. Using a fixed ReAct harness to isolate model capabilities from agent architecture, we evaluate frontier and open-weight models and find that even the best model achieves only 70.4\% on single-hop endpoint-style tasks and drops to 50--51\% on compositional APIs; performance degrades by over 50\% as reasoning depth increases, and policy-constrained questions expose severe failures (as low as 2.4\% on unanswerable queries). Trace analysis shows failures concentrate at language-mediated reasoning - entity disambiguation, cross-source grounding, rather than tool invocation mechanics. Code is available https://github.com/IBM/VAKRA. Dataset is available https://huggingface.co/datasets/ibm-research/VAKRA

</details>


### [76] [Constructing Dynamic Master Logic Models as Knowledge Graphs for Complex System Diagnostics Using Retrieval-Augmented Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12304)
*Saman Marandi,Yu-Shu Hu,Mohammad Modarres*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Automated framework converts system descriptions into executable knowledge graphs (KG-DML) using RAG and LLMs, enabling scalable diagnostic reasoning for complex systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic Master Logic (DML) requires expert interpretation of technical documentation, limiting scalability for complex systems; automated construction is needed to transform documentation into executable functional models.

Method: Uses Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Large Language Models to automatically construct KG-DML models from system descriptions, preserving functional dependencies and logical relationships across the DML hierarchy with targeted retrieval.

Result: Successfully extends automated KG-DML construction to large complex systems (Low-Pressure Coolant Injection system of a Boiling Water Reactor), demonstrating consistent reconstruction and supporting diagnostic reasoning, safety assessment, and failure propagation analysis.

Conclusion: Automated KG-DML construction can transform technical documentation into executable functional models for diagnostic and reliability analysis, addressing scalability limitations of manual DML construction.
