Abstract: Dynamic Master Logic (DML) provides a hierarchical framework for representing system behavior by linking functional objectives to underlying structural elements. However, DML construction typically relies on expert interpretation of technical documentation, limiting scalability for complex systems. This study presents a framework for automated construction of DML models from system descriptions and their representation as Knowledge Graphs (KG-DML), using Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Large Language Models as enabling tools. Building on prior work with small-scale systems, the framework extends automated KG-DML construction and evaluation to substantially larger and more complex systems. Model construction proceeds across the DML hierarchy using targeted retrieval while preserving functional dependencies and explicit logical relationships. The resulting KG-DML supports diagnostic reasoning, safety assessment, upward failure propagation, and downward dependency tracing. A multi-level validation methodology evaluates layer-specific precision and recall, logical gate consistency, and overall structural integrity. Application to the Low-Pressure Coolant Injection system of a decommissioned Boiling Water Reactor demonstrates consistent reconstruction across repeated runs. The results show that automated KG-DML construction can transform technical documentation into executable functional models for diagnostic and reliability analysis.

</details>


### [77] [Reproducing and Stress-Testing Two Approaches to LLM Reasoning Reliability: Test-Time Probability Aggregation and Logic-Representation Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.08514)
*Minhan Cho,Jimin Kweon*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper independently reproduces and stress-tests two LLM reliability methods (RPC and LCF) across domains and models, finding RPC's advantages over self-consistency are not significant and LCF's logic-validity effect is weak with mixed performance impacts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to validate reliability claims of RPC and LCF methods through independent reproduction and stress-testing, since original evaluations were conducted by the methods' authors without independent verification across models/domains, and LCF had no public code.

Method: Re-running RPC's published-path aggregation, re-implementing LCF's projector/contrastive/intervention pipeline, extending both methods to four new task domains, varying models, directly probing LCF's representations, and conducting statistical significance tests.

Result: RPC reproduces original results but shows no significant advantage over self-consistency on new domains; LCF's logic-validity direction exists but is weak (0.82 vs 0.95 semantic control), with one positive effect not significant and two models showing significantly reduced performance.

Conclusion: Claims about RPC and LCF's reliability improvements should be treated cautiously, as RPC's benefits don't generalize significantly and LCF's effects are weak with potential harms, highlighting the need for independent reproduction and broader validation.

Abstract: We independently reproduce two recent methods for making large language model (LLM) reasoning more reliable, and stress-test them across domains and models (RPC across four new task domains with Qwen3-8B, LCF across four 7-8B models). The first, RPC, aggregates token probabilities and self-consistency at inference; the second, LCF, trains projectors that split hidden states into "content" and "logic" and edits the logic part toward a valid region. Validating such reliability claims matters because the original evaluations are run by each method's own authors and were never independently reproduced or stress-tested across models and domains, and LCF shipped no public code. We re-run RPC's published-path aggregation and re-implement LCF's projector, contrastive, and intervention pipeline, then extend both to text-to-SQL, legal extraction, fallacy identification, and precedent grading, and probe LCF's representation directly. RPC reproduces the original grid exactly on the authors' released reasoning paths; on four new domains its edge over self-consistency is never significant (ties or small mixed differences, paired p >= 0.28), and on BIRD, the one domain where we vary the budget, the edge grows with K as predicted but its largest gap (+2.5 accuracy at K=32, p=0.16) reverses to -0.25 when we enlarge the sample to n=200. LCF's logic-validity direction is real but weak (0.82 separability at the single best sub-layer versus 0.95 for a semantic-attribute control); its one positive effect (Qwen3 $Δ$Prob) is not significant (p=0.56), while it significantly reduces $Δ$Prob on two of the other three models.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [78] [FarSky: Task-Aware Latent-Space Coupling for Generative Intra-Hour Solar Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11254)
*Yann Fabel,Bijan Nouri,Milon Miah,Niklas Blum,Luis F. Zarzalejo,Julia Kowalski,Robert Pitz-Paal*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FarSky: A generative forecasting framework that combines latent diffusion models with task-aware latent representations for probabilistic solar irradiance forecasting using all-sky imagers, achieving state-of-the-art performance in deterministic and probabilistic forecasts with improved ramp event detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate solar irradiance forecasting is essential for reliable photovoltaic power integration. While recent deep learning approaches have improved accuracy, they are often limited by deterministic predictions and poor anticipation of ramp events (sudden changes in irradiance).

Method: Proposes FarSky framework: 1) Multi-task autoencoder learns shared latent representation for both image reconstruction and irradiance estimation, 2) Latent diffusion model generates future latent states conditioned on recent observations, 3) Probabilistic forecasts obtained through stochastic sampling from the diffusion model, 4) Irradiance forecasts decoded directly from generated latent states.

Result: Evaluated on multi-year ASI dataset from Plataforma Solar de Almería, Spain. FarSky achieves best overall deterministic and probabilistic forecasting performance, improving forecast skill by up to 11 percentage points. Substantially improves ramp event detection with F1-scores above 60%, outperforming persistence, state-of-the-art end-to-end, and generative forecasting approaches.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the potential of combining generative models with task-aware latent-space coupling for solar forecasting. The approach effectively addresses limitations of deterministic predictions and ramp event anticipation while providing inherent probabilistic forecasting capabilities through stochastic sampling.

Abstract: Accurate solar irradiance forecasting is essential for the reliable integration of photovoltaic power into modern electricity grids. All-sky imagers (ASI) provide high-resolution observations of clouds, making them well suited for intra-hour forecasting. Recent deep learning approaches have substantially improved forecast accuracy but are often limited by deterministic predictions and a reduced capability to anticipate ramp events. This work proposes FarSky, a generative forecasting framework that leverages latent-space coupling to learn task-aware representations of sky images. A multi-task autoencoder first learns a shared latent representation for image reconstruction and irradiance estimation. A latent diffusion model then generates future latent states conditioned on recent observations, from which irradiance forecasts are directly decoded. Probabilistic forecasts are inherently obtained through stochastic sampling. The framework is developed using a multi-year ASI dataset acquired at the Plataforma Solar de Almería, Spain, and evaluated on two independent test datasets against persistence, state-of-the-art end-to-end, and generative forecasting approaches. FarSky achieves the best overall deterministic and probabilistic forecasting performance, improving forecast skill by up to 11 percentage points. Furthermore, it substantially improves ramp event detection over existing methods, achieving F1-scores above 60%. These results demonstrate the potential of combining generative models with task-aware latent-space coupling for solar forecasting.

</details>


### [79] [Why AI Detection Fails for Academic Integrity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11256)
*Jonathan A. Karr,Grigorii Khvatskii,Ting Hua,Nitesh V. Chawla*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AI detectors can't differentiate between legitimate AI editing assistance and full AI-generated content, leading to unfair sanctions for honest academic AI use while humanizer tools enable near-complete evasion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Commercial AI detectors are widely used by institutions to detect academic misconduct, but they cannot distinguish between legitimate AI editing/assistance (which may be guideline-compliant) and full LLM-generated drafts. This creates a policy failure where honest students using AI for editing get unfairly penalized while those using evasion tools escape detection.

Method: Controlled study comparing published English abstracts from four domains across two time periods (2013-2015 vs 2023-2025). Used proxy human/AI labels with tau=0.50 threshold. Tested three conditions: 1) "refine abstract only" edits (proxy for guideline-compliant AI assistance), 2) unmodified 2023-2025 originals, and 3) AI-labeled rewrites after Undetectable AI humanization. Analyzed detection rates using Pangram and GPTZero detectors, with statistical analysis of STEM vs non-STEM differences and linguistic features.

Result: Light "refine abstract only" AI edits were flagged at 64-80% detection rates. Unmodified 2023-2025 abstracts had 9-15% false positive rates, with non-STEM rates significantly higher than STEM (p<0.001). Detection patterns tracked linguistic features like long-token density and Academic Word List density rather than authorship intent. After humanization with Undetectable AI, evasion was near-total: <4% of AI-labeled rewrites remained flagged (FNR >96%), meaning honest AI editing results in higher sanction risk than humanizer-assisted evasion.

Conclusion: AI detector scores should not serve as standalone evidence of academic misconduct because they cannot reliably distinguish between legitimate AI assistance and full AI-generated content, while simultaneously being easily evaded by humanizer tools. This creates an unfair system where honest users face sanctions while dishonest users can escape detection.

Abstract: Institutions use commercial AI detectors for academic integrity, yet detectors cannot distinguish AI editing from full LLM drafts and may treat both as misconduct. In a controlled study of published English abstracts (four domains; 2013 to 2015 vs. 2023 to 2025), we quantify this policy failure under proxy human/AI labels at tau=0.50. Light "refine abstract only" edits, a proxy for guideline-compliant AI assistance, are flagged at 64 to 80% (Pangram/GPTZero). Unmodified 2023 to 2025 originals are flagged at 9 to 15%, with non-STEM rates far above STEM (p<0.001); elevated scores track long-token and Academic Word List density, not authorship intent alone. After Undetectable AI humanization, evasion is near-total: fewer than 4% of AI-labeled rewrites remain flagged (post-humanization detection rate <4%; FNR >96%). Honest AI-editing results in a higher sanction risk than humanizer-assisted evasion. Therefore, detector scores should not serve as standalone misconduct evidence.

</details>


### [80] [Basin: Efficient and Extensible Numerical Optimization in Rust](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11279)
*Johan Larsson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Basin is a comprehensive Rust library for numerical optimization problems with extensive solver options and constraint support


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Numerical optimization is fundamental across scientific and engineering domains for tasks like model fitting, simulation calibration, machine learning training, and cost minimization, but there's a need for a unified, consistent approach to state and solve such problems

Method: Developed as a Rust programming library that provides a single, consistent framework for formulating optimization problems, with a broad catalog of solvers and first-class support for constraints

Result: Created Basin - a numerical optimization library that offers unified problem specification and solving capabilities, extensive solver options, and robust constraint handling within the Rust ecosystem

Conclusion: Basin successfully addresses the need for a comprehensive, unified optimization solution in Rust, providing researchers and engineers with powerful tools for diverse optimization tasks across multiple domains

Abstract: Basin is a numerical optimization library for the Rust programming language. Numerical optimization is the task of finding the inputs that minimize a function, and it is a fundamental element across the sciences: fitting a model to data, calibrating a simulation, training a machine learning model, or choosing engineering parameters that minimize cost. Basin gives users a single, consistent way to both state and solve such problems, with a broad catalog of solvers and first-class support for constraints.

</details>


### [81] [Federated Learning for Distributed CNC Tool Wear Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11281)
*Afsana Khan,Morris Stallmann,Marcin Pietrasik,Charis Kouzinopoulos,Anna Wilbik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Federated learning enables collaborative tool wear prediction for CNC machining without sharing raw data, achieving near-centralized performance while significantly outperforming local models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning for tool wear prediction faces limitations in industrial settings due to distributed data across machines/sites/organizations and restrictions on data sharing, creating a need for privacy-preserving collaborative approaches.

Method: The paper investigates federated learning for CNC tool wear prediction by simulating clients with distributed tool trajectories, comparing federated models against centralized references and local client baselines.

Result: Federated learning achieves performance close to centralized learning and shows significant improvement over local client models, demonstrating its effectiveness for distributed CNC environments.

Conclusion: Federated learning can effectively support collaborative tool wear prediction in distributed CNC manufacturing environments, overcoming data sharing restrictions while maintaining model performance comparable to centralized approaches.

Abstract: Tool wear prediction is an important task in CNC machining, where accurate monitoring of tool condition supports product quality and process reliability. Machine learning methods have shown potential for this task, but their use in industrial environments is limited by the distributed nature of machining data and by restrictions on data sharing between machines, sites, or organizations. Federated learning offers a suitable framework for this setting by enabling collaborative model training without transferring raw operational data. This paper investigates federated learning for CNC tool wear prediction. Tool trajectories are distributed across simulated clients to represent a federated learning scenario. The federated models are compared against centralized references and local client baselines. Results show that federated learning achieves performance close to centralized learning and improves significantly over local client models. These findings indicate that federated learning can support collaborative tool wear prediction in distributed CNC manufacturing environments.

</details>


### [82] [Terminal Symmetry as a Decision Resource: Statewise Refinement for Anytime Verified Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11318)
*Yi Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a method (TRC) that transforms terminal symmetry in sequential construction tasks into a decision-making resource, achieving improved performance across various domains by using statewise refresh mechanisms and formal guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many sequential construction tasks have exact symmetry at completion but require directed, history-dependent execution during the process. The authors aim to leverage terminal symmetry as a reusable decision resource rather than treating it as a static property.

Method: Develops the Transport-Refine-Certify (TRC) framework: 1) Process evidence provides directionality, 2) Terminal correspondence transports structure across equivalent outcomes, 3) Realized-state evidence refines decision relevance after transitions, and 4) A fixed verifier certifies execution. This is instantiated with episode-fixed transported process structure, state-restricted process rank, state-dependent residual rank refreshed after accepted transitions, and an ordinal rank meet operation.

Result: The method achieves significant improvements: statewise refresh improves anytime AUC by up to 6.77, 21.75, and 8.68 points across CAD assembly, Mini-Programs, and exact-fill packing domains. On 1,135 target-removal episodes from GRN OOD scenes, it attains the lowest mean capped verifier cost at all three scales among compared GRN and CDGS-style planners. The approach provides completion guarantees under prefix coverage and tight worst-case verifier-query bounds.

Conclusion: Terminal symmetry can be transformed from a passive property into an active decision resource for directed construction tasks. The TRC framework provides formal guarantees and practical performance improvements across multiple domains, demonstrating that statewise refresh mechanisms effectively leverage symmetry for improved decision-making in sequential construction problems.

Abstract: Many sequential construction tasks exhibit exact symmetry at completion while their execution remains directed and history-dependent. We develop a decision-resource view of terminal symmetry: process evidence supplies directionality, terminal correspondence transports that structure across equivalent outcomes, realized-state evidence refines its current decision relevance after transitions, and a fixed verifier certifies execution. This decomposition yields transport--refine--certify. \method{} instantiates the principle with an episode-fixed transported process structure, its state-restricted process rank, a state-dependent residual rank refreshed after accepted transitions, and an ordinal rank meet whose top-$k$ set is exactly the union of the two proposal prefixes. The meet provides a completion guarantee under prefix coverage and attains the tight worst-case verifier-query bound under the corresponding prefix information model; a two-state construction predicts a strict post-transition dynamic--static separation. Across CAD assembly, Mini-Programs, and exact-fill packing, statewise refresh improves anytime AUC by up to $6.77$, $21.75$, and $8.68$ points, respectively. On 1,135 target-removal episodes from the official GRN OOD scenes, \method{} attains the lowest mean capped verifier cost at all three scales among the compared GRN and CDGS-style planners. The statewise signal also transfers across aggregation and scheduler organizations. Terminal symmetry thereby becomes a reusable decision resource for directed construction.

</details>


### [83] [Contextual Quality-Diversity Evolutionary Reinforcement Learning for HVAC Control in Tropical Commercial Buildings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11324)
*Tran Le Vu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CQD-ERL: contextual quality-diversity evolutionary RL controller for HVAC systems that maintains diverse policies for different operating conditions with safety shielding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for intelligent supervisory control of tropical water-cooled chiller plants that can handle diverse operating conditions (weather, load regimes) rather than converging to a single optimal policy.

Method: Proposes CQD-ERL controller that maintains product archive of specialized policies indexed by operating context and behavior descriptors, using evolutionary and policy-gradient operators sharing a replay buffer with deterministic safety shield.

Result: Controller trained on reduced-order environment representing Singapore building constraints and evaluated against ASHRAE Guideline 36 baseline in full annual backtest.

Conclusion: The approach enables adaptive supervisory control that maintains policy diversity for varying conditions while ensuring safety through shielding.

Abstract: This paper proposes a contextual quality-diversity evolutionary reinforcement-learning controller, CQD-ERL, for the supervisory control of a tropical, water-cooled chiller plant and its associated air side. Rather than converging to a single scalarised policy, the controller maintains a product archive of specialised policies indexed jointly by a data- driven operating context, a cluster of daily weather and load regime, and a context-invariant behaviour descriptor, filled by a gradient-free evolutionary operator and a soft-actor-critic policy-gradient operator that share one replay buffer. Every action is filtered through a deterministic safety shield before execution. The controller is trained on a two-tier reduced-order environment representing the latent load, cooling-tower approach and humidity constraints of a Singapore commercial building, and is evaluated over a full annual backtest against an ASHRAE Guideline 36 baseline.

</details>


### [84] [Long-Horizon Forecasting of Complete Financial Statements with Forma](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11327)
*Travis L. Johnson,Jiannan Jiang,Soumyabrata Chaudhuri,Yihao Chen,Lauren Falvey,Donal O'Cofaigh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Forma, a transformer model using masked-tuple Gaussian likelihood, outperforms all competitors in forecasting financial statements 1-20 quarters ahead, with its advantage growing at longer horizons where valuation accuracy matters most.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No prior work jointly forecasts complete financial statements beyond one year, yet most firm value in discounted-cash-flow valuation lies beyond that window. There's a need for accurate long-term financial statement forecasting.

Method: Forma is a transformer model that reads financial statements as sets of (account, quarter, value) tuples and maximizes a masked-tuple Gaussian likelihood. It uses ProForma-20Q benchmark with 78 line items forecasted 1-20 quarters ahead from past statements and industry codes.

Result: Forma beats all competitors (classical ML, chained gradient boosting, zero-shot time-series foundation model, and frontier LLMs). Its lead widens with forecast horizon, Gaussian predictive intervals never under-cover, forecasts nearly satisfy accounting identities, and scenario analysis is supported without retraining.

Conclusion: Specialist training beats generalist scale for financial statement forecasting. Forma demonstrates superior performance, especially at longer horizons critical for valuation, while maintaining accounting coherence and supporting practical applications like scenario analysis.

Abstract: Specialist training beats generalist scale when forecasting financial statements. To our knowledge, no prior work jointly forecasts complete financial statements beyond one year, yet in a discounted-cash-flow valuation most firm value sits past that window. We release ProForma-20Q, a reproducible benchmark for forecasting 78 statement line items 1-20 quarters ahead, for anonymized firms, from past statements and an industry code, scored by change-space $R^2$. On it, Forma, a transformer that reads statements as sets of (account, quarter, value) tuples and maximizes a masked-tuple Gaussian likelihood, beats every competitor we field: classical machine learning, chained gradient boosting, a zero-shot time-series foundation model, and frontier large language models. Its lead widens with horizon, where valuation needs accuracy most, and its Gaussian predictive intervals never under-cover. Forma's forecasts nearly satisfy accounting identities; exact coherence is recoverable at no statistically significant accuracy cost. Its tuple interface supports scenario analysis without retraining, and we show that pinning future revenue paths sharpens the rest of the statement.

</details>


### [85] [Weightless Fine-Tuning: Personalizing LLMs via Logit-Space Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11342)
*Bohan Zhang,Anqi Ni,Yixin Wang,Paramveer S. Dhillon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Weightless Fine-Tuning (WFT) is a training-free decoding method that approximates supervised fine-tuning effects without weight updates by computing residuals and using cross-prefix transport operators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is costly for personalization tasks where each user requires separate weight access, optimization, storage, and retraining, making it prohibitive for many applications.

Method: WFT computes supervised residuals on training sequences and transports them to current prompts using cross-prefix transport operators estimated from dropout-induced cross-covariance, replacing gradient updates with logit-space corrections.

Result: On three LaMP personalization benchmarks, WFT achieves best average performance, matches/exceeds SFT on individual tasks, outperforms lightweight baselines, and uses less than 7% of SFT's effective computation in budget-controlled comparisons.

Conclusion: WFT effectively captures the distributional effect of supervised adaptation without modifying model weights, achieving high cosine similarity (0.875) between its logit shifts and those of SFT, offering a cost-effective alternative for personalization tasks.

Abstract: Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is a standard approach for adapting LLMs to a target distribution, but in settings such as personalization, where each author requires separate weight access, optimization, storage, and retraining, its costs become prohibitive. We propose Weightless Fine-Tuning (WFT), a training-free decoding-time method that approximates the distributional effect of SFT without weight updates. WFT computes supervised residuals on an author's training sequence and transports them to the current prompt through a cross-prefix transport operator estimated from dropout-induced cross-covariance. The operator captures how a perturbation at one context propagates to predictions at another, replacing gradient-based parameter updates with logit-space corrections. On three LaMP personalization benchmarks, WFT achieves the best average performance across datasets, matches or exceeds SFT on individual tasks, and outperforms other lightweight baselines on average. In a budget-controlled comparison, WFT approaches SFT performance using less than 7% of the effective computation. Logit-level analysis shows a cosine similarity of 0.875 between the logit shifts induced by WFT and SFT over 95% of the next-token probability mass, suggesting that WFT captures the distributional effect of supervised adaptation without modifying model weights.

</details>


### [86] [Dynamics Models for Offline Hyperparameter Selection in Real-World RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11349)
*Jordan Coblin,Han Wang,Martha White,Adam White*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Calibration models trained on offline data enable RL hyperparameter selection in real-world industrial settings without costly online experimentation, demonstrated in a municipal water treatment plant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world RL deployment faces a key obstacle: hyperparameter selection without simulators is difficult and online experimentation is costly. Prior calibration models were only tested in simple simulations, not real industrial settings.

Method: Evaluated several calibration model approaches including k-nearest neighbors with Laplacian distance metric on high-dimensional, non-stationary sensor data for nexting prediction tasks. Tested scaling to year-long datasets, selection of fine-tuning learning rates for pre-trained agents, and robustness under distribution shift.

Result: Calibration models can generate realistic long-horizon rollouts and recover meaningful hyperparameter sensitivity trends. They successfully scale to large datasets and support hyperparameter selection in a real industrial water treatment plant.

Conclusion: The study provides a proof of concept for using offline dynamics models to support RL deployment in real-world environments, while highlighting important practical challenges for future work in industrial applications.

Abstract: A key obstacle to deploying reinforcement learning in real-world systems is hyperparameter selection, particularly when simulators are unavailable and online experimentation is costly. Prior work has proposed calibration models trained on offline data to approximate environment dynamics and enable offline hyperparameter selection, but these methods have so far been evaluated only in simple simulated settings. In this paper, we present the first application of calibration models in a real-world industrial setting: a municipal water treatment plant. We evaluate several calibration model approaches, including a k-nearest neighbors model with a Laplacian distance metric, on high-dimensional, non-stationary sensor data for nexting prediction tasks. Our results show that these models can generate realistic long-horizon rollouts and recover meaningful hyperparameter sensitivity trends. We further examine how calibration models scale to year-long datasets, how they support the selection of fine-tuning learning rates for pre-trained agents, and how robust they are under distribution shift. Overall, our findings provide a proof of concept for using offline dynamics models to support RL deployment in real-world environments, while highlighting important practical challenges for future work.

</details>


### [87] [Market-Information-Aware Gated-LoRA of Foundation Models for Transferable Day-Ahead Electricity Price Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11359)
*Hang Fan,Wei Wei,Shengwei Mei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A market-information-aware adaptation framework transfers Chronos-2 time-series foundation model to day-ahead electricity price forecasting using gated LoRA with market-specific signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Electricity price forecasting is difficult due to volatility and market-specific factors. Existing supervised methods rely heavily on historical data from each specific market, which limits their applicability in new or data-scarce electricity markets.

Method: The paper proposes a framework that constructs a multi-source market information (MSMI) interface aligning price context with pre-clearing market variables. It trains a source-domain gated low-rank adapter (LoRA) that updates only about 1% of model parameters without requiring target-market labels. The gate scales the frozen source adapter based on reserve-tightness and operating-state signals.

Result: Experiments on four Chinese provincial day-ahead spot markets show the framework reduces average MAE/RMSE by 6.24%/7.99% compared to market-information-aware zero-shot Chronos-2, and by 3.05%/3.52% compared to vanilla Source-LoRA. The gain is not reproduced by learned global scalar or random gate initialization.

Conclusion: Market-structured inputs and state-dependent gated LoRA provide a practical transfer path for data-scarce electricity markets, enabling effective cross-market knowledge transfer for price forecasting.

Abstract: Electricity price forecasting is crucial for market participants but remains difficult because prices are volatile, market-specific, and closely tied to anticipated system conditions. Existing supervised methods depend largely on market-specific historical data, limiting their use in newly established or data-scarce markets. This paper proposes a market-information-aware adaptation framework that transfers the Chronos-2 time-series foundation model to day-ahead electricity price forecasting. It first constructs a multi-source market information (MSMI) interface aligning 7-day price context with pre-clearing supply--demand, reserve, maintenance, generator-capacity, and intertie variables, and then trains a source-domain gated low-rank adapter (LoRA), updating about $1\%$ of model parameters without target-market labels. The gate scales the frozen source adapter according to reserve-tightness and operating-state signals. A leave-one-market-out protocol is adopted for evaluating cross-market transferability. Experiments on four Chinese provincial day-ahead spot markets show that the proposed framework reduces the average MAE/RMSE by $6.24\%/7.99\%$ relative to market-information-aware zero-shot Chronos-2 and by $3.05\%/3.52\%$ relative to vanilla Source-LoRA. Experiments show that the gain is not reproduced by a learned global scalar or by random gate initialization, while the additional improvement over Source-LoRA is limited. These results suggest that market-structured inputs and state-dependent gated LoRA can provide a practical transfer path for data-scarce electricity markets.

</details>


### [88] [Lifecycle-Optimal Tokenization: Vocabulary Size as a Deployment-Regime-Dependent Infrastructure Parameter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11361)
*Rima Mittal,Ankit Gubrani,Satyanarayana Kakollu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Vocabulary size in LLMs should be optimized based on deployment context (batch size and inference volume), not fixed at training time, as cost-optimal vocab shifts 16x from 32k to 524k depending on serving regime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tokenizer vocabulary size is typically fixed at training time based on convention rather than deployment analysis, ignoring that optimal vocabulary depends on serving regime and inference volume.

Method: Formalize total deployment cost as lifecycle cost function combining training and inference costs. Conduct controlled experiments on two GPU families (A10G and A100) across memory-bound to compute-bound regimes, varying batch sizes to analyze vocabulary impact.

Result: Inference-optimal vocabulary shifts 16x with batch size (32k at B=1 to 524k at B=64+); quality optimized at V=65k for 1.3-2.3B models; lifecycle-optimal diverges from training-optimal by up to 16x; quality is approximately invariant across optimal range.

Conclusion: Vocabulary size is a pure systems optimization with minimal quality penalty. Deployment guidance: on-device (B=1) use V≈32k; datacenter serving (B≥64, λ≥10) use V≈131-262k. Vocabulary should be chosen based on deployment context, not fixed at training.

Abstract: Tokenizer vocabulary size is a foundational design choice in large language model (LLM) infrastructure, yet it is typically fixed at training time based on convention rather than deployment analysis. We show that the cost-optimal vocabulary is not a constant but a function of the serving regime. We formalize total deployment cost as $C_{lifecycle}(V) = C_{train}(V) + λ\cdot C_{infer}(V, B)$, where $λ$ is inference volume and $B$ is the serving batch size. Through controlled experiments on two GPU families spanning the memory-bound to compute-bound regimes (A10G, ridge $\approx$ 117 FLOP/byte; A100, ridge $\approx$ 183 FLOP/byte), we demonstrate: (1) the inference-optimal vocabulary shifts 16x with serving batch, from 32k at $B=1$ to 524k at $B=64+$, driven by amortization of the $V \times d$ unembedding matrix read; (2) at 1.3-2.3B model scale, quality (bits per byte, BPB) is optimized at $V=65$k, confirming scale-dependent vocabulary preference; (3) the lifecycle-optimal vocabulary diverges from training-optimal by up to 16x for production deployments. Quality is approximately invariant across the optimal range ($<$2% BPB spread), making vocabulary a pure systems optimization with no quality penalty in the measured range. Our results provide actionable capacity planning guidance: on-device deployments ($B=1$) should use $V \approx 32$k; datacenter serving ($B \geq 64$, $λ\geq 10$) should use $V \approx 131$-262k.

</details>


### [89] [Is Per-Agent Policy Composition Safe? Rethinking Successor-Feature Transfer in Cooperative Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11658)
*Zijian Zhao,Sen Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multi-agent reinforcement learning extension of universal successor features that enables safe policy recombination across changing objectives for teams of agents, with guarantees against performance degradation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world RL systems (fleet management, traffic control) must adapt to dynamically changing objectives after deployment, but retraining policies for each new objective is prohibitively expensive. While single-agent solutions exist (successor features with generalized policy improvement), multi-agent transfer remains challenging because independent policy recombination can fail catastrophically due to teammate interactions.

Method: Proposes MA-USFA, a hierarchical two-layer method: 1) lower layer with universal successor feature approximators conditioned on teammates' objectives, and 2) an upper composer that coordinates which library entry each agent should follow, incorporating cross-agent corrections. The system is trained once over objective distributions and applied at deployment without per-task adaptation.

Result: Theoretical analysis shows independent composition can produce joint behavior strictly worse than any policy in the library due to teammate interactions invalidating value estimates. Synchronized composition is safe but inflexible. MA-USFA provides both safety guarantees and flexibility for serving objectives with different goals per agent.

Conclusion: MA-USFA addresses the critical multi-agent transfer problem by providing safe, flexible policy recombination across changing objectives, overcoming limitations of both independent composition (unsafe) and synchronized composition (inflexible), enabling practical deployment in real-world multi-agent systems.

Abstract: Many reinforcement learning systems, from fleet management to traffic signal control, must serve an objective that changes dynamically after deployment, and retraining a policy for each new objective is prohibitively expensive. For a single agent, this problem is well understood: successor features with generalized policy improvement, together with their universal extension, recombine a library of learned policies into a policy for any new objective, with a guarantee that the result is never worse than any policy in the library. However, multi-agent transfer has received far less attention, and the common practice of letting each agent recombine its own library independently inherits the recipe but not the guarantee. We prove that this independent composition can produce joint behavior strictly worse than every policy in the library, because recombining teammates changes the environment each agent faces and invalidates the values it relies on, a failure with no single-agent counterpart. We further show that the only unconditionally safe fixed rule is synchronized composition, which moves the whole team to one jointly trained policy but cannot serve objectives that assign different goals to different agents. To attain safety and flexibility at once, we propose MA-USFA, a hierarchical method with two layers: a lower layer of universal successor feature approximators that predicts each agent's successor features while conditioned on its teammates' objectives, and an upper composer that selects, across agents, which library entry each agent should follow and supplies the cross-agent correction a per-agent value cannot represent. Trained once over the distribution of objectives, it is applied at deployment with no per-task adaptation.

</details>


### [90] [PAIR: Pairwise-Aware Inclusion Reweighting for Adaptive Rollout Allocation in RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11368)
*Pixel Nomand,Elena Voss,Marcus Hale,Sofia Reyes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PAIR (Pairwise-Aware Inclusion Reweighting) is a new allocation method for RLVR that uses pairwise contrast information between rollouts to create unbiased gradient estimators, reducing token usage while improving accuracy compared to pointwise allocators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RLVR allocators use pointwise difficulty/utility metrics, but the statistical reality is that the gradient is a second-order U-statistic over rollout pairs. Completing one rollout reveals contrasts with others, and adaptive selection changes which pair terms are observable, creating a statistical mismatch.

Method: PAIR treats short rollout prefixes as vertices and pair-gradient terms as edges in a contrast graph. Uses: 1) prefix-only predictor for correctness and token cost estimation, 2) convex design for positive continuation probabilities under budget constraints, 3) inverse-weighting of edges by logged joint inclusion probability.

Result: On compute-matched RLVR runs with Qwen3-1.7B/4B models, PAIR improves average accuracy by +1.2 and +1.4 over the strongest pointwise allocator while using 51% and 52% fewer tokens than full-group GRPO. Audit confirms unweighted adaptive selection is biased, while PAIR recovers complete-pair target at matched suffix cost.

Conclusion: Pairwise-aware allocation through PAIR addresses the statistical mismatch in RLVR by properly accounting for pairwise contrast information, creating design-unbiased estimators that improve efficiency and accuracy compared to pointwise approaches.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) spends most of its compute generating groups of long reasoning trajectories. Recent allocators reduce this cost by assigning budgets to prompts, rollouts, or tokens according to a pointwise notion of difficulty or utility. We identify a statistical mismatch: the unclipped leave-one-out group-relative score gradient is not a sum of independent point contributions, but a second-order U-statistic over pairs of rollouts. Completing one rollout therefore reveals contrast with every other completed rollout, and adaptive endpoint selection changes which pair terms are observable. We introduce PAIR (Pairwise-Aware Inclusion Reweighting), which treats short rollout prefixes as vertices and pair-gradient terms as edges of a contrast graph. A prefix-only predictor estimates correctness and remaining token cost; a convex design chooses positive continuation probabilities under an expected suffix-token budget; and each edge induced by completed vertices is inverse-weighted by its logged joint inclusion probability. Under conditionally independent on-policy rollouts and an unclipped, unstandardized objective, the resulting estimator is design-unbiased for the complete candidate-pair gradient. Across compute-matched RLVR runs on Qwen3-1.7B/4B, PAIR improves average accuracy by +1.2 and +1.4 over the strongest pointwise allocator while using 51% and 52% fewer generated tokens than full-group GRPO. A frozen-population estimator audit confirms that unweighted adaptive selection is biased, whereas pair-inclusion correction recovers the complete-pair target at matched suffix cost.

</details>


### [91] [Towards an approach to multivariate outlier detection for District Heating System data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11375)
*Rajko Turudija,Dušan Stojiljković,Milan Zdravković,Marko Ignjatović*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Testing and comparing multiple outlier detection methods for district heating system data to identify irregular plant operation, with ensemble approach providing best results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To uncover irregular plant operation in district heating systems with the wider objective of reducing gas consumption and CO2 emissions in central heating plants.

Method: Tested multiple multivariate outlier detection methods on transmitted heat energy data with outside temperature: Z-score (univariate benchmark), Mahalanobis distances, PCA, Isolation Forest, and Hotelling's T-squared test. Considered domain-specific circumstances like zero energy timepoints indicating off-grid status.

Result: PCA, Isolation Forest, and Hotelling method provided relevant results. Domain experts discussed outcomes. Ensemble method based on agreement of all three best-performing methods was adopted as final approach.

Conclusion: An ensemble approach combining PCA, Isolation Forest, and Hotelling's T-squared test is effective for detecting outliers in district heating system data, helping identify irregular operations for energy and emissions reduction.

Abstract: In this paper, we test different methods for multivariate detection of outliers in the data of transmitted heat energy in the selected substation of local District Heating System, by also considering outside ambient temperature, namely Z-score (univariate, as a benchmark), Mahalanobis distances, Principal Component Analysis (PCA), Isolation Forest and Hotelling's T-squared test. The overall research aims at uncovering irregular plant operation, with a wider objective of identifying the opportunities for reducing the consumption of gas in central heating plants as well as the CO2 emission. The proposed approach considers specific domain circumstances, such as irrelevance of zero transmit-ted energy timepoints as indication of off-grid plant. The outcomes of the different methods are discussed with domain experts. It was concluded that PCA, Isolation Forest and Hotelling method provide relevant results. Finally, we adopt the ensemble method (selection based on the agreement of all three methods on the detected outliers) as the final approach.

</details>


### [92] [Reoptimization Algorithms for Contextual Bandits with Knapsack Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11383)
*Zhen Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Extension of UCB algorithms for Contextual Bandits with Knapsack achieves O((ln T)^3/T) average regret using re-optimization techniques, significantly improving over previous O(1/√T) bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a need for better algorithms for Contextual Bandits with Knapsack problems where decisions involve assigning customers to products made from limited resources, and current approaches for related dynamic-pricing problems only achieve O(1/√T) regret bounds using re-optimization techniques.

Method: Proposes a natural extension of the Upper-Confidence-Bound (UCB) family of algorithms combined with re-optimization techniques. The approach jointly learns the unknown mean reward function while making online assignments of customers to products under resource capacity constraints.

Result: The algorithm achieves an average regret of O((ln T)^3/T) where T is the horizon length, which significantly reduces the O(1/√T) bound in the literature for closely related dynamic-pricing problems.

Conclusion: Re-optimization techniques combined with UCB algorithms provide substantially improved regret bounds for Contextual Bandits with Knapsack problems, demonstrating the effectiveness of this approach for resource-constrained online decision making with unknown reward functions.

Abstract: We study new algorithms for Contextual Bandits with Knapsack. In these problems, there are finitely many types of customers, products, and resources. Each product is made from a fixed combination of resources, and resources have finite capacity. A decision maker must assign each arriving customer one out of a set of multiple possible products. Every assignment of a customer to a product will generate a random reward, which equals an unknown linear function of customer and product features, plus a noise term. The objective is to jointly learn the mean reward function, and to make online assignments to minimize the expected revenue loss relative to an optimal policy that knows the reward function. We propose a natural and simple extension of the Upper-Confidence-Bound (UCB) family of algorithms and apply re-optimization techniques. We show that by taking advantage of re-optimization, our algorithm achieves an average regret of $O(\frac{(\ln T)^3}{T})$ where $T$ is the horizon length. Our bound significantly reduces the $O(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}})$ bound in the literature for closely related dynamic-pricing problems that are based on re-optimization.

</details>


### [93] [Mechanism Design for Generative Engines: From Exploitation toward Win-Win Outcomes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11390)
*Chen Xu,Zitian Guo,Chenyan Xiong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Citation wars emerge in generative AI ecosystems as content providers optimize for model citations while platforms aim to preserve answer quality, leading to a proposed verifiable-content rewards mechanism to align incentives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative engines are reshaping the web by making citations crucial for attention, attribution, and value allocation. This creates strategic tension: content providers optimize for model citations while platforms must maintain answer quality and trustworthy attribution, potentially leading to citation wars.

Method: The study formulates the supplier-platform interaction as a repeated Stackelberg game with partial monitoring. It conducts repeated simulations where state-of-the-art generative engine optimization (GEO) attacks adapt to conventional defenses, and proposes a VCR (verifiable-content rewards) mechanism that credits rewrites surfacing checkable factual substance.

Result: Experiments on three benchmarks show that VCR consistently achieves the largest Net defense-utility score, outperforming the strongest baseline by an average of 12.1 percentage points, and produces a win-win outcome under the empirical equivalence criterion.

Conclusion: Citation competition can escalate into wars where GEO attacks degrade document quality with unsupported claims. The proposed VCR mechanism aligns creator incentives with answer trustworthiness by rewarding verifiable content rather than just penalizing suspicious rewrites, offering a solution to the strategic tension between content providers and platforms.

Abstract: Generative engines are reshaping the web ecosystem by making citations a key mechanism for allocating attention, attribution, and downstream value. This creates a strategic tension: content providers are incentivized to optimize for model citation, while platforms must preserve answer quality and trustworthy attribution. We show that this tension can escalate into citation wars. In repeated simulations, state-of-the-art generative engine optimization (GEO) attacks adapt to conventional defenses by producing citation-seeking rewrites that degrade document quality and introduce unsupported claims. To study this problem, we formulate the supplier--platform interaction as a repeated Stackelberg game with partial monitoring. A local best-response analysis identifies when citation competition approaches an inert stationary outcome. Motivated by this finding, we propose a platform--creator mechanism called VCR based on verifiable-content rewards. Rather than only penalizing suspicious rewrites, the platform also credits rewrites that surface checkable factual substance, aligning creator incentives with answer trustworthiness. Experiments on three benchmarks show that VCR consistently achieves the largest Net defense-utility score, outperforming the strongest baseline by an average of 12.1 percentage points, and produces a win--win outcome under our empirical equivalence criterion.

</details>


### [94] [Unmasking Toxic Mimicry in Medical Offline Reinforcement Learning for ICU Sepsis Management via Counterfactual Clinical Audits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11410)
*Hangqi Ren,Junyi Liao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Counterfactual Clinical Audit (CCA) to detect Toxic Mimicry in offline RL for ICU treatment, showing standard metrics like MSE/FQE can't catch harmful pattern replication, and demonstrates CCA reveals dangerous misalignments in Medical Decision Transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current offline RL evaluation metrics (MSE and FQE) can't detect Toxic Mimicry - the harmful replication of treatment patterns like treatment withdrawal during comfort-care transitions. This creates a dangerous gap between statistical performance and clinical safety in medical RL applications.

Method: Proposes Counterfactual Clinical Audit (CCA) framework that stress-tests RL agents through physiological perturbations anchored in Surviving Sepsis Campaign guidelines. Applied to MIMIC-III database, auditing Medical Decision Transformer (MedDT) vs Historical Causal Transformer (HCT-RL) with Causal Action Shielding, propensity-based weighting, and Conservative Q-Learning.

Result: CCA revealed MedDT paradoxically reduces vasopressor dosage as lactate escalates (contradicting resuscitation guidelines), while HCT-RL maintains physiologically consistent responses. This exposes systemic misalignment between statistical fit and clinical safety that standard metrics miss.

Conclusion: Counterfactual clinical audits are necessary evaluation standards for medical RL to detect dangerous misalignments that traditional metrics overlook, bridging the gap between statistical performance and clinical safety in critical care applications.

Abstract: Offline reinforcement learning (RL) offers considerable promise for optimizing ICU treatment decisions, yet standard evaluation metrics Mean Squared Error (MSE) and Fitted Q-Evaluation (FQE) assess only behavioral imitation and cannot detect Toxic Mimicry, a failure mode in which agents replicate harmful patterns such as treatment withdrawal during comfort-care transitions. Using the MIMIC-III database, we propose the Counterfactual Clinical Audit (CCA) framework, which stress-tests RL agents through physiological perturbations anchored in Surviving Sepsis Campaign (SSC) guidelines. We audit a Medical Decision Transformer (MedDT) and a Historical Causal Transformer (HCT-RL), the latter employing Causal Action Shielding, propensity-based importance weighting, and Conservative Q-Learning. CCA reveals that MedDT paradoxically reduces vasopressor dosage as lactate escalates, contradicting resuscitation guidelines, while HCT-RL maintains physiologically consistent responses. These findings expose a systemic misalignment between statistical fit and clinical safety, supporting counterfactual audits as a necessary evaluation standard for medical RL.

</details>


### [95] [Diffusion-Based Data-Driven Assortment Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11419)
*Junyi Liao,Xiaohui Jiang,Zhengwei Tong,Ethan X. Fang,Vahid Tarokh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a model-agnostic assortment optimization framework using guided discrete diffusion to generate high-quality, diverse product assortments without parametric choice model assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional parametric choice models (like MNL) for assortment optimization are sensitive to model misspecification and struggle to capture complex customer behavior, requiring a more robust, data-driven approach.

Method: Represents assortments as binary vectors and performs stochastic search via learned reverse diffusion process. Uses reward-guided mechanism to bias local transitions using revenue estimates, balancing exploration and exploitation.

Result: Consistently identifies high-quality assortments, remains robust under model misspecification, often recovers near-optimal solutions in high-dimensional settings, and produces diverse high-performing assortments.

Conclusion: Demonstrates generative modeling as a scalable and robust paradigm for combinatorial optimization in data-driven decision-making, offering flexibility beyond single deterministic solutions.

Abstract: Assortment optimization is a fundamental problem in revenue management, typically addressed using parametric choice models such as the multinomial logit (MNL) and its variants. While these models enable tractable formulations, their performance is sensitive to model misspecification and often struggles to capture complex customer behavior. In this paper, we propose a model-agnostic framework for assortment optimization based on guided discrete diffusion. We represent assortments as binary vectors and perform stochastic search via a learned reverse diffusion process, avoiding explicit combinatorial enumeration. To incorporate decision objectives, we introduce a reward-guided mechanism that biases local transitions using estimates of expected revenue. This allows the method to effectively balance exploration and exploitation during generation. Empirically, we show that the proposed approach consistently identifies high-quality assortments and remains robust under model misspecification, often recovering near-optimal solutions in high-dimensional settings. Moreover, the generative nature of diffusion enables the production of diverse high-performing assortments, offering flexibility beyond a single deterministic solution. These results highlight the potential of generative modeling as a scalable and robust paradigm for combinatorial optimization in data-driven decision-making.

</details>


### [96] [Analysis of Federated Aggregation under Model Poisoning and Backdoor Attacks: A Reconstructed Cross-Dataset and Cross-Architecture Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11423)
*Soumya Mazumdar,Vineet Kumar Rakesh,Tapas Samanta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive evaluation of federated aggregation methods across multiple conditions reveals Trimmed Mean achieves best clean performance while Krum performs best under adversarial conditions, with important caveats about metric definitions and provenance limitations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust comparisons of federated aggregation methods require joint consideration of predictive performance, threat definitions, metric semantics, and execution provenance, highlighting the need for comprehensive evaluation frameworks.

Method: Reconstructed a 500-cell seed-1 evaluation matrix across five aggregation methods, five datasets, five architectures, and four conditions (clean, sign-flipping, Gaussian, BadNets). Analyzed 454 original runs and 36 repaired executions with audit of BadNets metric implementation and FedPARETO scaffold.

Result: Trimmed Mean achieved highest clean macro-mean accuracy (76.02%) and lowest mean within-task rank (1.70). Krum attained highest accuracy under sign-flipping and Gaussian attacks. Relative rankings remained consistent across available task pairs.

Conclusion: Findings should be interpreted as descriptive comparisons within recorded configurations, not as statistical estimates or universal robustness claims, due to single-seed evaluations and incomplete lineage documentation. Important metric definition discrepancies were identified.

Abstract: Robust comparisons of federated aggregation methods require joint consideration of predictive performance, threat definitions, metric semantics, and execution provenance. A 500-cell seed-1 evaluation matrix was reconstructed across five aggregation methods, five datasets, five architectures, and four recorded conditions: clean, sign-flipping, Gaussian, and BadNets. Successful execution logs were identified for 454 original runs and 36 repaired or rerun executions, whereas 10 clean SVHN cells were supported by summary-only provenance. Trimmed Mean achieved the highest clean macro-mean accuracy (76.02%) and the lowest mean within-task rank (1.70). Krum attained the highest recorded accuracy under both sign-flipping and Gaussian configurations. These relative rankings remained unchanged when analysis was restricted to 21 task pairs for which original successful logs were available for every method-condition combination. Audit of the supplied BadNets metric implementation established that every test input is triggered prior to target-label counting; consequently, the retained metric represents Triggered Target-Label Rate (TTLR) rather than a conventional target-excluding attack success rate. An audit of the supplied FedPARETO scaffold further identified a pathway in which predictive summaries may characterize an uncorrupted local model while the aggregation weight is applied to a separately corrupted update, introducing a potential discrepancy between reported predictive outcomes and the updates used for aggregation. The canonical matrix contains a single identified seed for each cell, and exact attack and configuration lineage is incomplete. Accordingly, the findings should be interpreted as descriptive comparisons within the recorded configurations and not as statistical estimates or universal claims regarding robustness.

</details>


### [97] [Click2Poly: A VLM for vector mapping buildings and walls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11424)
*Nicolas Girard,Jawher Ben Abdallah,Arno Gobbin,Liuyun Duan,Sacha Lepretre*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Click2Poly is a human-in-the-loop AI assistant that speeds up manual editing of building and wall vector layers by using a Vision Language Model to respond to user clicks for direct vector editing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate vector mapping of buildings and walls is critical for geospatial applications but remains labor-intensive. While deep learning methods have improved automatic extraction, they still require human quality control and manual fixing to meet cartographic standards.

Method: Click2Poly extends the Florence-2 Vision Language Model (VLM) to respond to user clicks by editing building or wall vector layers directly. It's implemented as a QGIS plugin for use in real-world production environments.

Result: Click2Poly successfully speeds up manual editing of building and wall vector layers in real-world production environments, providing a practical human-in-the-loop AI assistant for geospatial vector editing.

Conclusion: The Click2Poly system demonstrates an effective human-in-the-loop approach that bridges automated extraction and cartographic standards by leveraging VLMs for interactive vector editing, significantly reducing manual labor in geospatial applications.

Abstract: Accurate vector mapping of buildings and walls is critical for geospatial applications but remains a labor-intensive process. While recent deep learning methods have improved automatic extraction, in order to meet cartographic standards they always require a human to perform quality control and fix complex cases in the extraction. We present Click2Poly, a human-in-the-loop AI assistant designed to speed up this manual step. Extending the Florence-2 Vision Language Model (VLM), Click2Poly responds to user clicks by editing the building or wall vector layer directly. Implemented as a QGIS plugin, Click2Poly speeds up the manual editing of building and wall vector layers in a real-world production environment.

</details>


### [98] [Three Tokens Force Exponential Feature Rank in Nonnegative Kernel Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11427)
*Vicente Opazo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Kernel attention requires exponentially more features than full attention for certain tasks, with lower bounds approaching exact exponential requirements as context length grows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the fundamental differences between full attention (which exposes every token pair) and kernel attention (which compresses sequences into fixed-dimensional sketches), particularly focusing on when kernel attention becomes exponentially expensive.

Method: Theoretical analysis comparing kernel attention vs full attention on Min-IP over Boolean inputs; proves exponential lower bounds for kernel attention with nonnegative kernels; studies dense softmax as comparison; extends analysis to position-dependent token maps and causal settings.

Result: 1) Rank-one normalized kernel attention solves sequences of length ≤ 2 exactly; 2) For 3-token sequences with error < 1/2, nonnegative kernel attention requires 2^{Ω(m)} features; 3) Dense softmax solves same task with m-dimensional scores and constant temperature; 4) For deterministic multihead multilayer sketch models with finite alphabet cross-token channels, proves linear transcript lower bounds.

Conclusion: Kernel attention fundamentally differs from full attention in requiring exponentially more features for certain tasks, with these requirements approaching exact exponential realizations as context length increases, establishing concrete computational limitations of kernel-based attention mechanisms.

Abstract: Full attention exposes every token pair, whereas kernel attention compresses a sequence into a fixed-dimensional sketch. We show that this distinction becomes exponential at the first context length containing two competing candidates. On Min-IP over Boolean inputs, rank-one normalized kernel attention solves every sequence of length at most two exactly. In contrast, any single normalized nonnegative kernel-attention head that succeeds on all three-token sequences with error strictly below $1/2$ requires $2^{Ω(m)}$ features, even with arbitrary finite-dimensional tokenwise values and an arbitrary query-dependent affine readout. Dense softmax solves the same task with $m$-dimensional scores and constant temperature. The conclusion survives position-dependent token maps and a causal final query. As context length grows, the lower bound approaches the exact $2^m$-feature realization. Separately, for deterministic multihead, multilayer sketch models whose cross-token channels have finite alphabets, we prove a transcript lower bound linear in the number of independent answers and logarithmic in their alphabet size.

</details>


### [99] [AutoGrable: What Is a Good Graph for a Table?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11431)
*Tamara Cucumides,Floris Geerts*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AutoGrable is a method to automatically construct graphs from relational data by selecting columns that create meaningful partitions of rows without requiring GNN training, using a label-alignment risk score to evaluate candidate graph constructions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current graph learning approaches require manually constructing graphs from relational data (tables/databases), which involves arbitrary decisions about nodes, edges, and relations. Existing methods either rely on heuristics or require training models on every candidate graph, which is inefficient.

Method: AutoGrable uses a label-alignment risk criterion that doesn't require training graph models. It treats each row as a node and evaluates candidate column subsets based on how well their induced partitions separate rows with different labels while keeping rows with same labels together. The method scores subsets using held-out risk of the best constant predictor on blocks, penalized by an occupancy term.

Result: Experiments show: 1) The score effectively filters out poor candidate graphs while retaining good ones; 2) AutoGrable recovers label-generating columns in controlled tasks; 3) It outperforms fixed, random, and task-aware constructors on real tasks; 4) It can decline to build graphs when none would help.

Conclusion: AutoGrable provides an efficient, model-free approach to automatic graph construction from relational data that avoids GNN training, works for both single tables and foreign-key schemas, and can intelligently decide when graph construction is beneficial.

Abstract: Graph learning presupposes a graph, and tables and relational databases do not come with one. Applying a GNN to them requires deciding which entities become nodes, which of them to connect, and through which relations---a decision made by hand, by schema heuristics, or by training a model on every candidate graph and keeping the best. We give a criterion that requires no trained graph model. In the minimal table-to-graph abstraction each row is a node, so a message-passing GNN, bounded by 1-WL, sees a construction only as a partition of the rows into colour-refinement classes: a construction is good for a task when that partition separates rows with different labels and does not split rows that share one. AutoGrable turns this criterion into a construction procedure. For incidence constructions the partition is fixed by the selected columns, so building a graph reduces to choosing them, and we score a candidate subset by a label-alignment risk: the held-out risk of the best predictor constant on its blocks, penalised by an occupancy term measuring how thinly the blocks are populated. The score materialises no graph and trains no GNN, so AutoGrable can search the space of subsets greedily and cheaply, and returns the resulting grable for single tables and for foreign-key schemas alike. Our experiments show that over a space of candidate graphs the score discards a large fraction while retaining the best; that AutoGrable recovers the columns that generate the label on controlled tasks and outperforms fixed, random, and task-aware constructors on real tasks under a fixed predictor; and that it is the only method compared that can decline to build a graph when none helps.

</details>


### [100] [Variational Parameter Calibration with Physics-Aware Latent-Space Surrogates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11435)
*Qiyao Zhou,Xujia Zhu,Pierre Joli,Yu Cong,Sibo Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A differentiable autoencoder framework for reduced-order modeling that enables both accurate forward flow prediction and effective parameter calibration by incorporating observable supervision to maintain parameter-correlated information in latent representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing surrogate models lack systematic end-to-end differentiable formulations that can effectively couple deep-learning-based reduced-order models with variational parameter estimation, especially for parametric dynamical systems requiring both accurate state prediction and informative parameter calibration.

Method: Proposed a physics-aware neural network latent-space framework using autoencoder architecture that maps physical parameters to flow fields through differentiable latent representations. Uses observable supervision during training to encourage latent variables to retain parameter-correlated information, and solves inverse problems through surrogate-induced observation operators.

Result: The framework demonstrates that reconstruction accuracy alone is insufficient for inverse modeling due to lack of end-to-end differentiability or physics awareness. Observable supervision improves latent-space case-level separability and temporal organization. Experiments show robustness with various realistic measurement settings (noisy, low-resolution, partial observations) and reduced calibration error/variability compared to standard surrogate models.

Conclusion: The proposed differentiable physics-aware latent-space framework effectively bridges forward modeling and variational parameter estimation, demonstrating that systematic end-to-end differentiability and observable supervision are crucial for accurate parameter calibration beyond just state reconstruction accuracy.

Abstract: Forward and inverse modeling of parametric dynamical systems requires surrogate models that are not only accurate for state prediction, but also informative for parameter calibration. However, a systematic end-to-end differentiable formulation for coupling deep-learning-based reduced-order surrogates with variational parameter estimation remains underdeveloped. In this work, we introduce a physics-aware neural-network-based latent-space framework for reduced-order forward modeling and variational parameter estimation. The proposed autoencoder-based approach yields a differentiable surrogate that maps physical parameters to predicted flow fields through a latent representation. The observable supervision is used during offline training to encourage the latent variables to retain information correlated with system parameters, while the online inverse problem is solved in the parameter space through the surrogate-induced observation operator. The method is evaluated on two computational-fluid-dynamics benchmarks. The results show that reconstruction accuracy alone is insufficient for inverse modeling, owing to the lack of end-to-end differentiability or physics awareness for variational parameter calibration. Quantitative latent-space analysis further shows that observable supervision improves case-level separability and temporal organization of latent representations. Experiments with realistic measurement settings, including noisy, low-resolution, randomly masked, and block-wise partial observations, demonstrate the robustness of the proposed framework and show that it generally reduces calibration error and variability compared with the standard surrogate models.

</details>


### [101] [XGBoost "is all you need": the case of forecasting transmitted heat energy in District Heating Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11446)
*Milan Zdravković*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: XGBoost outperforms LSTM for heat energy forecasting in district heating systems, offering better performance with lower computational cost and environmental impact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To compare conventional ML (XGBoost) vs deep learning (LSTM) for time series forecasting in district heating systems, examining when traditional ML outperforms deep learning and evaluating computational cost and environmental benefits.

Method: Comparative study using two approaches - XGBoost and LSTM neural networks - applied to a real-world District Heating Systems dataset for forecasting transmitted heat energy. The analysis includes experimentation, performance evaluation, and error distribution analysis.

Result: XGBoost consistently outperformed LSTM in heat energy forecasting. Analysis revealed LSTM makes more significant errors in intervals with less data availability. XGBoost requires reduced computational demands leading to cost savings and minimized carbon footprint.

Conclusion: For this specific forecasting task in district heating systems, conventional ML (XGBoost) is superior to deep learning (LSTM) in both performance and efficiency, highlighting the importance of choosing appropriate algorithms based on task requirements and environmental considerations.

Abstract: This paper presents a comparative study of two distinct approaches, XGBoost and Long-Short Term Memory (LSTM), for forecasting transmitted heat energy in District Heating Systems (DHS). The objective is to explore scenarios in which conventional ML algorithms demonstrate better performance over deep learning networks in time series forecasting and the associated benefits in terms of computational cost and environmental impact. The study focuses on a real-world DHS dataset. Through experimentation and analysis, it is demonstrated that XGBoost consistently outperforms LSTM in this specific forecasting task. The difference is explained by the error distribution illustrating that LSTM makes more significant errors in the intervals of less data availability. The reduced computational demands of conventional ML approaches not only result in cost savings but also minimize the carbon footprint associated with data analysis tasks in energy systems.

</details>


### [102] [PAC-Bayes Beyond Parameter Space: Behavioral Equivalence, Z-Information, and Exact Complexity Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11465)
*Vasant G. Honavar,Satish Kumar Keshri,Neil Ashtekar,Zehao Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper decomposes classical PAC-Bayes KL divergence into behavioral and realization components, introducing Z-information to measure the gap between predictive behavior uncertainty and internal realization variation in over-parameterized systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical PAC-Bayes theory doesn't distinguish between uncertainty over predictive behavior and variation among behaviorally equivalent internal realizations, which is problematic in over-parameterized systems where many distinct configurations produce identical predictions.

Method: Formalize behavioral equivalence through measurable behavior maps, use measure disintegration to decompose probability measures into distributions over predictive behaviors and conditional distributions over behavioral fibers, yielding exact decomposition of PAC-Bayes KL divergence.

Result: Exact structural decomposition of classical PAC-Bayes complexity into behavior-selection term and realization-level term, introduction of Z-information as the gap measure, variational characterization of behavior-selection term, and demonstration of natural symmetry and invariance properties.

Conclusion: Predictive behavior is the natural object of PAC-Bayes complexity, and the decomposition provides a refined understanding of generalization guarantees in over-parameterized systems by separating behavioral uncertainty from internal realization variation.

Abstract: PAC-Bayes theory provides generalization guarantees by controlling the Kullback--Leibler (KL) divergence between posterior and prior distributions over a chosen hypothesis representation. However, predictive risk depends only on the predictive behavior induced by a hypothesis, not on the particular internal realization that implements that behavior. In over-parameterized systems, many distinct configurations induce identical predictive behavior, yet the classical PAC-Bayes KL divergence does not distinguish uncertainty over predictive behavior from variation among behaviorally equivalent realizations.
  We show that this distinction induces an exact structural decomposition of classical PAC-Bayes complexity. We formalize behavioral equivalence through a measurable behavior map and use measure disintegration to decompose probability measures on the configuration space into a distribution over predictive behaviors and conditional distributions over behavioral fibers. This yields an exact decomposition of the classical PAC-Bayes KL divergence into a behavior-selection term and a realization-level term given by an expected conditional KL within fibers.
  We define Z-information as the negative of this realization-level contribution: the exact gap between the KL divergence and the complexity of uncertainty over predictive behavior alone. We further show that the behavior-selection term admits an exact variational characterization: it is the minimum KL divergence among all posteriors inducing the same distribution over predictive behaviors, attained by a canonical fiber-symmetrized representative.
  Finally, we show that symmetry, behavior-preserving directions, fiber geometry, and invariance under fiber-preserving perturbations arise naturally from the same behavior-map structure. Together, these results identify predictive behavior as the natural object of PAC-Bayes complexity.

</details>
