### [103] [Dual-Primal Graph VAEs for Noisy Label Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11473)
*Patrick Stinson,Nikolaus Kriegeskorte*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A graph VAE architecture using GAT-based message passing for unsupervised ground-truth inference from noisy crowdsourced labels, achieving SOTA performance and allowing integration of side information like neural network representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neural network methods for crowdsourcing either rely on simple generative models or require pseudo-labels/synthetic data to train aggregate classifiers. There's a need for more sophisticated, fully unsupervised methods that don't need separate classifier training.

Method: Proposed a graph VAE architecture where decoder and encoder use GAT-based message passing on the crowdsourcing dataset's adjacency graph and its dual. Ground-truth labels are treated as latent variables, enabling unsupervised representation learning without separate classifier training.

Result: The model achieves state-of-the-art performance on crowdsourcing benchmarks. Additionally, shows how the crowdsourcing graph can be augmented with side information (neural network classifier representations trained on noisy labels) to substantially boost classification performance at test time.

Conclusion: The proposed graph VAE approach provides an effective unsupervised method for inferring ground-truth from noisy crowdsourced labels, offering both superior performance and flexibility to incorporate additional side information for improved results.

Abstract: Inferring the ground-truth from noisy crowdsourced labels is an important theoretical and practical problem. Neural network-based methods offer an alternative to classical Bayesian models which require specifying a family of generative models used for inference. However, current models either still rely on fairly simple generative models for inference or require pseudo-labels or synthetic data to train the aggregate classifier. We propose a graph VAE architecture in which the decoder and encoder use GAT-based message passing on the adjacency graph of a crowdsourced dataset and its dual, respectively. The ground-truth labels are treated as latent variables, enabling unsupervised representation learning without needing to train a separate classifier. We show our model achieves state of the art performance on crowdsourcing benchmarks. We then demonstrate the generality of our approach by showing how the original crowdsourcing graph can be augmented to incorporate side information such as representations from neural network classifiers trained on the noisy labels to substantially boost their classification performance at test time.

</details>


### [104] [Convergence Guarantees of Gradient Descent for Neural Networks via Generalized Lipschitz Smoothness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11479)
*Siqiao Mu,Diego Klabjan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Gradient descent converges for general feedforward neural networks without special initialization or dataset requirements, achieving O(1/T^{1/L}) convergence rate for L-layer networks by leveraging Lipschitz properties of activation functions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing convergence guarantees for neural network training often require special initialization conditions, specific architecture constraints, or ideal datasets. The paper aims to establish more general convergence guarantees that work for arbitrary network width/depth without special requirements.

Method: The authors prove that Lipschitz properties of activation functions (smoothness, continuity, linear boundedness) are partially preserved through repeated compositions, leading to a novel generalized Lipschitz smoothness condition. This condition bounds gradient changes by parameter changes multiplied by polynomial terms of parameter norms, enabling a descent lemma for convergence analysis.

Result: For L-layer neural networks with Lipschitz smooth activation functions (linear, tanh, softplus, sigmoid) and mean-squared error loss, gradient descent achieves convergence where the minimum squared gradient norm decreases to zero at rate O(1/T^{1/L}) in T iterations, without requiring special initialization or dataset conditions.

Conclusion: The paper establishes strong convergence guarantees for gradient descent on general feedforward neural networks, showing that standard Lipschitz properties of activation functions are sufficient to ensure convergence without restrictive assumptions on architecture, initialization, or data distribution.

Abstract: We establish convergence guarantees of gradient descent for general feedforward neural networks of arbitrary width or depth, with no special requirements on the initialization or dataset. We only assume that the activation functions are Lipschitz smooth, Lipschitz continuous, and linearly bounded--- properties that hold for linear, tanh, softplus, and sigmoid activation functions. For the loss function, we require that it is Lipschitz smooth in the model outputs, which is true for mean-squared error. The key theoretical insight is that the Lipschitz properties of the activation functions are partially preserved even through repeated compositions, leading to a novel generalized Lipschitz smoothness condition where the change in gradient is upper bounded by the change in the parameter space, multiplied by polynomial terms of the parameter norms at both endpoints. This type of condition holds for both the model function and the loss function, enabling a descent lemma where the loss decreases as long as the learning rate is small enough with respect to the parameter norms. By ensuring that the parameter norms do not grow too quickly to infinity, we prove that the minimum squared gradient norm converges to zero in $T$ iterations at rate $O(1/T^{1/L})$ for an $L$-layer neural network.

</details>


### [105] [Defending against Model Extraction for GNNs with Model Reprogramming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11495)
*Yan Wen,Zhenyi Wang,Heng Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GraphRP is a proactive defense framework that uses structure-aware model reprogramming to protect GNNs from model extraction attacks by creating a dynamic structural firewall that preserves utility for benign queries while degrading adversarial ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNNs deployed in MLaaS are vulnerable to model extraction attacks where adversaries steal intellectual property through API queries. Existing defenses suffer from "Euclidean bias" - they transfer image-based strategies to graphs, ignoring complex topological dependencies, resulting in severe utility degradation. Passive methods like watermarking also fail to prevent theft in real time.

Method: Proposed GraphRP framework repurposes Model Reprogramming for security. It introduces a Structure-Aware Gating Mechanism driven by learnable topological prototypes. This creates a dynamic "structural firewall" that selectively modulates the model's decision boundary: preserving fidelity for benign queries on the training manifold while maximizing Fisher Information along perturbation direction for adversarial queries.

Result: Extensive experiments on both hard-label and soft-label model extraction attacks demonstrate that GraphRP significantly degrades attack effectiveness while preserving benign utility. Under standard assumptions, the authors prove a lower bound on the attacker's estimation error that increases with the structural sensitivity of the reprogramming noise.

Conclusion: GraphRP provides an effective proactive defense against model extraction attacks on GNNs by bridging the gap between security and utility through structure-aware reprogramming, overcoming the limitations of existing Euclidean-biased defenses and passive protection methods.

Abstract: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) serve as the backbone for high-stakes applications in Machine-Learning-as-a-Service (MLaaS). Still, their black-box deployment exposes them to Model Extraction (ME) attacks, in which adversaries steal intellectual property by querying APIs. Existing defenses suffer from a critical ''Euclidean bias'': they transfer image-based strategies (e.g., random noise) to graphs, ignoring the complex topological dependencies between nodes, which often results in severe utility degradation. Passive methods like watermarking also fail to prevent theft in real time. To bridge this gap, we propose GraphRP (Graph Reprogramming Protection), a proactive defense framework that repurposes Model Reprogramming for security. Unlike static perturbations, GraphRP introduces a Structure-Aware Gating Mechanism driven by learnable topological prototypes. This creates a dynamic ''structural firewall'' that selectively modulates the model's decision boundary: it preserves fidelity for benign queries residing on the training manifold, while maximizing the Fisher Information along the perturbation direction for adversarial queries. Under standard assumptions (bounded loss, optimal attacker, and local second-order approximation), we prove a lower bound on the attacker's estimation error that increases with the structural sensitivity of the reprogramming noise. Extensive experiments on both hard-label and soft-label ME attacks demonstrate that GraphRP significantly degrades attack effectiveness while preserving benign utility.

</details>


### [106] [HyperFix: Combinatorial Nonlinear Correction for Task Vector Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11499)
*Hyo Seo Kim,Ren Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HyperFix: A hypernetwork that learns nonlinear corrections for merging task vectors across varying subsets without per-subset optimization, outperforming existing linear merging methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing task vector merging methods use scalar tuning for specific subsets, requiring repeated tuning across different subsets and restricting merging to linear rescaling, which is inefficient and limited when dealing with varying subsets of tasks to merge.

Method: Formulate task vector merging as a combinatorial correction problem and introduce HyperFix, a lightweight hypernetwork that predicts subset-conditioned nonlinear corrections in weight space. The hypernetwork is trained once on singleton, pair, and triple subsets from a task bank and generalizes to larger subsets without per-subset optimization.

Result: Experiments across diverse benchmarks show that HyperFix outperforms existing task vector merging methods while significantly reducing tuning costs, as it requires only one-time training rather than repeated per-subset optimization.

Conclusion: HyperFix provides an efficient and effective solution for task vector merging across varying subsets by learning nonlinear corrections from small task updates, overcoming the limitations of linear rescaling methods that require repeated tuning for each subset.

Abstract: Task vectors enable model merging without joint retraining. In practice, the subset of task vectors to be merged may vary, but many existing methods use scalar tuning for a particular subset, requiring repeated tuning across subsets and restricting task vector merging to linear rescaling. We therefore formulate merging across varying task subsets as a combinatorial correction problem and introduce HyperFix, a lightweight hypernetwork that predicts subset-conditioned nonlinear corrections in weight space. Trained once on singleton, pair, and triple subsets from a task bank, HyperFix generalizes to larger subsets without per-subset optimization. Our local perturbation analysis bounds the residual correction beyond linear merging and motivates learning it from small task updates. Experiments across diverse benchmarks show that HyperFix outperforms existing task vector merging methods while reducing tuning cost.

</details>


### [107] [RelShap: Relationally Consistent Shapley Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11508)
*Seungeun Lee,Joao Fonseca,Julia Stoyanovich*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RelShap is a framework that incorporates relational constraints into Shapley value computation for feature attribution, ensuring explanations only consider relationally valid data configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Shapley value-based feature attributions flatten relational data and assume feature independence, evaluating models on unrealistic feature combinations that violate underlying relational constraints, leading to misleading explanations.

Method: RelShap incorporates relational constraints and data provenance into Shapley value computation by restricting background data and coalition evaluation to relationally valid configurations. It exploits functional dependencies to create equivalence classes over feature coalitions, reducing runtime without changing Shapley values. The framework is estimator-agnostic and works with Kernel SHAP, Monte Carlo, and Leverage SHAP.

Result: Experiments across multiple datasets, models, and estimators show RelShap produces more faithful explanations that correctly identify dominant features in controlled settings where existing methods (Conditional SHAP and ManifoldShap) fail.

Conclusion: RelShap provides a principled approach to feature attribution for relational data by incorporating relational constraints, ensuring explanations are faithful to the data-generating process while maintaining compatibility with existing SHAP estimators.

Abstract: Machine learning pipelines commonly flatten relational data into single-table representations, discarding structural constraints. Widely used Shapley value-based feature attributions then rely on feature independence, evaluating the model on combinations that could never arise in the underlying data, producing misleading explanations. We propose RelShap, a framework that incorporates relational constraints and data provenance into Shapley value computation, restricting both background data and coalition evaluation to relationally valid configurations. The framework is estimator-agnostic and composes with Kernel SHAP, Monte Carlo, and Leverage SHAP without altering their sampling or weighting properties. Functional dependencies further induce equivalence classes over feature coalitions, which RelShap exploits to reduce runtime without changing Shapley values; we provide a combinatorial characterization of the expected speedup. Experiments across multiple datasets, models, and estimators show that RelShap produces explanations that are more faithful to the data-generating process, correctly identifying the dominant feature in controlled settings where existing methods, including Conditional SHAP and ManifoldShap, do not. Our code is available at: https://github.com/duneag2/relshap.

</details>


### [108] [Let it Cook: Learning to Wait in Sequential Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11511)
*Christopher Watson,Arjun Krishna,Dinesh Jayaraman,Rajeev Alur*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes training "waiting policies" in sequential decision making that learn when to pause action and sensing to conserve resources while maintaining task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In sequential decision making, agents typically observe and act at every timestep, but many real-world tasks (like brewing coffee) have periods where constant monitoring and control are unnecessary. Active participation wastes resources that could be conserved or redirected to other tasks.

Method: The authors formalize "learning to wait" as minimizing sensing and decision frequency without sacrificing task performance. They propose using reinforcement learning with lexicographically ordered objectives to train waiting policies that decide where and how long to wait, committing to wait actions that forgo sensing for set periods.

Result: The approach successfully learns waiting behaviors across 4 discrete-state household tasks and 3 continuous-state environments. It can adapt pre-trained policies to incorporate waiting where appropriate. Different tasks permit varying amounts of waiting without performance loss, with the approach consistently finding solutions with significant waiting - sometimes waiting for over 50% of task duration.

Conclusion: Learning to wait is feasible and beneficial for conserving agent resources in sequential decision-making tasks. Reinforcement learning with lexicographically ordered objectives can effectively train waiting policies that identify opportunities to pause sensing and action without compromising task performance, enabling more efficient resource utilization.

Abstract: In sequential decision making, an agent typically observes its environment and acts at every timestep. However, such active participation may not always be necessary; tasks such as brewing coffee include periods that are served equally well by letting the environment evolve without constant monitoring and control. During such periods, the agent could simply wait to conserve its resources, or redirect its attention to another task. We capitalize on these opportunities by training a "waiting policy" that decides where and how long to wait. This involves forgoing sensing to commit to a wait action, representing a deliberate pause for a set number of timesteps. We formalize "learning to wait" as minimizing the frequency of sensing and decision making without sacrificing task performance (e.g., the total amount of time to complete a task). To train a waiting policy, we propose an approach that employs reinforcement learning with lexicographically ordered objectives. In experiments across 4 discrete-state household tasks and 3 continuous-state environments, we show that our approach successfully learns waiting behaviors, and can adapt pre-trained policies to wait where appropriate. While different tasks permit different amounts of waiting without sacrificing task performance, our approach consistently finds solutions with significant waiting, sometimes waiting for over 50 percent of the task duration.

</details>


### [109] [FLARE++: Low-rank attention with dynamic attention routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11519)
*Vedant Puri,Yongjie Jessica Zhang,Levent Burak Kara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FLARE++ improves upon FLARE by replacing static learned latent queries with dynamic, input-conditioned queries while maintaining linear complexity through low-rank attention routing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full self-attention has quadratic cost limitations for high-resolution PDE problems on irregular domains. While FLARE reduces cost using learned latent queries, these static query templates cannot adapt to different inputs, restricting flexibility.

Method: FLARE++ introduces dynamic token routing by reusing the encoder to generate input-conditioned queries. Learned latent seeds drive an extra encode call to gather N input tokens into M input-conditioned queries, using only standard scaled dot-product attention calls while maintaining O(NM) complexity.

Result: FLARE++ improves fixed-query FLARE by 24% on average across standard PDE surrogate benchmarks and gains 2.3 points of average accuracy on Long Range Arena, while maintaining linear computational complexity.

Conclusion: Dynamic token routing through input-conditioned queries significantly improves performance over static query approaches while preserving computational efficiency, making it competitive for PDE surrogate tasks and general sequence modeling.

Abstract: Full self-attention is a strong token mixer for PDE surrogates on irregular domains, but its quadratic cost limits its use on high-resolution problems. Efficient latent-attention models such as the Fast Low-rank Attention Routing Engine (FLARE) avoid that cost by routing all N tokens through M << N learned latent queries, but those queries are parameters: once trained, the same learned query templates serve every input. We remove this restriction with FLARE++, a low-rank attention architecture with dynamic token routing. FLARE++ reuses FLARE's own encoder to build its routing queries: learned latent seeds drive one extra encode call that gathers the N input tokens into M input-conditioned queries, and those queries then determine how the same tokens are compressed and redistributed. This preserves FLARE's explicit low-rank factorization and linear O(NM) complexity, and expresses the complete routing operation with standard scaled dot-product attention (SDPA) calls alone. We also provide a multi-GPU context-parallel implementation that shards input tokens across devices without ever gathering the full token sequence on one of them. FLARE++ is competitive across a set of standard PDE surrogate benchmarks, improving on fixed-query FLARE by 24% on average, and it gains 2.3 points of average accuracy on Long Range Arena.

</details>


### [110] [Hierarchical Federated Transfer Learning in Digital Twin-Based Vehicular Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11532)
*Qasim Zia,Saide Zhu,Haoxin Wang,Zafar Iqbal,Yingshu Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Hierarchical Federated Transfer Learning (HFTL) for DT-VANETs to address data heterogeneity and sparsity through vehicle type-based clustering and data quality mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated Learning in DT-VANETs struggles with data heterogeneity and sparsity across different vehicle types, leading to suboptimal global model accuracy for precise predictions.

Method: Combines Federated Transfer Learning (FTL) for vehicle type-based clustering, proposes HFTL framework with cloud server model updates, intra-cluster federated transfer learning algorithms, and data quality score mechanism to prevent malicious vehicle influence.

Result: Experiments on real-world datasets with various performance metrics verify the effectiveness and efficiency of the proposed HFTL algorithm.

Conclusion: The proposed HFTL framework successfully addresses FL challenges in DT-VANETs through vehicle clustering and quality control mechanisms, improving global model accuracy while maintaining data privacy.

Abstract: In recent research on the Digital Twin-based Vehicular Ad hoc Network(DT-VANET), Federated Learning (FL) has shown its ability to provide data privacy. However, Federated learning struggles to adequately train a global model when confronted with data heterogeneity and data sparsity among vehicles, which ensure suboptimal accuracy in making precise predictions for different vehicle types. To address these challenges, this paper combines Federated Transfer Learning (FTL) to conduct vehicle clustering related to types of vehicles and proposes a novel Hierarchical Federated Transfer Learning (HFTL). We construct a framework for DT-VANET, along with two algorithms designed for cloud server model updates and intra-cluster federated transfer learning, to improve the accuracy of the global model. In addition, we developed a data quality score-based mechanism to prevent the global model from being affected by malicious vehicles. Lastly, detailed experiments on real-world datasets are conducted, considering different performance metrics that verify the effectiveness and efficiency of our algorithm.

</details>


### [111] [Robust Ambiguity Detection (RAD) From Model- and Feature-Space Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11541)
*Manya Singh,Mark T. Keane,Arjun Pakrashi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RAD (Robust Ambiguity Detection) is a framework that quantifies predictive ambiguity in ML models using Model-Space and Feature-Space Consistency scores, visualized through RAD Plots, to help identify and abstain from ambiguous predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ML models should remain predictively consistent under permissible variations (functionally equivalent models or minor input perturbations), and should abstain from or flag ambiguous predictions, especially in high-stakes scenarios. However, identifying such ambiguity is difficult once models are deployed.

Method: Proposes the RAD framework with two complementary metrics: Model-Space Consistency (robustness to model variations) and Feature-Space Consistency (robustness to input perturbations). These scores form the RAD Score-Pair visualized via RAD Plots to characterize ambiguity sources and suggest user actions.

Result: RAD was evaluated on synthetic datasets with controlled overlap and real-world datasets where ambiguity cannot be directly inspected. It demonstrated a downstream application where samples are ranked by RAD Pareto-Rank, and the most ambiguous ones are abstained from prediction, achieving performance comparable to existing rejection-based approaches.

Conclusion: RAD provides an interpretable framework for detecting and quantifying predictive ambiguity in ML models, enabling better decision-making about when to abstain from predictions, particularly important for high-stakes applications where model robustness and reliability are critical.

Abstract: Machine learning models should be robust, in the sense of remaining predictively consistent under permissible variations. A model's predictions should ideally remain unchanged when it is replaced by a functionally equivalent one, or when its inputs are subject to minor, admissible perturbations. If such changes alter a prediction significantly, then the prediction is "ambiguous" with respect to the model. Models should abstain from making such ambiguous predictions and/or should flag them for human inspection, especially in high-stakes decision-making scenarios. However, in practice, such ambiguity is not easy to identify once a model is deployed. Here, the Robust Ambiguity Detection (RAD) framework is advanced for quantifying predictive ambiguity using two complementary metrics: Model-Space Consistency and Feature-Space Consistency. These two scores, the RAD Score-Pair, visualised through the RAD Plot, provide an interpretable characterisation of the sources of ambiguity and the actions a user may consider in response. RAD is evaluated on synthetic datasets with systematically controlled overlap, as well as several real-world datasets where the level of ambiguity cannot be directly inspected. Finally, we demonstrate a downstream application of RAD where samples are ranked by their RAD Pareto-Rank and the most ambiguous are abstained from prediction, achieving performance comparable to existing rejection-based approaches.

</details>


### [112] [Certifying What Helps Customer-Return Timing: A Screen-and-Confirm Test for Conditioning Signals, and Why Decay Is Nearly Enough](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11555)
*Sang Su Lee,Vineeth Loganathan,Shishir Dash,Vijay Raghavan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A method to credibly test if additional signals improve temporal point process models for customer returns, finding that continuous-time decay is nearly sufficient and additional conditioning is often redundant or harmful.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practitioners keep adding more signals to customer-return models, and the TPP literature follows with covariate-conditioned intensities, but it's unclear if this actually improves event timing predictions or how to test it properly.

Method: A screen-and-confirm protocol with positive controls that plants known-signal couplings to verify model capability before testing real data, plus a model-free ceiling to quantify how much timing is point-predictable at all.

Result: On three public benchmarks and a real marketplace: continuous-time decay (inter-event clock) is nearly sufficient for predicting returns, while additional conditioning signals are statistically null (0.06 NLL or less on benchmarks) or mildly harmful (on marketplace data).

Conclusion: The continuous-time decay mechanism is nearly sufficient for customer return timing, and additional conditioning signals the field keeps adding are often redundant or harmful, with tools provided to credibly certify such findings and avoid evaluation pitfalls.

Abstract: Practitioners enrich customer-return models with ever more signals (lifetime value, category, recency/frequency, calendar, geography), and the temporal-point-process (TPP) literature follows suit with covariate- and external-covariate-conditioned intensities. But does any of it improve the timing, and how would you know? A null ("feature X doesn't help") is only meaningful if the model could have found a signal. We make two contributions--a method and a measurement--to answer this credibly. (i) A screen-and-confirm protocol that certifies whether a candidate signal improves a TPP's event-timing likelihood: a positive control plants a coupling of known strength and confirms the model recovers it, so a real-data null can be read as "no signal" rather than "weak method." The control is validated for categorical and continuous encodings, and on a real clock-driven dataset (NYC taxi hour-of-day). (ii) A model-free ceiling quantifying how little of customer-return timing is point-predictable at all (a single-digit percentage of gap variance from any covariate; returns are near-memoryless). With these we certify a clean result on three public benchmarks (Amazon, Taobao, RetailRocket) and a real marketplace (Thumbtack): the inter-event clock--continuous-time decay, long known to beat frozen-intensity models--is nearly sufficient, and the conditioning the field keeps adding is redundant or harmful on top of it (statistically null on the public benchmarks, at most 0.06 NLL; null to mildly harmful on the marketplace). We do not claim to discover that decay helps; our contribution is the tools that turn "conditioning doesn't help" into a checkable, certified statement--plus an honest-evaluation account of the read-out/leakage pitfalls we hit and retracted.

</details>


### [113] [When Offline Evaluation Misleads: A Diagnostic Protocol for Reward and Policy Selection in Delayed-Feedback Contextual Bandits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11560)
*Sang Su Lee,Vineeth Loganathan,Shishir Dash,Vijay Raghavan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A diagnostic protocol for evaluating contextual multi-armed bandits under delayed feedback, focusing on reward alignment and policy learnability to avoid misleading offline evaluations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Teams face challenges when using contextual multi-armed bandits (CMABs) for personalized marketing due to delayed downstream conversions. They train bandits on fast proxy rewards but struggle to determine if CMABs are worth their complexity over sending a single best message. Traditional offline evaluation methods can systematically mislead under delayed feedback conditions.

Method: The authors propose an ordered diagnostic protocol that screens reward-and-policy candidates on two axes: alignment (whether optimizing the reward moves the north-star metric) and learnability (whether the bandit can identify the reward-optimal policy). They validate the protocol using a public off-policy-evaluation benchmark, a controllable synthetic generator, and a deployed large-marketplace push notification system with five arms.

Result: The protocol reveals two key lessons: (1) A single offline number can mis-rank rewards because denser reward signals give the bandit more learning opportunities, causing rewards that appear tied in static estimates to separate during online learning. (2) If you cannot identify the single best message in advance, a per-user policy may simply avoid betting on the wrong one - this looks like personalization but is actually robustness, leading to overstated "personalization premiums."

Conclusion: The contribution is methodological rather than algorithmic, providing a practical diagnostic protocol for delayed-feedback CMABs that helps teams make better decisions about reward selection and personalization strategies by surfacing important insights about alignment and learnability before trusting reported lift metrics.

Abstract: Personalizing marketing messages with contextual multi-armed bandits (CMABs) drives real business value, yet the objective that ultimately matters - a downstream conversion - is observed only weeks later, too late to drive online learning. Teams therefore train the bandit on a fast proxy reward, and separately must judge whether a contextual bandit is worth its complexity over sending one best message. Settling both decisions with the usual offline checks - a batch off-policy estimate, a marginal arm-discrimination test, a confidence interval - can mislead systematically under delayed feedback. We give an ordered diagnostic protocol that screens a reward-and-policy candidate on two axes, alignment (does optimizing the reward move the north-star?) and learnability (can the bandit identify the reward-optimal policy?), before trusting any reported lift. We validate it where the truth is known - a public off-policy-evaluation benchmark and a controllable synthetic generator - and illustrate it on a deployed large-marketplace push system (where, with five arms and one split, the evidence is directional rather than powered). Two lessons recur. (N1) A single offline number can mis-rank rewards: a denser reward signal gives the bandit more to learn from, so rewards that look tied in a static estimate pull apart once learning happens online. (N2) If you cannot tell in advance which single message is best, a per-user policy partly just avoids betting on the wrong one - that looks like personalization but is really robustness, so a "personalization premium" is easily overstated. Our contribution is methodological rather than algorithmic: the ordered protocol, the two lessons it surfaces, and the end-to-end experience of applying it to a delayed-feedback CMAB.

</details>


### [114] [Sparse and robust geometric twin support vector machine via asymmetric RoBoSS loss function](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11567)
*Kai Qi,Xinji Huang,Hongchun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes aR loss function for l1-norm penalized geometric twin SVM (aRSGTSVM) for robust classification and regression with feature selection, handling label/feature noise through asymmetric robust loss and statistical analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world data contains redundant features, label noise, and feature noise, which challenge machine learning efficiency. Standard SVM with l2-norm and hinge loss lacks feature selection capability and is sensitive to noise.

Method: Proposes novel asymmetric, robust, bounded, sparse and smooth (aR) loss function for l1-norm penalized geometric twin SVM. Uses l1-norm for feature selection, aR loss to mitigate label noise and enhance stability to feature noise. Develops proximal gradient descent algorithm for the nonconvex, nonsmooth optimization.

Result: Experimental results show superiority over state-of-the-art methods on synthetic and UCI datasets. Application to index tracking tasks for China stock market indices demonstrates satisfactory performance.

Conclusion: aRSGTSVM effectively addresses noise challenges in real-world data through robust loss design and feature selection, with practical applications in financial markets.

Abstract: In real-world scenarios, the training data usually contains redundant features, label noise and feature noise, which provide severe challenges for the efficiency of machine learning methods. Since standard support vector machine (SVM) adopts $l_2$-norm penalty and hinge loss function, it lacks the ability of selecting significant features and is sensitive to noise. To address these issues, this paper proposes a novel asymmetric, robust, bounded, sparse and smooth (aR) loss function for $l_1$-norm penalized geometric twin SVM (aRSGTSVM) to handle classification and regression tasks. The $l_1$-norm penalty can achieve the feature selection. The proposed aR loss function can not only effectively mitigate the impact of label noise, but also significantly enhance the stability to resampling noise, i.e., the zero-mean feature noise around the boundary hyperplanes. Furthermore, a statistical analysis of the robustness of aRSGTSVM was also conducted using the influence function. Since aRSGTSVM involves nonconvex and nonsmooth optimization, we develop a fast and stable proximal gradient descent based solving algorithm. Compared with related state-of-the-art methods, experimental results demonstrate the superiority of the proposed aRSGTSVM on both synthetic and UCI datasets. Furthermore, we apply aRSGTSVM to index tracking tasks, where results for tracking the different indices in the China stock market show that it can achieve satisfactory performance.

</details>


### [115] [RECAST: A Machine-Learning Framework for Correction and Super-Resolution of Coarse-Grid PDE Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11572)
*Maryam Reza,Farbod Faraji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RECAST is a ML framework that corrects coarse-grid PDE solver errors during time-stepping and reconstructs fine-grid states, enabling accurate simulations with 8-16x coarser spatial grids while maintaining solution fidelity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coarse-grid numerical solvers reduce computational cost for time-dependent PDE simulation, but under-resolution degrades both trajectory accuracy and spatial fidelity of solutions. Current approaches lose accuracy when using substantially coarser grids.

Method: RECAST combines learned error correction within numerical time-stepping loops with reconstruction of fine-grid states from corrected coarse history. It operates in closed-loop rollouts for 1000-step simulations on 1D PDE systems spanning transport, diffusion, dispersion, reaction, and wave dynamics.

Result: RECAST reduces time-averaged relative error by 50-92% compared to uncorrected coarse-grid solvers, maintains close alignment with fine-grid references, generalizes to unseen PDE parameters, and outperforms contemporary coarse-correction architectures over 5000-step rollouts.

Conclusion: RECAST demonstrates that learned correction and reconstruction can enable substantially coarser PDE evolution without loss of solution fidelity, providing a proof-of-concept route toward machine-learning acceleration of higher-dimensional numerical simulations across science and engineering.

Abstract: Coarse-grid numerical solvers can substantially reduce the computational cost of time-dependent PDE simulation, but under-resolution often degrades both the trajectory and the spatial fidelity of the solution. We introduce RECAST (Recurrent Error Correction And Super-resolution of coarse-grid Trajectories), a machine-learning framework designed to restore this lost accuracy while retaining coarse-grid evolution. RECAST combines learned correction within the numerical time-stepping loop with reconstruction of the corresponding fine-grid state from the corrected coarse history. We evaluate the framework on six one-dimensional PDE systems spanning transport, diffusion, dispersion, reaction, and wave dynamics, using spatial grids coarsened by factors of 8-16 and 1000-step closed-loop rollouts from unseen initial conditions. Across the test cases, RECAST remains closely aligned with the fine-grid reference solutions and reduces time-averaged relative error by approximately 50-92% compared with the corresponding uncorrected coarse-grid solvers. Additional tests show generalization to unseen PDE parameter values, while comparison with a contemporary coarse-correction architecture shows that RECAST achieves lower error and better long-horizon agreement with the fine-grid reference over 5000-step rollouts. These results demonstrate that the learned correction and reconstruction capabilities of RECAST can enable substantially coarser PDE evolution without the corresponding loss of solution fidelity, providing a proof-of-concept route toward machine-learning acceleration of higher-dimensional numerical simulations across science and engineering.

</details>


### [116] [Dion3: Full-Stack Orthogonal Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11612)
*Noah Amsel,Jack Zhang,Kwangjun Ahn,Ali Naeimi,Austin Feng,Berlin Chen,Tri Dao,John Langford*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Dion3 is an optimized version of Muon optimizer that reduces cubic-time orthogonalization overhead by 6x through algorithmic improvements, specialized kernels, and selective row orthogonalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon optimizer suffers from significant overhead due to its cubic-time Newton-Schulz orthogonalization step, especially when weights are sharded where communication compounds computational costs, eroding Muon's benefits in many settings.

Method: Three-level optimization approach: 1) Gram Newton-Schulz algorithm reduces FLOP cost of orthogonalization, 2) CuteDSL kernels accelerate computation by exploiting symmetry, 3) megabatching strategy reduces communication overhead, plus a novel update rule that selects only a fraction of momentum matrix rows to orthogonalize at each step.

Result: Dion3 matches or improves on loss achieved by Muon while reducing optimizer step time by up to 6x, outperforming Dion (previous compressed version of Muon) in both speed and performance.

Conclusion: Dion3 successfully addresses Muon's efficiency problems through systematic optimizations at multiple stack levels, providing a practical drop-in replacement with significant speed improvements without sacrificing performance, available as an open-source package.

Abstract: The Muon optimizer incurs a significant overhead cost due to its cubic-time Newton-Schulz orthogonalization step. When weights are sharded, communication overhead compounds this computational cost, eroding the benefits of Muon in many settings. We present Dion3, a revision of Muon that targets this overhead at every level of the stack. Our Gram Newton-Schulz algorithm reduces the FLOP cost of orthogonalization, our CuteDSL kernels accelerate it by exploiting symmetry, and our megabatching strategy reduces communication overhead. Moreover, we propose a simple change to the update rule that cuts costs even further: selecting only a fraction of the momentum matrix's rows to orthogonalize at each step. This update rule improves on Dion (another "compressed" version of Muon), in both speed and performance. Overall, Dion3 matches or improves on the loss achieved by Muon but reduces optimizer step time by up to 6x. Dion3 is available via the dion package (https://github.com/microsoft/dion) as a drop-in replacement for Muon.

</details>


### [117] [A Local Sinkhorn Framework for Conditional Distribution Reconstruction of Multidimensional Random Fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11613)
*Mingtao Xia,Qijing Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a local Sinkhorn divergence framework for conditional distribution reconstruction of multidimensional random fields, offering a scalable alternative to exact optimal transport with balanced accuracy and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Need for scalable conditional distribution reconstruction methods for multidimensional random fields that balance geometric fidelity, statistical efficiency, and computational scalability in uncertainty quantification and probabilistic scientific machine learning.

Method: Uses debiased Sinkhorn divergence to create a differentiable local distribution matching objective for training stochastic neural networks, establishing theoretical generalization error estimates that characterize bias-statistical efficiency trade-offs.

Result: The framework provides effective balance between reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency, maintaining good scalability for multidimensional stochastic systems as demonstrated through numerical examples comparing with other loss functions and uncertainty quantification methods.

Conclusion: The local Sinkhorn divergence framework offers a practical compromise solution for conditional distribution reconstruction that bridges approximation quality, statistical properties, and computational feasibility for uncertainty quantification in scientific machine learning.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose a local Sinkhorn divergence framework for conditional distribution reconstruction of multidimensional random fields. By utilizing the debiased Sinkhorn divergence, our proposed approach develops a differentiable and computationally efficient local distribution matching objective to train stochastic neural networks (SNNs). Furthermore, we establish theoretical generalization error estimates for our local Sinkhorn divergence framework, which explicitly characterizes the trade-off between approximation bias and statistical efficiency controlled by the regularization parameter and reveals how our proposed local Sinkhorn divergence loss function can be efficiently applied to learning multidimensional random field models. The proposed framework provides a scalable alternative to exact local optimal transport for conditional distribution reconstruction, offering a practical compromise between geometric fidelity, statistical efficiency, and computational scalability for uncertainty quantification and probabilistic scientific machine learning. Through various numerical examples, we compare our proposed local Sinkhorn divergence framework with other loss functions to train SNNs and with other machine-learning-based uncertainty quantification frameworks, demonstrating that the proposed local Sinkhorn divergence framework achieves an effective balance between reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency while maintaining good scalability for multidimensional stochastic systems.

</details>


### [118] [FM-LLM: A frequency-enhanced mixture-of-experts framework for adapting LLMs to time series forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11623)
*Rentao Gu,Yihang Ding,Junjie Li,Yi Ding,Weijing Sang,Xiaoli Huo,Xin Qin,Yuefeng Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FM-LLM is a frequency-enhanced autoregressive framework that adapts frozen large language models to time-series forecasting without textual prompts, using spectral token alignment and asymmetric MoE architecture to achieve state-of-the-art performance across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based time-series forecasting methods heavily rely on textual prompts for modality alignment, which introduces significant computational overhead and fails to leverage the rich spectral dynamics inherent in time-series data.

Method: The proposed FM-LLM uses: 1) Fourier Analysis Network-based spectral token aligner to inject structured harmonic representations directly into frozen LLMs, 2) Asymmetric Mixture-of-Experts decoder with shared experts for global periodic backbone and routed experts for non-periodic residuals, 3) Time-frequency hybrid loss function for joint optimization of temporal accuracy and spectral consistency.

Result: FM-LLM achieves state-of-the-art performance on 59 out of 78 evaluation metrics across eleven public benchmarks, with average improvements of 5.3% in MSE and 5.6% in MAE over strongest autoregressive LLM-based baselines, and maximum gains reaching 8.0% for MSE and 8.4% for MAE.

Conclusion: The proposed frequency-enhanced framework enables effective adaptation of frozen LLMs to time-series forecasting without textual prompts, achieving superior performance through structured spectral representations and asymmetric expert specialization, while demonstrating robust transferability in few-shot and zero-shot scenarios.

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have spurred cross-modal solutions for time-series forecasting. However, existing methods rely heavily on textual prompts for modality alignment-introducing nontrivial computational overhead and failing to leverage the rich spectral dynamics inherent in time-series data. To enable prompt-free, frequency-aware adaptation of frozen LLMs, we propose FM-LLM (Frequency-Enhanced Mixture-of-Experts for adapting LLMs to Time Series Forecasting), an autoregressive framework grounded in constrained asymmetric coupling. A Fourier Analysis Network (FAN)-based spectral token aligner injects structured harmonic representations directly into the frozen LLM with numerical compatibility. An asymmetric Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) decoder enforces role separation: shared experts with lightweight FAN layers reconstruct the global periodic backbone, while routed experts-restricted to standard FFNs-specialize in modeling non-periodic residual dynamics. A time-frequency hybrid loss function jointly optimizes temporal accuracy and spectral consistency, mitigating error accumulation during long-horizon autoregressive rollouts. Evaluated across eleven public benchmarks, FM-LLM achieves state-of-the-art performance on 59 out of 78 evaluation metrics. Compared to the strongest autoregressive LLM-based baseline, it delivers average improvements of 5.3% in MSE and 5.6% in MAE, with maximum gains reaching 8.0% for MSE and 8.4% for MAE. FM-LLM also demonstrates robust transferability, maintaining superior performance in 10% few-shot and zero-shot forecasting scenarios.

</details>


### [119] [Transferable Above-Ground Biomass (AGB) Estimation Model from Multi-Sensor Data with Sparse Field Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11638)
*Pann Thinzar Seint,Bryan Atwood,Subas Chhatkuli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A globally-trained CNN combines multi-sensor data (optical, SAR, terrain) with lightweight local field calibration to produce spatially continuous forest above-ground biomass maps, overcoming limitations of sparse field inventories and satellite LiDAR.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Field inventories are spatially sparse while spaceborne LiDAR (GEDI) provides broad but discontinuous biomass samples with systematic underestimation in high-biomass forests. There's a need for spatially continuous AGB quantification for credible carbon accounting and actionable mitigation strategies.

Method: A single globally-trained CNN combines Sentinel-2 optical, Sentinel-1 C-band SAR, ALOS-2 PALSAR-2 L-band SAR, and DEM terrain data, trained against GEDI biomass references. Lightweight empirical field calibration uses local plots for scale-and-bias correction without retraining. The pipeline harmonizes data on 10m grid, computes vegetation indices/polarimetric ratios, normalizes data, and uses hybrid log-domain SmoothL1 with RMSE loss.

Result: Global GEDI-based model achieved R²≈0.78, RMSE≈22 Mg/ha on held-out validation. After field calibration with Random Forest fine-tuning (10-fold CV), local performance improved to R²≈0.82 and RMSE≈15 Mg/ha, outperforming uncalibrated model and ESA CCI Biomass product against field plots.

Conclusion: The operational framework successfully provides spatially continuous forest AGB quantification through a global CNN model with lightweight local adaptation, addressing limitations of existing methods and enabling more accurate carbon accounting and mitigation planning.

Abstract: Spatially continuous quantification of forest above-ground biomass (AGB) is what makes carbon accounting credible and mitigation strategies actionable. While field inventories provide high localized accuracy, they are spatially sparse; conversely, spaceborne LiDAR from the Global Ecosystem Dynamics Investigation (GEDI) offers broad biomass samples but lacks spatial continuity and systematic underestimation of high-biomass forests. This paper presents an operational framework centered on a single globally trained convolutional neural network (CNN) that is seamlessly adapted to each new landscape through a lightweight empirical field-calibration workflow. The global model combines optical (Sentinel-2), C-band SAR (Sentinel-1), L-band SAR (ALOS-2 PALSAR-2), and terrain (DEM) data. It is trained once against GEDI Level-4A biomass reference data spanning multiple regions and both wet and dry seasons so that it learns the persistent woody-structure rather than a single-date appearance. To avoid retraining for every landscape, the framework applies a small number of local field plots to fit a scale-and-bias correction that aligns the global prediction with ground truth in each region. The pipeline harmonizes sensor data onto a shared 10 m grid, derives vegetation indices and polarimetric ratios, computes per-band normalization stats, and trains the CNN with a hybrid log-domain SmoothL1 with RMSE loss for skewed biomass distribution. On held-out validation the global GEDI-based model achieved R^2 approximately 0.78 and RMSE approximately 22 Mg/ha. A subsequent field calibration combining Random Forest fine-tuning under a 10-fold cross-validation eliminates localized regional biases. This improves local validation performance to R^2 approximately 0.82 and reduces RMSE to approximately 15 Mg/ha, outperforming both the uncalibrated global model and the ESA CCI Biomass product against field plots.

</details>


### [120] [Towards a Formal Definition of Agent Memory: Basis, Span, Optimality, and the Sequential Memory Problem](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11654)
*Hongyao Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper develops a formal framework for understanding agent memory systems, treating memory as a basis for knowledge and defining answerability as a coverage problem, with optimal memory maximizing expected coverage under capacity constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite widespread deployment of memory in large-model agents, there is no unified formal account of what memory is or when it is optimal, creating a need for a standardized framework to measure and compare memory systems.

Method: The paper develops a formal mathematical framework where memory is treated as a basis, knowledge as its span, and answerability as a coverage problem. It defines optimal memory as capacity-constrained maximizer of expected coverage, extends this to noisy memory systems, formalizes continual agent-memory problems as sequential MDPs, and instantiates the framework on Homer's Odyssey for concrete analysis.

Result: The framework establishes a utility-capacity frontier as a common yardstick for comparing memory systems, analyzes coverage versus precision tradeoffs in noisy memories, provides concrete metrics through instantiation on Homer's Odyssey, and positions existing systems within the framework to make memory quality measurable.

Conclusion: The paper provides a foundational formal framework for agent memory that makes memory quality measurable, recasts open problems in memory construction and learning as concrete research questions, and establishes a systematic approach to evaluating and optimizing memory systems in AI agents.

Abstract: Despite the wide deployment of memory in large-model agents, there is no unified formal account of what a memory is or when it is optimal. This paper takes a first step toward this account. The central idea is that memory is a basis, knowledge is its span, and answerability is a coverage problem: an agent stores events extracted from a material; a generation operator turns any event set into the knowledge it entails; and a query is answerable exactly when some single item in the span covers it. The optimal memory is then the capacity-constrained maximizer of expected coverage, and its value traces a utility--capacity frontier, the common yardstick on which memory systems can be compared. Next, we consider noise in the memory and discuss coverage versus precision under it: a memory may store false claims, so the write policy must infer the truth of what it stores. Drawing an analogy with biological memory, which is formed continuously through ongoing experience, we formalize the continual agent-memory problem in a sequential MDP that covers multiple levels, where memory is the state, writing is the action, and the utility settled at query time is the delayed reward that drives learning. To make the framework concrete, we instantiate it on Homer's \emph{Odyssey}, turning the frontier, the compression zone, and the divergence of coverage from precision into concrete numbers. Finally, we position existing systems within the framework, making ``how good is a memory'' measurable and recasting the open problems of constructing and learning agent memory as concrete research questions.

</details>


### [121] [Continuous-Latent Predictive Modeling with Semantic Alignment for EEG-Language Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11656)
*Myeong-Ju Cho,Hye-Bin Shin,Seo-Hyun Lee,Seong-Whan Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BLPM is an EEG-language foundation model that reformulates EEG decoding as continuous semantic embedding prediction, addressing limitations of current pretraining approaches by aligning EEG signals with natural-language semantics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current EEG foundation models face challenges: masked autoencoding prioritizes low-level signal reconstruction over task-relevant semantics, while autoregressive modeling creates a mismatch between continuous neural dynamics and discrete token spaces. There's a need to effectively align continuous EEG representations with natural-language semantics for integration with large language models.

Method: Proposes Brain Latent Predictive Model (BLPM) with two key components: 1) Continuous EEG Latent Predictive (CELP) encoder learns transferable representations through latent target prediction, and 2) Multi-Query Semantic Decomposition (MQSD) module extracts task-relevant information and aligns continuous EEG representations with textual semantics in a shared latent space based on semantic relationships.

Result: Experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate consistent generalization performance across diverse tasks, showing that continuous latent semantic prediction is an effective paradigm for EEG-language foundation models.

Conclusion: BLPM successfully establishes continuous latent semantic prediction as an effective approach for EEG-language foundation models, overcoming limitations of existing pretraining paradigms and enabling better alignment between EEG signals and natural language semantics.

Abstract: Recent advances in EEG foundation models have demonstrated the potential of large-scale pretraining to enable generalizable neural decoding across subjects, recording environments, and datasets. However, dominant pretraining paradigms face key challenges: masked autoencoding tends to prioritize low-level signal reconstruction over task-relevant semantics, while autoregressive modeling creates a mismatch between continuous neural dynamics and discrete token spaces. To address these challenges, new strategies are needed to effectively align continuous EEG representations with natural-language semantics and enable their integration with large language models. Accordingly, we propose Brain Latent Predictive Model (BLPM), an EEG-language foundation model that reformulates heterogeneous EEG decoding tasks as a continuous semantic embedding prediction problem. BLPM introduces a Continuous EEG Latent Predictive (CELP) encoder that learns transferable representations through latent target prediction. Building on these representations, a Multi-Query Semantic Decomposition (MQSD) module extracts task-relevant information and aligns continuous EEG representations with textual semantics within a shared latent space according to their semantic relationships. Experiments across multiple benchmarks demonstrate consistent generalization performance across diverse tasks, establishing continuous latent semantic prediction as an effective paradigm for EEG-language foundation models.

</details>


### [122] [Low-Interaction-Rank Learning: Unifying Multiplicative Dual-Encoder Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11661)
*Zijian Zhao,Sen Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A unified theory of multiplicative dual-encoder networks shows that their performance depends on interaction rank and spectral decay, normalization acts as gauge fixing, and whitening recovers interpretable interaction modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multiplicative dual-encoder networks are widely used across domains but lack unified theoretical foundations for key design decisions like interaction modes, normalization, and applicability conditions.

Method: Introduces functions of low interaction rank with interaction spectrum complexity measure, decomposes error into spectral truncation and encoder-realization terms, analyzes normalization as gauge fixing, and uses whitening to identify interaction modes.

Result: Theoretical framework explains sample complexity (sum rather than product of encoder complexities), establishes usability criterion via spectral decay, shows whitening recovers interpretable modes (up to permutation/sign), and validates with synthetic kernels, operator learning, and CLIP models.

Conclusion: Provides unified theoretical foundation for multiplicative dual-encoder networks, explaining design choices, performance limitations, and enabling interpretable representations through whitening that removes arbitrary rotations in learned coordinates.

Abstract: A multiplicative dual-encoder network computes a real-valued output for a pair of inputs as the inner product of their separate encodings. This architecture has been developed independently in operator learning, bipartite matching, contrastive vision-language models, retrieval, and other areas, yet no unified theory guides the basic design decisions: how many interaction modes to represent, how to normalize the encoders, and when the architecture should be avoided. We provide such a foundation by introducing the class of functions of low interaction rank, a class whose intrinsic complexity is measured by its interaction spectrum. Within this framework, approximation error decomposes into a spectral truncation term and an encoder-realization term; sample complexity is governed by the sum of the two encoder complexities rather than their product; and a usability criterion based on spectral decay determines when the architecture can succeed. The same framework exposes a central identifiability problem: the encoders are defined only up to a linear gauge symmetry that leaves the learned coordinates arbitrary. We show that normalization is gauge fixing and that whitening pins the interaction modes up to permutation and sign, thereby explaining the uninterpretability of contrastive dimensions and providing a constructive remedy. Experiments on synthetic kernels, operator learning, and CLIP models validate the theoretical predictions: spectral decay rates match the predicted scaling, whitening recovers the true modes, and independently trained CLIP models are related by a single rotation which, after removal by whitening, exposes interpretable concept axes. The code of this paper is provided at https://github.com/RS2002/Mul-Net .

</details>


### [123] [Rubric Dropout: A Simple Way to Mitigate Reward Hacking in Rubric-as-Reward RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11669)
*Minglai Yang,Xinyu Guo,Utkarsh Tyagi,Mian Zhang,Razvan Dumitru,Sunjie Hou,Yunzhong He,Daniel Yue Zhang,Ying Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper identifies reward hacking in LLM fine-tuning against fixed rubrics and proposes Rubric Dropout to prevent over-optimization by randomly dropping rubric criteria during training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed rubrics are imperfect proxies for quality, and policies trained against them will eventually exploit this difference (reward hacking). The authors want to measure and prevent this divergence between training judge scores and gold judge scores on out-of-distribution benchmarks.

Method: Train Qwen3-8B with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on medical/science rubrics. Measure divergence between training judge and gold judge scores on OOD benchmarks. Propose Rubric Dropout: randomly drop subsets of rubric criteria during training while keeping dropped subset consistent across each rollout group to maintain comparable advantages.

Result: Without dropout, training judge scores keep climbing while gold judge scores peak then fall (3 points on HealthBench-Hard, 22 points on ResearchQA). Rubric Dropout (30-50%) raises OOD gold scores (+1-2 points on HealthBench-Hard, +6-7 on ResearchQA), reduces hacking measures, and costs nothing in-domain. Criteria reweighting performs worse than no intervention.

Conclusion: Reward hacking in rubric-based RL is measurable and problematic. Rubric Dropout effectively prevents over-optimization to fixed rubrics, improving generalization to OOD benchmarks without sacrificing in-domain performance, outperforming alternative interventions like criteria reweighting.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning against rubrics, lists of criteria graded by an LLM judge, has become a standard way to post-train language models on tasks with no deterministic answer. The rubric, however, is a fixed proxy for quality, never a complete description of it, and a policy trained against it long enough will learn to exploit the difference. We measure this directly. Training Qwen3-8B with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on medical and science rubrics and grading out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks with both the training judge and a stronger gold judge, we find that the two scores diverge during training. The training judge's score keeps climbing while the gold judge's score peaks and then falls, by 3 points on HealthBench-Hard and by 22 points on ResearchQA. A judge with a fixed bias would shift the gold curve by a constant, not send it down while the training score rises, so the divergence is reward hacking, not judge noise. We propose Rubric Dropout, a one-line fix borrowed from neuron dropout. At every step, we randomly drop a subset of the rubric's criteria before computing the reward, so the policy never optimizes the same rubric twice. The dropped subset is shared across each rollout group, so GRPO's group-relative advantages stay comparable, and evaluation always uses the full rubric. Comparing no dropout against dropout at 30% and 50% on both benchmark pairs, dropout raises the OOD gold score at every matched checkpoint (+1 to +2 points on HealthBench-Hard, +6 to +7 points on ResearchQA), lowers the two hacking measures we track, and costs nothing in domain. Sweeping the dropout fraction shows a broad 30-50% sweet spot, while the natural alternative, reweighting criteria by how useful they are to training, performs worse than no intervention at all in our setting.

</details>


### [124] [GCPO: Diagnosing and Constraining Subspace Geometry in Rollout RL for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11674)
*Kai Yang,Jingwei Xu,Wanyu Wang,Kai-Yuan Guo,Zhenbo Yu,Yi Wang,Yu Qiao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GCPO (Geometrically Constrained Policy Optimization) is a new reinforcement learning method that uses orthogonal projections to constrain policy updates, addressing training instabilities and performance degradation in LLM post-training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-policy rollout methods like GRPO suffer from training instabilities, cross-task capability degradation, and response-length inflation in large language model post-training. The relationship between stepwise geometry of updates and model performance is unclear, and transient alignment spikes often precede performance degradation.

Method: Introduces Principal-Subspace Overlap (a dimension-corrected measure of individual rollout updates) and proposes GCPO, which applies hard bilateral orthogonal projections to constrain updates to complementary subspaces, preventing harmful alignment excursions by construction.

Result: GCPO consistently outperforms GRPO and recent variants (DAPO, GSPO) across mathematical reasoning, code generation, and tool-use tasks on Qwen3-8B and GLM4-9B, improving over base models by up to 27.69 points and strongest baseline by 2.37 points. It preserves general capabilities, eliminates response-length inflation, and stabilizes policy entropy.

Conclusion: GCPO provides both a new diagnostic lens through Principal-Subspace Overlap and a principled design perspective for stable reinforcement learning post-training, addressing key limitations of existing on-policy rollout methods while maintaining and improving model performance.

Abstract: On-policy rollout methods such as GRPO are central to post-training of large language models, yet they frequently suffer from training instabilities, cross-task capability degradation, and response-length inflation. Although prior work has characterized the subspace geometry of aggregate updates, the stepwise variation of this geometry and its relationship to model performance remain unclear. We introduce Principal-Subspace Overlap, a dimension-corrected measure of individual rollout updates relative to the dominant singular subspaces of pretrained weights. Despite low average overlap, transient spikes often precede performance degradation. To address this, we propose GCPO (Geometrically Constrained Policy Optimization), which applies hard bilateral orthogonal projections to constrain updates to the complementary subspaces, preventing such excursions by construction. Across mathematical reasoning, code generation, and tool-use tasks on Qwen3-8B and GLM4-9B, GCPO consistently outperforms GRPO and recent variants, including DAPO and GSPO, improving over the base models and the strongest baseline by up to 27.69 and 2.37 points, respectively. Furthermore, GCPO preserves general capabilities, eliminates response-length inflation, and stabilizes policy entropy. Our findings provide a new diagnostic lens and a principled design perspective for stable reinforcement learning post-training.

</details>


### [125] [FunnelCausalNet: Funnel-aware Joint Conversion-Revenue Uplift for Multi-tier Coupon Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11675)
*Yu Zhang,Zhihan Wang,Guanlin Chen,Min Jiang,Shuai Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FunnelCausalNet is a neural uplift estimator that decomposes GMV into conversion and conditional value components to improve variance reduction in coupon campaign optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coupon campaigns aim to increase both conversion and revenue, but GMV (gross merchandise value) has challenging statistical properties: it follows a conversion funnel, is zero-inflated (many zero values), and heavy-tailed. Existing methods struggle with these characteristics, creating a need for better uplift estimation.

Method: Proposes FunnelCausalNet, which couples binary conversion head with nonnegative conditional-value head through μ_gmv = μ_conv × μ_val. Uses explicit RCT assumptions, support, rate-gap, and cross-head covariance control. Paired with marginal split-conformal CATE summaries combined via Bonferroni union as audit bands, and Lagrangian budgeted allocator using RCT-anchored estimates for subsidy-aware ROI accounting.

Result: On semi-synthetic Criteo-MT7 data, mean AUUC_GMV is within one seed standard deviation of leading baseline; GMV effect error reduced by 18-48% vs direct GMV regression. On industrial Hotel-Coupon RCT logs (4.9M hold-out records), FunnelCausalNet has best seed-averaged mean DeltaROI at all seven correlated anchors (10-60%). Revenue-focused rankers dominate uplift-curve proxies in sparse binary-spend benchmarks.

Conclusion: Funnel composition through conversion-value decomposition can reduce variance and improve uplift estimation for zero-inflated, heavy-tailed GMV in coupon campaigns, though this is heuristic rather than guaranteed. The method shows practical improvements across both synthetic and real-world industrial data.

Abstract: Coupon campaigns seek to lift both conversion and revenue, but gross merchandise value (GMV) follows a deterministic funnel from conversion to conditional order value and is zero-inflated and heavy-tailed. We propose FunnelCausalNet, an uplift estimator coupling a binary conversion head with a nonnegative conditional-value head through $μ_{\mathrm{gmv}}=μ_{\mathrm{conv}}μ_{\mathrm{val}}$. Under explicit RCT, support, rate-gap, and cross-head covariance-control assumptions, an idealized leading-order MSE comparison identifies a regime in which funnel composition can reduce pointwise variance; this is a heuristic, not a guarantee for the shared-representation neural model. The estimator is paired with marginal split-conformal CATE summaries, combined through a Bonferroni union as audit bands, and a Lagrangian budgeted allocator using RCT-anchored estimates for subsidy-aware ROI accounting. On semi-synthetic multi-tier Criteo-MT7, FunnelCausalNet's mean AUUC_GMV is within one seed standard deviation of the leading feature-interaction baseline among eleven baselines, while a controlled ablation reduces GMV effect error versus direct GMV regression by 18--48% across tested zero-inflation regimes. On de-identified industrial Hotel-Coupon RCT logs with about 4.9 million hold-out exposure records per seed, expected-outcome evaluation sweeps full LP frontiers; FunnelCausalNet has the best seed-averaged mean DeltaROI at all seven correlated anchors from 10% to 60%, which we treat as descriptive frontier consistency rather than independent significance. On sparse binary-spend public benchmarks, revenue-focused rankers can dominate uplift-curve proxies, defining an explicit regime boundary.

</details>


### [126] [Drift and Dependence: Layer-wise Information-Theoretic Bounds for Replay-Based Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11690)
*Tieliang Gong,Zhongbo Zhang,Wen Wen,Yong-Jin Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper presents a layer-wise information-theoretic framework for analyzing continual learning with replay, decomposing generalization gap into representation drift and optimization effects, with operational refinements for identifying critical layers and predicting forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing analyses of replay in continual learning conflate two important effects: finite memory replacement of past distributions with empirical proxies, and the coupling of buffer, current data, and hypothesis through shared optimization trajectories. This coupling makes it difficult to understand how replay actually prevents catastrophic forgetting.

Method: Develops a layer-wise information-theoretic framework that separates memory effects from optimization effects at every network depth. Uses a Wasserstein relaxation to handle support mismatch for drift analysis, and an SGLD (Stochastic Gradient Langevin Dynamics) instantiation to simplify optimization analysis into a trajectory-level log-determinant budget.

Result: 1. Main decomposition: expected generalization gap = replay-induced representation drift + optimization-dependence term (further resolved into stability, plasticity, interaction, and residual-coupling components). 2. Identifies depth-dependent drift-sensitivity trade-off with optimal interior layer for stabilization. 3. Derives curvature-aware gradient-alignment statistic as online diagnostic of task-wise forgetting. 4. Experimental confirmation of predicted memory scaling, interior funnel effects, and alignment signal's link to forgetting.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully separates and quantifies the effects of replay in continual learning, providing both theoretical insights and practical diagnostics. The depth-dependent analysis reveals which layers are most critical for preventing forgetting, and the gradient-alignment statistic offers a practical tool for monitoring forgetting during training.

Abstract: Continual learning must absorb new tasks without erasing old ones, and replay---mixing a small buffer of past examples into current training---is among the most effective remedies for catastrophic forgetting. Yet its generalization behavior is shaped by two coupled effects that existing analyses fold into a single hypothesis-level quantity: finite memory replaces each past distribution with an empirical proxy, and repeated reuse couples the buffer, the current data, and the final hypothesis through a shared optimization trajectory. We develop a layer-wise information-theoretic framework that separates these effects at every depth. Our main result decomposes the expected generalization gap into a replay-induced representation drift and an optimization-dependence term, the latter further resolved into stability, plasticity, interaction, and residual-coupling components. Two refinements make the framework operational. A Wasserstein relaxation of the drift term, valid under support mismatch, yields a depth-dependent drift--sensitivity trade-off whose minimizer identifies which interior layer to stabilize. An SGLD instantiation of the optimization term reduces it to a trajectory-level log-determinant budget, exposing a curvature-aware gradient-alignment statistic that serves as an online diagnostic of task-wise forgetting. Controlled and benchmark experiments confirm the predicted memory scaling, the interior funnel, and the alignment signal's link to forgetting.

</details>


### [127] [LEMUR: Latent Entropy-aware Multimodal Unlearning via Visual-anchored Reasoning Redirection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11691)
*Xinhao Zhong,Yuxia Qiao,Junhao Li,Hao Fang,Yi Sun,Bin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RL-trained multimodal reasoning models leak sensitive information in reasoning traces even after unlearning, and LEMUR uses entropy dynamics to detect and sanitize this leakage during inference without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper identifies a critical privacy vulnerability in RL-trained multimodal reasoning models: sensitive facts successfully removed from final answers still appear in reasoning traces (chains of thought), a problem worse in RL-trained models than base models.

Method: LEMUR is a training-free inference-time unlearning framework that uses token-level entropy dynamics as a control signal to detect sensitive reasoning, then redirects reasoning trajectories via entropy-modulated visual-anchor latent injection to replace sensitive tokens with sanitized, probability-weighted embeddings grounded in the input image.

Result: LEMUR consistently outperforms existing unlearning methods across diverse MLRMs, better suppressing both reasoning-trace and answer leakage while preserving non-sensitive utility and output fluency.

Conclusion: RL-induced entropy dynamics provide a distinctive signal for privacy leakage, enabling effective training-free unlearning for reasoning-capable multimodal models, addressing a vulnerability that existing unlearning methods miss.

Abstract: Reinforcement-learning (RL) post-training equips multimodal large reasoning models (MLRMs) with exploratory chains of thought (CoT), substantially improving visual reasoning. However, we find that this capability introduces a distinct privacy vulnerability: even when a sensitive fact is successfully unlearned from the final answer, the model may still reproduce it in its reasoning trace. This leakage is substantially more pronounced in natively RL-trained MLRMs than in their non -reasoning base models, revealing a privacy risk that existing unlearning methods are not designed to address. We show that RL-induced exploration leaves sensitive content with a distinctive token-level entropy signature that is largely absent from base models.
