Based on this observation, we propose LEMUR, a fully training-free, inference-time unlearning framework for natively RL-trained multimodal models. LEMUR uses entropy dynamics as a control signal to identify when sensitive reasoning begins and when sanitization should stop. During this interval, it redirects the reasoning trajectory through entropy-modulated visual-anchor latent injection, replacing committed tokens with sanitized, probability-weighted embeddings re-grounded in the input image. Across diverse MLRMs, LEMUR consistently outperforms existing unlearning met hods in suppressing both reasoning-trace and answer leakage, while better preserving non-sensitive utility and output fluency. These results demonstrate that RL-induced entropy dynamics provide a distinctive signal for privacy leakage and that exploiting this signal enables effective training-free unlearning for reasoning-capable multimodal models.

</details>


### [128] [REOPD: Reliability-Adaptive Reward Extrapolation for On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11698)
*Yang Sun,Lichao Ma,Houyuan Qin,Yuxin Liu,Hanyang Lu,Yao Zhu,Pinlong Cai,Guohang Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REOPD is a reliability-adaptive reward extrapolation framework for on-policy distillation that uses token-level compatibility weights and batch-level adaptive budgets to selectively extrapolate only along reliable teacher-reference directions, improving stability and performance across domains without needing additional models or rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reward-extrapolation methods like ExOPD use a single global coefficient (λ) for all tokens, which can cause reward hacking and unstable training as students fit extreme peaks in implicit rewards. Additionally, the optimal λ varies across domains, requiring costly hyperparameter sweeps.

Method: REOPD combines token-level compatibility weight with batch-level adaptive budget to create token-wise coefficients λ_{b,t}=1+γ_b q_t. This preserves teacher alignment while selectively extrapolating only along reliable teacher-reference directions. The approach doesn't require verifiers, reward models, value models, or extra rollouts beyond standard OPD.

Result: REOPD outperforms G-OPD on single-teacher mathematics tasks and on both domains in multi-teacher settings, while matching G-OPD performance on single-teacher code tasks. This demonstrates effective fine-grained reliability adaptation across different domains and teacher configurations.

Conclusion: REOPD provides a more robust and adaptive approach to on-policy distillation that mitigates reward hacking and instability through fine-grained reliability adaptation, eliminating the need for costly hyperparameter sweeps while maintaining or improving performance across diverse domains.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own trajectories under dense token-level supervision from a teacher. Reward-extrapolation methods such as ExOPD amplify the teacher-reference log-likelihood ratio to move beyond direct imitation, but apply a single global coefficient $λ$ to every token. This can drive the student to fit extreme peaks in the implicit reward, causing reward hacking and unstable training, and the optimal $λ$ varies across domains, requiring costly sweeps. We propose REOPD, a reliability-adaptive reward extrapolation framework for OPD. REOPD combines a token-level compatibility weight with a batch-level adaptive budget, yielding a token-wise coefficient $λ_{b,t}=1+γ_b q_t$ that preserves teacher alignment while selectively extrapolating along reliable teacher-reference directions. It requires no verifier, reward model, value model, or extra rollout beyond standard OPD. REOPD outperforms G-OPD on single-teacher mathematics and on both domains in the multi-teacher setting, while matching G-OPD on single-teacher code, demonstrating effective fine-grained reliability adaptation across domains and teacher configurations.

</details>


### [129] [Consolidator: Learning Persistent Routed Memory Across Context Boundaries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11701)
*Sungwoo Goo,Hwi-yeol Yun,Sangkeun Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Consolidator operator transforms short-term memory into long-term memory, enabling stored information to both persist and influence subsequent memory access through hierarchical routing, improving updated-mapping recall from 44% to 87%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the problem that merely preserving short-term memory in a slower store doesn't guarantee the retained state will influence subsequent memory access operations, distinguishing between persistence and functional influence.

Method: Using Consolidator, a shared slot-local operator that transforms routed short-term memory before accumulating it into long-term memory without replaying source tokens. After each consolidation, the KV cache and STM are cleared, but LTM remains accessible and conditions hierarchical routing for subsequent inputs.

Result: Direct LTM routing improves updated-mapping recall from 44.38% to 87.02% (+42.64 percentage points) on a two-segment modulo-10 mapping task. Learned consolidation outperforms forced identity accumulation by 21.40 percentage points without routing and 68.70 with routing, using only 12.35K trainable parameters.

Conclusion: Consolidated long-term memory serves dual functions: as retrievable content that can be read, and as an access state that shapes subsequent slot selection through hierarchical routing, demonstrating that retention alone is insufficient for functional memory influence.

Abstract: Copying short-term memory (STM) into a slower store can preserve state across a context boundary, but persistence alone does not ensure that the retained state influences subsequent memory access. We test this distinction in a Phasor Memory Network (PMNet) using Consolidator, a shared slot-local operator that transforms routed STM before accumulating it into long-term memory (LTM), without replaying the source tokens. After each consolidation, the KV cache and STM are cleared. The retained LTM can still be read and is also fed into the hierarchical router, thereby conditioning which explicit-memory slots subsequent inputs access. We evaluate this mechanism on a two-segment modulo-10 mapping task in which the second segment updates the mapping at the same memory address. Following a second consolidation and reset, a held-out query must recover the updated mapping from LTM. The backbone and memory interface are frozen, leaving only 12.35K Consolidator parameters trainable (0.041\% of a 29.95M model). Across five paired runs from the same STM-pretraining checkpoint, direct LTM routing raises updated-mapping recall from $44.38\pm1.94\%$ to $87.02\pm1.76\%$ ($+42.64\pm1.10$ percentage points), while immediate STM recall remains 89.90\% in both conditions; both train separate Consolidators and retain the same LTM read paths. Learned consolidation outperforms forced identity accumulation by $21.40\pm1.91$ percentage points without routing and $68.70\pm1.76$ with routing. Thus, on this task, consolidated LTM serves as both retrievable content and an access state that shapes subsequent slot selection.

</details>


### [130] [Robust and Efficient Noisy-Label Time-Series Classification via Dynamic Time Warping Based Granular Ball Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11704)
*Ziqiang Li,Yun Liu,Gouhei Tanaka*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DTW-GBC organizes time-series data into granular balls for classification, improving robustness against label noise while reducing computational cost compared to traditional DTW-based 1-NN classifiers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional DTW-based nearest-neighbor classifiers have two main limitations: vulnerability to mislabeled training samples (label noise) and high computational cost during inference due to numerous DTW calculations.

Method: Proposes DTW-based Granular Ball Computing (DTW-GBC) that clusters temporally similar training samples into granular balls and performs classification at the granule level instead of individual sample level. Develops two different strategies for constructing these granular balls.

Result: Experiments on four benchmark datasets with symmetric label noise show that both DTW-GBC variants mitigate performance degradation caused by label noise while requiring substantially fewer comparisons than DTW-based 1-NN during inference.

Conclusion: DTW-GBC provides a favorable balance between classification robustness against label noise and inference efficiency, offering a more practical solution for real-world time-series classification tasks with potential data quality issues.

Abstract: Dynamic Time Warping (DTW)-based Nearest-Neighbor (NN) classifiers are effective for time-series classification but are vulnerable to mislabeled training samples and require numerous DTW computations during inference. We propose DTW-based Granular Ball Computing (DTW-GBC), which organizes temporally similar training samples into granular balls and performs classification at the granule level. We further develop two granular-ball construction strategies for DTW-GBC. Experiments on four benchmark datasets with symmetric label noise show that the two DTW-GBC variants generally mitigate the performance degradation caused by label noise while requiring substantially fewer comparisons than DTW-based 1-NN during inference. These findings suggest that DTW-GBC provides a favorable balance between classification robustness and inference efficiency.

</details>


### [131] [High-dimensional Multi-objective Bayesian Optimization with Learned Variable Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11713)
*Hongyan Wang,Jiayu Huang,Haotian Zheng,Xin Gao,Chi Ding,Ying Liu,Xia Wang,Qing Xu,Keqiang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ViaMOBO is a framework for high-dimensional expensive multi-objective Bayesian optimization that uses variable interaction analysis to partition decision space and perform local optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MOBO approaches are limited to low-dimensional decision spaces due to exponential sampling complexity, creating a need for effective methods for high-dimensional expensive multi-objective problems.

Method: ViaMOBO utilizes a variable interaction analysis model to determine if decision space can be completely or partially divided, then performs local Bayesian optimization in the divided decision subspaces to handle separability in objectives.

Result: Experimental results on synthetic and real-world benchmarks show that ViaMOBO outperforms state-of-the-art MOBO methods in approximating Pareto fronts of high-dimensional expensive multi-objective problems.

Conclusion: ViaMOBO provides an effective generic framework for high-dimensional expensive multi-objective optimization that addresses the dimensionality limitations of existing MOBO approaches through intelligent variable interaction analysis and subspace optimization.

Abstract: Multi-objective Bayesian optimization (MOBO) is effective in identifying the Pareto fronts for expensive black-box problems. However, most current MOBO approaches are limited to low-dimensional decision space due to its exponential sampling complexity. This paper presents decision variable interaction analysis-based MOBO, ViaMOBO, a generic framework for expensive multi-objective problems with high-dimensional decision space. The key idea of ViaMOBO is that it utilizes a variable interaction analysis model to determine whether the decision space can be completely or partially divided, and then performs local Bayesian optimization in the divided decision subspaces. Through the variable analysis model, it can be derived whether the objectives in black-box problems are separable, partially separable, or non-separable based on the potential independent or interdependent relationships among decision variables without any strong assumptions. We compare ViaMOBO with the state-of-the-art MOBO methods on both synthetic and real-world benchmarks. The experimental results demonstrate that ViaMOBO outperforms other related MOBO baselines in approximating the Pareto front of high-dimensional expensive multi-objective problems.

</details>


### [132] [Chain-of-Thought Shows the Path to a Tree: Realizing Branching Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11716)
*Debanjan Dutta,Anish Chakrabarty,Swagatam Das*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper provides concrete depth-bounded Transformer constructions for tree traversal algorithms (DFS and Dijkstra) using unique hard-attention decoders, enabling computation of tree complexity measures like Strahler number and width with linear steps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While theoretical characterizations link Chain of Thought (CoT) steps to circuit complexity classes, there's a lack of explicit, depth-bounded constructions and traversal procedures. The paper aims to close this gap for branching complexity by providing concrete instantiations.

Method: Constructs CoT realizations of depth-first search (DFS) and Dijkstra's algorithm using unique hard-attention decoders of at most two layers. Reuses these decoders as computational substrates: DFS decoder computes Strahler number in 2n-1 steps with four layers, Dijkstra decoder computes tree width in n-1 steps with three layers. Also provides independent constructions on Dyck path representation via classical bijection between ordered trees and Dyck paths.

Result: Successfully implements DFS and Dijkstra algorithms with bounded-depth Transformers (≤2 layers). Achieves linear-step computations for tree complexity measures: Strahler number in 2n-1 steps with four layers, and tree width in n-1 steps with three layers. Handles arbitrary n-ary trees without layer normalization or positional encodings, demonstrating non-trivial capabilities in the linear-step regime of the CoT hierarchy.

Conclusion: The paper provides explicit, depth-bounded constructions that bridge theoretical CoT characterizations with practical implementations for branching complexity, demonstrating that Transformers with unique hard attention can perform non-trivial tree computations in the linear-step CoT regime without typical architectural components like layer normalization or positional encodings.

Abstract: Chain of Thought (CoT) lifts the expressive ceiling of bounded-depth Transformers, with characterizations tying the number of CoT steps to circuit complexity classes. What remains largely missing are concrete instantiations with explicit, depth-bounded constructions, and the traversal procedures such characterizations presuppose. We close this gap for branching complexity. We give CoT realizations of depth-first search (DFS) and of Dijkstra algorithm, the latter subsuming breadth-first search, by unique hard-attention decoders of at most two layers, and use them as a shared computational substrate: reusing the DFS decoder yields the Strahler number of an $n$-vertex tree in $2n-1$ steps with four layers, and reusing the Dijkstra decoder yields its width in $n-1$ steps with three. Since computing the Strahler number of a binary tree given as a term is \textsf{NC\textsuperscript{1}}-complete, and our constructions handle arbitrary $n$-ary trees without layer normalization or positional encodings, this is a non-trivial witness for the linear-step regime of the CoT hierarchy. Exploiting the classical bijection between ordered trees and Dyck paths, itself realized by our DFS construction, which emits the path as it traverses, we give independent constructions for both measures on the path representation.

</details>


### [133] [Epiplexity Guided Data Selection and Generation for Out-of-Distribution Generalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11746)
*Ellen Su,Andres Potapczynski,Shikai Qiu,Edward Hughes,Andrew Gordon Wilson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Epiplexity (structural information measure) is used as an online training signal for adaptive data selection and synthetic data generation, improving downstream task transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern systems need to generalize to tasks not specified during training. The paper investigates what data facilitates such generalization, hypothesizing that data rich in structural information contains shared circuits/subprograms that can be recycled across downstream settings.

Method: Two approaches: 1) Data selection - fitting scaling laws to training loss curves to predict epiplexity gain as function of training tokens, using this to adaptively determine domain sampling weights. 2) Synthetic data generation - using REINFORCE policy gradients with reward defined as change in learner epiplexity over generated data buffer, guiding generator toward epiplexity-maximizing distribution.

Result: Higher epiplexity predicts improved downstream performance on both zero-shot and fine-tuning tasks, supporting the hypothesis that structural information-rich data yields representations that transfer across domains.

Conclusion: Epiplexity can be effectively operationalized as an online training signal for both data selection and generation, demonstrating that focusing on structural information content in data leads to better generalization and transfer across unanticipated tasks.

Abstract: Modern systems are increasingly expected to transfer across tasks not specified during training. What data facilitates generalization in these new, unanticipated settings? One hypothesis is that data with more structural information could contain shared circuits and subprograms that could be recycled in a wider array of downstream settings. Epiplexity, a recently proposed measure of the structural information a compute-bounded learner can extract from data, provides a mechanism to reason about this relationship. In this paper, we show how to operationalize epiplexity as an online training signal for data selection and synthetic data generation. For selection, we fit scaling laws to the training loss curves of natural data domains to predict the expected epiplexity gain as a function of training tokens, and use this signal to adaptively determine the sampling weights over domains during training. For synthetic data generation, we define a generator's reward as the change in learner epiplexity over a buffer of previously generated data and use REINFORCE policy gradients to guide the generator toward an epiplexity-maximizing distribution. In both cases, higher epiplexity predicts improved downstream performance on zero-shot and fine-tuning based tasks, supporting the hypothesis that data rich in structural information yield representations that transfer across domains.

</details>


### [134] [MOON: Multi-Objective OrthoNormalized Updates for Multitask Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11749)
*Shiji Zhou,Kunlin Lyu,Lei Zhang,Ruodong Wang,Yifan Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MOON is a novel multi-objective optimization method that performs gradient manipulation under matrix geometry instead of Euclidean space, improving optimization efficiency and multi-task performance for modern architectures like Transformers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MOO methods flatten model parameters into vectors and perform gradient manipulation under Euclidean geometry, overlooking the matrix structure in modern architectures (e.g., Transformers). This approach doesn't yield the steepest descent direction under matrix geometry, potentially limiting optimization efficiency.

Method: Proposes MOON (Multi-Objective OrthoNormalized Updates), which performs gradient manipulation under spectral-nuclear norm geometry and uses orthonormalized manipulated gradients for parameter updates, based on steepest descent theory for matrix-valued parameters.

Result: Theoretically establishes convergence of the averaged Pareto-stationarity measure at rates of O(T^{-1/2}) (deterministic) and O(T^{-1/4}) (stochastic). Empirical results show MOON consistently improves both optimization efficiency and final multi-task performance across various benchmarks.

Conclusion: MOON addresses limitations of Euclidean-based gradient manipulation by working under matrix geometry, providing better optimization efficiency and performance for multi-task learning, especially with matrix-structured architectures like Transformers.

Abstract: Multi-objective optimization (MOO) has demonstrated significant success in multi-task learning by mitigating task conflicts through gradient manipulation. However, most existing methods flatten model parameters into vectors and perform gradient manipulation under Euclidean geometry, thereby overlooking the matrix structure prevalent in modern architectures such as Transformers. In this paper, we show that gradient manipulation in Euclidean space does not generally yield the steepest descent direction under matrix geometry, potentially limiting optimization efficiency. Drawing from the theory of steepest descent for matrix-valued parameters, we propose MOON (Multi-Objective OrthoNormalized Updates), which performs gradient manipulation under spectral--nuclear norm geometry and uses the orthonormalized manipulated gradient for parameter updates. Theoretically, for smooth non-convex objectives, we establish convergence of the averaged Pareto-stationarity measure at rates of $\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2})$ in the deterministic setting and $\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/4})$ under stochastic gradients. Empirical results across various benchmarks show that MOON consistently improves both optimization efficiency and final multi-task performance. Our code is available at https://github.com/KunlinLyu/MOON.

</details>


### [135] [TradingMoE: Routing the Right Experts in Evolving Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11785)
*Chang Zhou,Xingtong Yu,Minbin Huang,Zhennan Wu,Yuan Fang,Hong Cheng,Xinming Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TradingMoE is a trading-oriented sparse Mixture-of-Experts framework that improves financial trading decisions by better matching experts to market conditions using a Query-Key router and sparse expert selection updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based trading systems have limitations: they either coordinate human-defined external experts or use conventional MoE routers that don't properly evaluate how individual experts contribute to trading decisions. These routers don't receive signals indicating when inactive experts become more suitable as market conditions change, and native router scores poorly reflect expert usefulness, often leaving better alternatives unselected.

Method: Proposes TradingMoE with: 1) A Query-Key router that represents token-specific expertise needs as low-dimensional queries matched with learnable expert keys, leveraging the discovered low-dimensional structure of expert usefulness; 2) A sparse expert selection update mechanism that samples inactive experts during training to estimate if they should replace the weakest expert in the current Top-k selection, enabling adaptation to changing market conditions while preserving sparse computation.

Result: Experiments with 22 baselines on stock and cryptocurrency markets show TradingMoE improves cumulative return over the best-performing baselines by 30.89% (stocks) and 30.7% (cryptocurrency). Rolling paper-trading experiments demonstrate its advantage persists under forward-only deployment.

Conclusion: TradingMoE successfully addresses limitations of existing LLM-based trading systems by introducing a trading-oriented sparse MoE with better expert selection mechanisms that adapt to changing market conditions, resulting in significantly improved trading performance across different financial markets.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have shown strong potential for financial analysis and trading, but direct trading remains challenging because the predictive capabilities required can vary across assets, decision fields, and market conditions. Existing LLM-based trading systems either coordinate human-defined external experts or adopt conventional internal Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routers that do not directly evaluate how individual experts contribute to trading decisions. Moreover, these routers receive no direct signal indicating when an inactive expert has become more suitable as market conditions change. We find that native router scores poorly reflect how much individual experts improve trading decisions, frequently leaving better alternatives unselected. We further reveal that token-specific expert usefulness exhibits a compact low-dimensional structure. Based on these findings, we propose TradingMoE, a trading-oriented sparse MoE that augments a frozen dense LLM with lightweight residual experts. We introduce a Query-Key router that represents the expertise required by each token under the current market context as a low-dimensional query and matches it with learnable expert keys. We further propose a sparse expert selection update mechanism that samples a few inactive experts during training and estimates whether they should replace the weakest expert in the current Top-k route. This mechanism enables the router to update expert selection as market conditions change while preserving sparse computation. Experiments against 22 baselines on stock and cryptocurrency markets show that TradingMoE improves cumulative return over the best-performing baselines by 30.89% and 30.7%, respectively. Rolling paper-trading experiments further demonstrate that its advantage persists under forward-only deployment.

</details>


### [136] [High-Order Liquid Evidence Encoding for Gradual GNSS Spoofing Detection in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11790)
*Muhammad Ayub Sabir,Junbiao Pang,Fatima Ashraf*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A causal high-order liquid evidence framework for GNSS spoofing detection that models the evolution of GNSS-motion inconsistencies through multiple evidence streams, achieving state-of-the-art performance on real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNSS-based localization is critical for autonomous driving safety, but continuous subtle spoofing attacks are hard to detect because individual GNSS observations may appear plausible while the inconsistency between GNSS-implied displacement and actual vehicle motion gradually increases over time. Existing methods fail to explicitly model this temporal evolution of inconsistencies.

Method: Proposes a causal high-order liquid evidence framework: 1) constructs a physics-guided GNSS-motion inconsistency residual comparing GNSS-implied displacement with onboard-motion-derived displacement, 2) forms three separate evidence streams for residual level, first-order discrete variations, and second-order discrete variations with contextual cues, 3) processes each stream with separate adaptive liquid encoders, and 4) hierarchically couples temporal states to predict spoofing at window endpoints using only current and past observations.

Result: Achieves highest F1-scores among evaluated temporal models on real-world AV-GPS dataset subsets: 0.9535 on Dataset 1 and 0.9777 on Dataset 3. On Dataset 3, detects both labeled normal-to-attack transitions within four sampling steps.

Conclusion: The proposed causal high-order liquid evidence framework effectively models the temporal evolution of GNSS-motion inconsistencies for spoofing detection, outperforming existing methods by capturing the gradual nature of subtle attacks through multiple evidence streams and adaptive liquid encoding.

Abstract: Accurate Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS)-based localization is essential for safe and reliable autonomous driving. However, spoofing attacks can manipulate vehicle position estimates. Continuous and subtle attacks are particularly difficult to detect because individual GNSS observations may remain plausible while the inconsistency between GNSS-implied displacement and onboard vehicle motion gradually increases. Existing methods often rely on static vehicle-behavior features or a single residual signal and do not explicitly model this evolution. To address this problem, we propose a causal high-order liquid evidence framework for GNSS spoofing detection. The method first constructs a physics-guided GNSS--motion inconsistency residual by comparing GNSS-implied displacement with onboard-motion-derived displacement. It then forms separate evidence streams for the residual level and its first- and second-order discrete variations, with relevant contextual cues selected according to the evidence order. Each stream is processed by a separate adaptive liquid encoder, and the resulting temporal states are hierarchically coupled to predict spoofing at the window endpoint using only current and past observations. Experiments on three subsets of the real-world AV-GPS dataset show that the proposed method achieves the highest F1-scores among the evaluated temporal models on Dataset~1 and Dataset~3, reaching 0.9535 and 0.9777, respectively. On Dataset~3, it detects both labeled normal-to-attack transitions within four sampling steps. Code and datasets are publicly available at: https://github.com/pangjunbiao/GNSS_Spoofing.git.

</details>


### [137] [Orientation, not magnitude: the causal structure of task-vector interference in merged language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11797)
*Chencheng Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that magnitude is insufficient for diagnosing model merging failures, revealing that cross-term transport and direction, not magnitude, are causally responsible for interference, with quantization roughness explaining apparent "universality" patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current field diagnoses model merging failures using magnitude-based approaches (layerwise representation bias, deviations from cross-task linearity, parameter overlap), but these are insufficient and inconsistent across model families.

Method: Used factorial ledger to track exact layerwise cross-terms of merged LLMs; performed systematic interventions including erasure experiments, basin tests with six displacements, dose-response causality tests, and instruction wrapper analysis; all 46 predictions were preregistered.

Result: Found cross-term is dominated by amplifying transport (~65-70% gain >1 per late block), carries interference as an attractor direction, causal erasure removes interference dose-dependently, instruction wrappers gate effects by amplifying cross-terms, and apparent "universality" patterns are quantization artifacts.

Conclusion: Magnitude is a coarse correlate at best; the true causal mechanism is directional transport of cross-terms as attractors, with quantization explaining apparent magnitude patterns, fundamentally changing how model merging failures should be diagnosed.

Abstract: Model merging by task arithmetic works until it doesn't, and the field diagnoses why with magnitudes: layerwise representation bias, deviations from cross-task linearity, parameter overlap. Tracking the exact layerwise cross-term of merged LLMs through a factorial ledger and intervening on it directly, we find magnitude insufficient - and inconsistent across model families - as a diagnostic axis. An exact decomposition of the layerwise flux shows it is dominated by amplifying transport of the existing cross-term (~65-70% in both families, gain >1 per late block), and erasing the term is undone by propagation - rebuilt to 99% of its norm at cosine 0.99 - unless applied near the output; a basin test with six starting displacements establishes the carried direction as an attractor of the forward pass. That direction is causally load-bearing: erasure along it removes expressed interference dose-dependently and saturates at exact erasure, while norm-matched wrong-direction controls fail or backfire. Instruction wrappers gate the effect: the same erasure finds 13x less relative interference to remove under a wrapper that internally amplifies the cross-term, because the wrapper drowns the interaction in a template-pinned main effect rather than shrinking it - a structure that replicates across further instruction templates but not under a length-matched control. Magnitude, by contrast, is at best a coarse correlate, and the striking +-15% "universality" of naive bfloat16 generation turns out to be quantization roughness. Task pairs whose local cross-term generation differs by at most 1.9x differ by 14x-337x in causally removable interference. All 46 predictions were preregistered and frozen before their data; falsifications, including of our own headline expectations and of behavioral recovery under a validated continuous endpoint, are reported as such.

</details>


### [138] [JAPE: Joint Anomaly Prediction and Intrinsic Explanation in Multivariate Time Series](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11801)
*Yian Wei,Yuanyuan Yao,Lu Chen,Xiangmin Zhou,Tianyi Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: JAPE is a framework that jointly performs multivariate time-series anomaly prediction and explanation by modeling evolving dependency structures, going beyond traditional numerical-deviation approaches to detect subtle anomaly precursors and provide native variable-level explanations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing anomaly prediction methods focus on numerical deviations in future values, which can miss subtle dependency changes caused by weak anomaly precursors. They also lack native variable-level explanations alongside predictions, making it hard to understand why anomalies occur.

Method: JAPE uses three key components: 1) Decoupled Spatio-Temporal Representation backbone that separates temporal and spatial modeling with lag-aware dependencies; 2) Dual-view alerting that fuses numerical forecasts with dependency graphs for predictions; 3) Native Predictive Explanation that reuses predicted dependency graphs to rank variables by structural deviations without extra models.

Result: Extensive experiments on five real-world benchmarks across three prediction horizons show JAPE improves average F1 by 19.7% and AUC-PR by 41.3% compared to existing methods, while improving explainability with 26.6% gain in MRR.

Conclusion: JAPE successfully bridges the gaps in existing methods by lifting anomaly prediction from numerical-deviation modeling to dependency-structure modeling, enabling both accurate point-wise alerting and native variable-level explanation through explicit modeling of evolving dependency structures.

Abstract: Multivariate time-series anomaly prediction aims to identify whether and when anomalies will occur over a future horizon from historical observations. Existing methods primarily characterize anomalies as deviations in future numerical values, which may overlook subtle dependency changes induced by weak anomaly precursors and provide no native variable-level explanation together with the alert. To bridge these gaps, we propose JAPE, a Joint Anomaly Prediction and Explanation framework that lifts anomaly prediction from numerical-deviation modeling to dependency-structure modeling. JAPE is the first anomaly prediction framework to explicitly model evolving dependency structures for both point-wise alerting and native variable-level explanation. Specifically, JAPE (i) proposes a Decoupled Spatio-Temporal Representation (DSTR) backbone that decouples temporal and spatial modeling and captures lag-aware dependencies via learnable lag aggregation, thereby perceiving structural precursors before numerical deviations emerge; (ii) designs a dual-view alerting mechanism that fuses numerical forecasts with evolving dependency graphs for point-wise anomaly prediction, capturing structural evidence even under subtle numerical deviations; and (iii) presents Native Predictive Explanation (NPE), which directly reuses the predicted dependency graphs to rank variables by structural deviations without additional models or training. Extensive experiments on five real-world benchmarks across three prediction horizons demonstrate that JAPE improves average F1 and AUC-PR by 19.7% and 41.3%, respectively, while improving explainability with 26.6% gain in MRR.

</details>


### [139] [Learning with Bilevel-Minimax Optimization for Efficient and Reliable Transfer Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11815)
*Yaohua Liu,Yifan Guo,Jiaxin Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BMAT is a bilevel-minimax framework for adversarial transfer that jointly optimizes initialization, surrogate adaptation, and gradient dynamics to improve attack transferability across black-box victim models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current transfer-based adversarial attacks rely on surrogate models, but their transferability is limited by the complex coupling between initialization, surrogate adaptation, and gradient dynamics. There's a need to better understand and optimize this fundamental relationship for improved attack effectiveness across different victim architectures.

Method: Proposes BMAT (Bilevel-Minimax Adversarial Transfer) with a bilevel formulation capturing initialization-perturbation dependency and an inner minimax problem for surrogate robustness. Uses integrated bottom-up solver with Soft Weight Modulator and Implicit Gradient Approximator for ternary coupling optimization.

Result: BMAT outperforms more than 10 strong baselines across 30+ victim models, improves both intra- and cross-architecture transfer, and achieves up to 2x reduction in mIoU on classification and segmentation benchmarks.

Conclusion: The bilevel-minimax perspective provides a principled framework for understanding and optimizing adversarial transfer, with BMAT demonstrating superior performance through its integrated approach to coupling initialization, surrogate adaptation, and perturbation dynamics.

Abstract: Transfer-based adversarial attacks craft adversarial examples using surrogate models to mislead black-box victim models. Beyond perturbation generation, transferability is fundamentally governed by the coupling of initialization, surrogate adaptation, and gradient dynamics. We revisit this challenge from a bilevel-minimax perspective and propose BMAT (Bilevel-Minimax Adversarial Transfer). The bilevel formulation captures the dependency between initialization and perturbation, while the inner minimax problem promotes surrogate robustness for cross-architecture generalization. Algorithmically, we develop an integrated bottom-up solver that combines a Soft Weight Modulator and an Implicit Gradient Approximator to enable ternary coupling among initialization, surrogate adaptation, and perturbation optimization. We further provide theoretical insights into the optimization dynamics of the proposed bilevel-minimax framework. Extensive experiments on classification and segmentation benchmarks show that BMAT outperforms more than 10 strong baselines across more than 30 victim models, improving both intra- and cross-architecture transfer and yielding up to a 2x reduction in mIoU. Code is available at https://github.com/callous-youth/BMAT.

</details>


### [140] [Towards Understanding On-Policy Distillation through the Lens of Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11829)
*Xinmu Ge,Zizhuo Zhang,Yu Huang,Jianing Zhu,Lin Yuan,Wanli Gu,Weichang Wu,Weiran Huang,Xiaolu Zhang,Bo Han,Jun Zhou,Jiangchao Yao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: On-policy distillation (OPD) improves LLM reasoning primarily by enhancing sampling efficiency rather than genuinely expanding capability boundaries, acting more like "illusory distillation" than true knowledge transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To examine the prevailing belief that OPD enables student models to distill knowledge from stronger teacher models and expand capabilities beyond the pre-OPD base model.

Method: Analyzed OPD through test-time scaling by varying sampling budget K and evaluating performance with pass@K and avg@K metrics across several OPD variants, and conducted problem-level solvability analysis using pass@1024 as criterion.

Result: OPD-trained models show superior avg@K across sampling budgets, but the advantage in pass@K gradually shifts to pre-OPD base models as K increases. OPD causes more previously solvable problems to become unsolvable than previously unsolvable problems to become solvable.

Conclusion: OPD primarily improves sampling efficiency rather than consistently expanding reasoning capability boundaries, behaving like "illusory distillation" where apparent gains come from improved efficiency rather than acquiring genuinely new capabilities.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has emerged as a promising post-training technique for enhancing LLM reasoning. It is commonly believed to enable the student model to distill knowledge from a stronger teacher model, thereby expanding capabilities beyond the pre-OPD base model. In this study, we examine this view through the lens of test-time scaling by varying the sampling budget K and evaluating performance with pass@K and avg@K. Specifically, across several OPD variants, we observe that OPD-trained models maintain superior avg@K performance across sampling budgets, while the advantage in pass@K gradually shifts to the pre-OPD base models as K increases. These results suggest that OPD primarily improves sampling efficiency rather than consistently expanding the student's reasoning capability boundary. The pass@K dynamics throughout OPD training further reveal a progressive shift toward stronger small-K performance at the expense of the large-K capability boundary. Furthermore, a problem-level solvability analysis using pass@1024 as the criterion reveals an asymmetry: OPD causes more previously solvable problems to become unsolvable than previously unsolvable problems to become solvable. Together, these findings suggest that, from the perspective of capability expansion, OPD behaves more like an "illusory distillation": its apparent gains arise primarily from improved sampling efficiency rather than from acquiring genuinely new reasoning capabilities from the teacher.

</details>


### [141] [Kernel Methods for Learning Operators with Multiple Inputs and Outputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11831)
*Adrien Weihs,Chunyang Liao,Jingmin Sun,Hayden Schaeffer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A kernel-based encoder-decoder framework for operator learning that separates observation, representation, learning, and reconstruction, achieving competitive accuracy with reduced computational costs compared to neural operators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning mappings between infinite-dimensional objects is a central challenge in scientific machine learning, particularly for operators mapping between products of potentially distinct function spaces in multi-input, multi-output settings.

Method: General kernel-based encoder-decoder framework that separates four components: observation, representation, learning, and reconstruction. Introduces KernelMO family with operator-valued and product-space formulations for multiple operator learning, featuring closed-form training and inference.

Result: The framework shows theoretically that convergence rate is governed by the most challenging constituent approximation problem rather than overall dimension. Practically, across five families of parametric PDEs, achieves competitive/state-of-the-art predictive accuracy with reduced training and inference costs relative to neural operators and deep learning models.

Conclusion: The kernel-based approach offers an efficient, lightweight alternative to neural operator architectures, combining mathematical tractability with computational efficiency for operator learning in scientific ML applications.

Abstract: Learning mappings between infinite-dimensional objects is a central challenge in scientific machine learning. We introduce a general kernel-based encoder-decoder framework for operator learning that separates observation, representation, learning, and reconstruction. We develop this framework for multi-input, multi-output operator learning, where operators map between products of potentially distinct function spaces. Our approximation theory shows that, although the number of inputs and outputs can increase, the convergence rate is governed by the most challenging constituent approximation problem rather than the overall problem dimension. The framework leads to practical kernel methods with closed-form training and inference, combining mathematical tractability with computational efficiency. We further specialize the approach to multiple operator learning by introducing KernelMO, a family of kernel methods with complementary operator-valued and product-space formulations. Across five families of parametric partial differential equations, the proposed methods achieve competitive or state-of-the-art predictive accuracy while reducing training and inference costs relative to neural operator architectures and deep learning based models, offering an efficient and lightweight alternative.

</details>


### [142] [Air Quality Station Simulation via LSTM and Attention-Based Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11839)
*Alexander Kostadinov,Petar O. Hristov,Dessislava Petrova-Antonova*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SATADL is a deep learning model that generates multiple-hour-ahead air quality forecasts to simulate measurements from faulty stations, acting as a virtual proxy during station downtime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Air quality monitoring stations are prone to hardware faults and blackouts, leading to data gaps that compromise air quality analysis and public health monitoring.

Method: SATADL uses a deep learning architecture that extracts complex relations from various aspects of air quality data to predict multiple-hour-ahead pollutant concentrations.

Result: SATADL outperforms baseline and published deep learning models across different prediction windows (up to 48 hours) in PM10 concentration forecasting, measured by coefficient of determination and root mean squared error.

Conclusion: SATADL is an effective virtual proxy station solution that can maintain data continuity during measurement station failures, enhancing air quality monitoring reliability.

Abstract: Poor air quality in urban areas is driven by a complex chain of processes and presents a significant public health concern. To better understand and control the mechanisms that determine air quality, cities deploy networks of measurement stations, and launch initiatives for collecting denser data about the concentration of pollutants in the atmosphere. Extracting insights from the stations relies on their reliable and uninterrupted operation. However, hardware is susceptible to faults and black- outs that may result in data unavailability, which affects the overall quality of analyses. In this paper, we present a deep-learning model, called SATADL, which can infer complex relations and output multiple-hour-ahead air-quality forecasts. The goal of the model is to simulate the mea- surements of an unresponsive station until its operation is restored. The architecture of the model, which allows it to extract information from different aspects of the data, is described in detail and a careful examination of all of its components is provided. We demonstrate the performance of SATADL on four sets of air quality stations from around the world, by using it to simulate the concentration of PM10 for periods of hypothetical failures of one of the measurement stations, lasting for as long as 48 hours. A selection of baseline and published deep learning models were trained and used as a benchmark. The results show that SATADL per- forms better across different prediction windows, for both coefficient of determination and root mean squared error, demonstrating its suitability as a virtual proxy station.

</details>


### [143] [Small-Scale Experiments: Are We There Yet?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11859)
*Nicholas Lourie,Kyunghyun Cho,Karen Ullrich,Sanae Lotfi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Scaling laws can work at small model scales if you properly tune hyperparameters, but hyperparameter sensitivity fades as models grow larger, requiring extensive search to find the true scaling frontier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling laws haven't delivered on their promise of cost-effective experiments because researchers found them unreliable at small scales and concluded that large models are necessary. The paper aims to show that scaling laws actually exist at small scales when hyperparameters are properly tuned.

Method: The authors ablate the basic scaling law recipe to show hyperparameter importance, analyze how hyperparameter loss surfaces become lower-dimensional with scale, and develop a new methodology for model-centric research that synthesizes insights with recent literature. They demonstrate this methodology by solving the transformer normalization layer placement question through small-scale experiments.

Result: Small models are highly sensitive to hyperparameters, but this sensitivity fades with scale. Scaling laws do exist in small models but require extensive hyperparameter tuning to reveal. The paper's methodology successfully recovered the large-scale finding that pre-normalization works better as models grow in size, all from small-scale experiments.

Conclusion: With proper hyperparameter tuning and methodology, small-scale experiments can deliver on scaling laws' promise of cost-effective research, overcoming previous reliability issues and avoiding the need for always training large models.

Abstract: Scaling laws promised cost-effective experiments; six years later, they have yet to fully deliver. Instead, researchers have found them unreliable at small scales (starting at 4M parameters) and concluded that sizable models cannot be avoided. We show this is not the case: the confounding factor is hyperparameters. Small models are highly sensitive, but hyperparameter sensitivity fades with scale. This small-scale sensitivity makes scaling laws easy to miss because they only emerge on the fully tuned frontier, and reaching that frontier requires an extensive search far beyond what most ever run. By ablating the basic scaling law recipe, we show well-tuned hyperparameters matter more than any other ingredient. Further, we reveal why those hyperparameters become easier to find: as scale increases, the hyperparameter loss surface becomes lower dimensional. Nevertheless while scaling laws exist in small models, extrapolation hits statistical limitations. A holistic approach is required. Synthesizing our insights with the recent literature, we develop a new methodology for model-centric research and demonstrate it on a question that once took the field years to settle: where to place normalization layers in the transformer architecture. From small-scale experiments, we recover the large scale result: pre-normalization works better as models grow in size. With the right tools and a better understanding, small-scale experiments can deliver on scaling laws' long-awaited promise.

</details>


### [144] [Forward and Inverse Virtual Metrology for Phototransistor Gain: A Hierarchical, Uncertainty-Aware Approach for Small Production Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11868)
*Mahshid Amirabgir,Lorenza Ferrario,Paolo Conci,Mahdieh Amirabgir,Giancarlo Orengo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A predictive model for silicon bipolar phototransistor gain using process parameters, with forward prediction, inverse recipe search, and multi-level data quality assessment for fabrication optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The fabrication process for silicon bipolar phototransistors requires months of cleanroom time, making predictive models valuable even with moderate accuracy. The paper addresses small-sample, hierarchically structured fabrication data unlike conventional large-dataset virtual metrology.

Method: Study of real fabrication history (13-14 process runs), variance decomposition of device gain, development of forward gain predictor with uncertainty awareness, inverse search for target gain recipes, and multi-level data quality assessment with explicit cross-level linkage scoring for nested fabrication entities (batch, wafer, die).

Result: Variance analysis shows roughly half of device gain variance lies between process runs rather than within them, establishing recipe-only prediction limits. The forward predictor provides uncertainty-aware signals, inverse search returns recipes for target gains, and multi-level quality assessment successfully captures nested fabrication structure.

Conclusion: The approach enables meaningful prediction despite small-sample limitations by addressing hierarchical data structure, provides uncertainty-aware tools for fabrication optimization, and releases normalized dataset and code for reproducibility, establishing foundation for pre-fabrication device performance prediction.

Abstract: The customization, optimization and stabilization of the process flow of a silicon bipolar phototransistor commits months of cleanroom time before a finished device can be measured, so a model that predicts device gain from process parameters before a run has value out of proportion to its accuracy. We study this problem on a real fabrication history, thirteen to fourteen process runs of a single device: a small-sample, hierarchically structured setting unlike the large-corpus regime of conventional virtual metrology. Decomposing the variance of device gain, we find that roughly half of it lies between process runs rather than within them, so recipe-only prediction is bounded by construction. Building on these findings we provide a forward gain predictor with a relative, uncertainty-aware signal, an inverse search that returns recipes for a target gain, and, as the foundation for all of it, a multi-level data-quality assessment tailored to the nested physical entities of fabrication (batch, wafer, die) with an explicit cross-level linkage score. The normalized dataset and analysis code are released for full reproducibility.

</details>


### [145] [DCM Bandits: Multiplayer Information Asymmetric Cascading Bandits for Multiple Clicks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11873)
*Andy Wang,Charlton Shih,William Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper extends the Dependent Click Model (DCM) Bandits to a multiplayer, information-asymmetric setting with multiple agents interacting with a shared ranked list and multiple clicks per session, providing sublinear regret guarantees for asymmetric environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address new challenges in selection strategies that arise in multiplayer information-asymmetric settings where multiple agents interact with a shared ranked list and may observe multiple clicks per session, which extends beyond traditional single-agent models.

Method: The method involves extending the Dependent Click Model (DCM) Bandits to multiplayer settings, studying asymmetry in both actions and rewards, developing algorithms that handle these asymmetries, and showing that for small termination probabilities, the termination ranking need not be known (improving prior results).

Result: The results provide sublinear regret guarantees for three settings where at least one type of asymmetry (actions or rewards) is present, with experiments confirming that the algorithms perform well across asymmetric environments and highlighting the critical role of feedback structure (full vs first-click feedback) in coordinating exploration and minimizing regret.

Conclusion: The work successfully extends bandit algorithms to multiplayer information-asymmetric settings with shared ranked lists, demonstrates practical performance in asymmetric environments, and identifies feedback structure as crucial for coordination, though establishing matching information-theoretic lower bounds remains an open problem.

Abstract: In this work, we extend the Dependent Click Model (DCM) Bandits to a multiplayer information-asymmetric setting, where multiple agents interact with a shared ranked list and may observe multiple clicks per session, introducing new challenges for selection strategies. We study asymmetry in (1) actions and (2) rewards, providing sublinear regret guarantees for three settings where at least one asymmetry is present. Establishing matching information-theoretic lower bounds for these settings is left as an open problem. We further show that for small termination probabilities, the termination ranking need not be known, improving on prior single-agent results. Experiments confirm that our algorithms perform well across asymmetric environments and highlight the critical role of feedback structure, specifically the distinction between full versus first-click feedback, in coordinating exploration and minimizing regret.

</details>


### [146] [Disentangling the Expressivity of RoPE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11909)
*Selim Jerad,Anej Svete,Jiaoda Li,Ryan Cotterell*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RoPE transformers have two theoretical explanations: modular predicate expressivity (periodic) vs. positional anchor mechanisms. The paper formalizes both, showing conventional RoPE differs from purely periodic variants, leading to precision-dependent bounded locality rather than true modular generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to reconcile two recurring theoretical accounts of rotary position embeddings (RoPE): one emphasizing expressivity through modular predicates (periodic behavior), and another focusing on mechanistic positional anchors and offsets. There's a gap between theoretical characterizations and practical RoPE implementations that needs clarification.

Method: The authors formalize both accounts for fully uniform, finite-precision soft-attention transformers. They analyze RoPE's theoretical properties, comparing conventional non-repeating rotations with constructed periodic variants. They conduct controlled experiments to test length generalization on modular languages and examine position-invariant context access.

Result: Purely periodic RoPE variants recognize exactly languages definable in past temporal logic with modular predicates. Conventional RoPE, with non-repeating rotations, yields precision-dependent bounded simulation of look-back operators rather than true modular characterization. Experimental results confirm this separation: periodic variants generalize on modular languages, while conventional RoPE acts more like bounded locality bias and can impair tasks needing distant context access.

Conclusion: The findings bridge theoretical expressivity characterizations with practical RoPE models, showing conventional RoPE's behavior stems from its non-repeating rotations creating precision-dependent locality rather than true modular periodicity. This clarifies the disconnect between two theoretical accounts and brings theory closer to understanding real-world transformer behavior.

Abstract: Two accounts recur in explanations of the success of rotary position embeddings (RoPE). Expressivity studies associate periodic position information with modular predicates, whereas mechanistic and long-context studies emphasize positional anchors and local offsets. We formalize both accounts for fully uniform, finite-precision soft-attention transformers. We find that, if every rotary component is periodic, RoPE transformers recognize exactly the languages definable in past temporal logic with modular predicates. Conventional RoPE is different: The rotations it computes never repeat. This yields a precision-dependent bounded simulation of fixed-offset look-back operators, rather than an all-length modular characterization. Controlled experiments match this separation: Constructed periodic schedules length-generalize on modular languages, while conventional RoPE behaves more like a bounded locality bias and can impair tasks requiring position-invariant access to distant context. Altogether, our findings shed light on RoPE transformers, bringing theoretical expressivity characterizations closer to models used in practice.

</details>


### [147] [A Factor Graph Approach to Scalable Multi-Output Gaussian Process Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11917)
*Wouter W. L. Nuijten,Esther G. van Pelt,Albert Podusenko,İsmail Şenöz,Wouter M. Kouw*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A factor-graph approach for multi-output Gaussian process regression using nearest-neighbor chains that scales linearly with data points, handles missing observations gracefully, and remains competitive with exact and approximate baselines while being computationally efficient.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-output Gaussian process regression scales cubically with observations times outputs and requires complex handling when outputs are observed at different inputs. Existing methods struggle with computational complexity and missing data patterns.

Method: Express multi-output GP regression as a Forney-style factor graph with a nearest-neighbor chain ordering inputs into a 1D sequence. Use Matérn processes evolving through linear-Gaussian transitions, mixed via linear model of coregionalization into outputs with deterministic mixing and per-output observation factors.

Result: Factor-graph method achieves O(C(DL² + L³)) computational cost after chain construction, handles missing observations naturally without covariance restructuring. In low dimensions, closely tracks exact kernel-matrix posterior; maintains competitiveness as dimension increases. On electricity time series, matches baselines in accuracy while scaling linearly with data points.

Conclusion: The factor-graph formulation provides an efficient alternative to exact multi-output GP regression that scales well with data size, handles missing observations seamlessly, and remains accurate while being computationally feasible where exact methods fail.

Abstract: Multi-output Gaussian process regression scales cubically in the number of observations times outputs, and dense kernel-matrix methods need bespoke handling whenever different outputs are observed at different inputs. We express multi-output Gaussian process regression as a Forney-style factor graph in which a nearest-neighbor chain orders a fixed candidate set of $C$ inputs into a one-dimensional sequence. Along this chain, latent Matérn processes evolve through linear-Gaussian transition factors, while the linear model of coregionalization mixes $L$ latent processes into $D$ outputs through a deterministic mixing factor and per-output scalar observation factors. Posterior computation reduces to exact Gaussian message passing on the chain at cost $\mathcal{O}(C(DL^2 + L^3))$ after chain construction, and missing observations omit their local factor without any covariance-matrix restructuring. The formulation therefore scales in the number of data samples and in the rate of missing observations, while remaining best suited to candidate sets in low input dimension.We compare the factor-graph formulation against an exact kernel-matrix baseline, a sparse-variational inducing-point baseline, and a nearest-neighbor baseline on a synthetic input-dimension sweep and on electricity time series forecasting. At low input dimension the factor-graph posterior tracks the exact kernel-matrix posterior closely, and the gap grows gradually as input dimension increases while staying competitive with both approximate baselines. On the electricity time series our factor-graph formulation matches all three baselines in forecast accuracy while scaling linearly in the number of data points, where the exact kernel-matrix method becomes infeasible and the inducing-point baseline remains substantially slower.

</details>


### [148] [Distillation of Foundation Models for Time-dependent PDEs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11937)
*Daniel Musekamp,Boshra Ariguib,Andrei Manolache,Mathias Niepert*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TREX is a knowledge distillation framework that compresses large foundation models for PDEs into efficient student models using teacher-generated synthetic trajectories, achieving comparable accuracy with orders-of-magnitude speedup and parameter reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Foundation models for PDEs are large and computationally intensive, limiting their usefulness as fast surrogates for numerical solvers despite their strong generalization capabilities after fine-tuning.

Method: TREX distills knowledge from fine-tuned teacher foundation models into compact students by: 1) Generating long synthetic trajectories through teacher rollouts with optional periodic noise injection, 2) Sampling from teacher-induced rollout distribution without requiring initial-condition distribution knowledge, 3) Exposing students to long-horizon states and local recovery behavior, and 4) Allowing students to incorporate task-specific inductive biases like equivariance.

Result: On multiple PDE benchmarks, TREX-trained students match or surpass teacher accuracy while reducing parameters by several orders of magnitude and achieving more than an order-of-magnitude inference speedup.

Conclusion: TREX successfully addresses the computational limitations of foundation models for PDEs through effective knowledge distillation, making them practical for fast surrogate applications while maintaining or improving predictive performance.

Abstract: Foundation models for time-dependent partial differential equations (PDEs) are trained on large and diverse collections of physical systems and can generalize effectively to new downstream tasks. After fine-tuning on only a few trajectories from a target domain, they can achieve strong accuracy in low-data regimes. However, these models are typically large and computationally intensive, limiting their usefulness as fast surrogates for numerical solvers. We propose Teacher Rollout Extension (TREX), a knowledge distillation framework that transfers the predictive capability of a pretrained foundation model into a compact and efficient student. Starting from a fine-tuned teacher, TREX augments limited downstream data by generating long synthetic trajectories through teacher rollouts, optionally with periodic noise injection. This procedure samples from the teacher-induced rollout distribution without requiring explicit knowledge of the initial-condition distribution, while exposing the student to long-horizon states and local recovery behavior around states encountered during autoregressive prediction. The student can further incorporate task-specific inductive biases, such as equivariance, that the teacher does not necessarily enforce. We evaluate TREX on multiple PDE benchmarks. The resulting students can match or surpass the teacher's accuracy while reducing the number of parameters by several orders of magnitude and achieving more than an order-of-magnitude speedup in inference.

</details>


### [149] [TailBooster: A Dual-Layer Generative Framework for Extreme Value Augmentation with Operational Validity Enforcement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11951)
*Karim Aly,Alexei Sharpanskykh,Jacco Hoekstra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TailBooster: A dual-layer generative framework that combines anomaly detection with targeted synthetic data augmentation to improve extreme-event prediction in air transport by addressing both tail under-representation and operational infeasibility in mixed-type tabular data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extreme events in air transport (severe delays, abnormal air times) cause cascading network disruptions with high operational, economic, and safety costs. Existing approaches fail because: 1) rare events in historical data provide insufficient training signals for ML models, 2) conventional generative models under-represent distributional tails, and 3) no guarantees against operationally infeasible synthetic instances. No current method addresses both limitations for mixed-type tabular records.

Method: Proposes TailBooster, a dual-layer generative framework with two anomaly detection layers: 1) Statistical layer extracts extremes via interquartile range to provide tail-concentrated training signals to a Tabular Variational Autoencoder, 2) Deep learning layer applies autoencoder-based cleaning to discard synthetic records that violate operational envelopes learned from historical data. The framework is evaluated across five dimensions: diversity, statistical similarity, fidelity, operational validity, and utility.

Result: Data-driven cleaning significantly improved operational validity, while targeted augmentation enhanced utility for extreme-event prediction. Across six regression algorithms, training on TailBooster's records reduced Mean Absolute Error by 47-49% on extreme air time and 29-57% on extreme arrival delay prediction compared to conventional synthetic data. Comparable gains achieved when enriching real records with synthetic extremes.

Conclusion: TailBooster effectively addresses both tail under-representation and operational feasibility for extreme-event prediction in air transport. The framework is fully data-driven and model-agnostic, making it extensible to other domains where extreme-event prediction is critical and domain-specific rules are unavailable.

Abstract: Extreme events in air transport, such as severe arrival delays and abnormal air times, cause cascading network disruptions with substantial operational, economic, and safety costs. Such events are rare in historical records, leaving insufficient training signal for machine learning models. Synthetic data augmentation offers a principled solution, but conventional generative models under-represent distributional tails and give no guarantee against operationally infeasible instances, such as a short air time paired with a long flight distance. No existing approach addresses both limitations for mixed-type tabular records. We propose TailBooster, a dual-layer generative framework combining generative modelling with two anomaly detection layers. A statistical layer extracts extremes via the interquartile range, supplying tail-concentrated training signal to dedicated generative models, here a Tabular Variational Autoencoder. A deep learning layer then applies autoencoder-based cleaning, discarding synthetic records that violate the operational envelope learned from historical data. The framework was evaluated on US flight records across five dimensions: diversity, statistical similarity, fidelity, operational validity, and utility, the latter two being the primary improvement targets. Data-driven cleaning markedly improved operational validity, while targeted augmentation enhanced utility for extreme-event prediction. Across six regression algorithms, training on the framework's records reduced Mean Absolute Error by 47-49% on extreme air time and 29-57% on extreme arrival delay prediction relative to conventional synthetic data, with comparable gains when real records were enriched with synthetic extremes. Being fully data-driven and model-agnostic, TailBooster extends to domains where extreme-event prediction is critical and domain-specific rules are unavailable.

</details>


### [150] [LoongReflect: Boosting Long-Horizon Reflection in Search Agents via Global Perspective Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11967)
*Zhixin Zhang,Xinke Jiang,Zhibang Yang,Weixuan Xu,Guohong Qiu,Xu Chu,Junfeng Zhao,Yasha Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoongReflect is a training framework that formulates reflection as a memory-control policy for LLM agents, combining global teacher distillation with outcome-based reinforcement learning to improve long-horizon reasoning in complex tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agents struggle with reflection in long-horizon reasoning tasks because reflection operates locally within a branch while its utility is only determined by final outcomes. This creates a local-global mismatch where outcome-based RL provides only sparse, delayed supervision for reflective decisions.

Method: LoongReflect formulates reflection as a memory-control policy where agents operate on reversible trajectory trees using explicit reflect and backtrack actions. It uses a two-channel learning approach: a fast channel distills globally informed reflective behavior from a privileged teacher (supervision on reflection/backtracking tokens only), and a slow channel optimizes complete trajectories using outcome-based GRPO.

Result: Experiments on multi-hop retrieval-augmented generation and mathematical reasoning benchmarks show consistent improvements over outcome-only reinforcement learning and self-distillation baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed LoongReflect framework effectively addresses the local-global mismatch in reflection learning for LLM agents by combining global distillation with outcome optimization, demonstrating superior performance in complex reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Large language model agents increasingly rely on long-horizon reasoning to solve complex tasks involving planning, tool use, and memory. A critical capability in such settings is reflection: assessing trajectory progress, identifying missing evidence and unreliable intermediate states, and deciding whether to continue, revise, or abandon the current branch. Learning effective reflection, however, is challenging because reflection is performed locally within the current branch, whereas its utility can only be determined by its contribution to the final trajectory outcome. This local-global mismatch makes outcome-based reinforcement learning provide only local, sparse and delayed supervision for reflective decisions. To solve these, we propose LoongReflect, a training framework that formulates reflection as a memory-control policy. The agent operates over a reversible trajectory tree using explicit reflect and backtrack actions. Reflection consolidates verified facts, missing evidence, and branch-specific risks into working memory, while backtracking removes an unreliable branch from the active context and preserves a concise corrective lesson. To learn this policy, LoongReflect combines two complementary signals through a look-ahead, extragradient-style coordination mechanism. A fast channel distills globally informed reflective behavior from a privileged teacher, with supervision restricted to reflection and backtracking tokens. A slow channel optimizes complete trajectories using outcome-based GRPO, aligning local control decisions with final task success. Experiments on multi-hop retrieval-augmented generation and mathematical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over outcome-only reinforcement learning and self-distillation baselines.

</details>


### [151] [TESLA: Taylor Expansion of Sinusoidal Learnable Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11970)
*Daehwa Ko,Jaehyeon Kim,Seunghyun Ham,Jay Hoon Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TESLA is a new neural activation function combining learnable sine and cosine terms that enables explicit control over polynomial degree and selective amplification of high-order components, achieving strong generalization on the parity problem and transferring well to vision tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The parity problem remains challenging for standard neural networks due to linear inseparability and the need for global interactions, motivating the development of activation functions that can better capture high-order structure.

Method: Proposed TESLA activation function defined as a learnable combination of sine and cosine terms, enabling explicit control over polynomial degree and selective amplification of high-order components. Theoretical analysis shows coefficient constraints yield Lipschitz/Rademacher complexity bounds and shape training dynamics.

Result: On parity with n=32, TESLA achieves strong generalization with only 100K training samples (0.002% of input space) and remains robust under heavy corruption (up to 30% label noise). Outperforms periodic/frequency baselines (SIREN, SNAKE, Fourier features) on parity and Forrelation. Achieves comparable performance on ImageNet-100, showing transfer to vision workloads.

Conclusion: TESLA demonstrates that activation-level degree control can effectively solve challenging problems like parity while transferring to practical vision tasks, offering a promising approach for designing neural networks that can capture complex global interactions.

Abstract: The parity problem--deciding whether the number of ones in a binary vector is odd or even--remains challenging for standard neural networks due to linear inseparability and the need for global interactions. We propose TESLA, an activation defined as a learnable combination of sine and cosine terms, enabling explicit control over polynomial degree and selective amplification of high-order components. Theoretically, we show that constraining TESLA's coefficients yields Lipschitz/Rademacher complexity bounds and shapes the training dynamics to emphasize higher-frequency structure. Empirically, on parity with input length n = 32, TESLA attains strong generalization with 100K training samples (approximately 0.002% of the 2^32 input space) and remains robust under heavy corruption, retaining high accuracy with up to 30% label noise. We also compare against periodic and frequency-based baselines (SIREN, SNAKE, and Fourier feature embeddings) on parity and Forrelation. Beyond synthetic structure, TESLA delivers comparable performance on ImageNet-100, indicating that activation-level degree control transfers to more general vision workloads. Code: https://github.com/KAU-QuantumAILab/TESLA

</details>


### [152] [Remote Sensing and Machine Learning-Based Analysis of Land Use and Vegetation Change in Dhaka District, Bangladesh](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12001)
*Muhammad Masud Tarek,Md. Alamgir Hossain,Md. Samiul Islam,Muntasir Hasan Kanchan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Remote sensing and machine learning reveal rapid urban expansion in Dhaka District (59.5% increase in built-up areas) from 2019-2024, with significant vegetation (-8.46%) and water body (-7.77%) losses, highlighting environmental pressures from unregulated urbanization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rapid urbanization in Dhaka District, Bangladesh has triggered substantial alterations in land use and environmental conditions, necessitating systematic monitoring for informed urban planning and ecological sustainability.

Method: Uses remote sensing data (Sentinel-2 MSI and Landsat 8) and machine learning (Decision Tree, KNN, Random Forest classifiers) within Google Earth Engine to classify land cover and compute spectral indices (NDVI, NDBI, NDWI) for 2019-2024 period with accuracy assessment via confusion matrices and kappa statistics.

Result: Found 59.5% increase in urban built-up areas, 8.46% decline in vegetation, and 7.77% decline in water bodies over five years; Random Forest achieved highest classification accuracy; identified land conversion from vegetated/aquatic areas to urban infrastructure as dominant trend.
